# Testing Decision Log

The single source of truth for *why* the test suite is shaped the way it is. Code comments in the suite
state what the code does; every contestable choice — one a competent engineer could reasonably have made
differently — is recorded here instead, with the alternatives that were available, the reason the choice was
made, and the risk it carries.

A deviation from a literal reading of the requirements without a row in this table is a defect.

## Programme scope

Building this repository's first executable test suite: five layers — backend unit, backend integration,
frontend unit, frontend component and end-to-end — asserted against the code exactly as it exists rather
than as the design documents describe it, with exactly two pre-authorized production touches (§22) and no
other production change.

## How to read this log

Every decision is a table row with four columns: **Decision**, what was decided; **Alternatives
considered**, what else was genuinely on the table; **Why this choice**, the reason it won; and **Risks it
carries**, what it costs. No cell is ever left empty — a decision with no stated risk has not been thought
through. The leading `#` column is a stable citation handle, and other files cite these ids from `@see`
comments, so an id is never reused, renumbered or retired. Measured across the tree this branch ships,
excluding `documentation/`: **35** files outside this log carry at least one `D` or `C` id — 36 counting
this file itself — and **52** link to this document. Those are two different counts of two different
things, and the citation count is the one an id rename would break.

To add a decision: append a row to the section it belongs to, or open a new section if none fits, taking the
next unused id. Never edit a shipped row to change its meaning — append a new row that names the row it
supersedes or refines, and mark the superseded row in place. That convention is what keeps every citation in
the tree pointing at the reasoning that was current when it was written (D190).
The same holds for the evidence a row quotes: a measurement in a **Why this choice** or **Risks it
carries** cell is what that check printed when the decision was taken, so a row may name a suite total
that the suite has since grown past, or a render count that a later slide or diagram has moved. Those
readings are left as they were recorded. The delivered tree's current figures live in
[`./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`](./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md) §1.1 and §6.1–§6.5, which `dashboard-extract.py`
regenerates from the run artifacts rather than from prose.

The four user-specified rules are named wherever they bear on a decision, summarised rather than
transcribed; their full text lives in the project's rules document. **Rule 1 (Explainability)** is the rule
this file is the deliverable for. **Rule 2 (Observability)** is answered for the test system in §8, scoped
by C1, with its production gaps recorded in §23. **Rule 3 (Onboarding & Continued Development)** owns the
four onboarding documents, which project §23 as their suggested next tasks. **Rule 4 (Executive
Presentation)** owns the executive deck, which projects the risks column of every table below.

## Related artifacts

- [`./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`](./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md) — the bidirectional matrix Rule 1 requires for a
  migration, §A through §H, including the 25-function legacy disposition in §E and the coverage ceilings
  in §G.
- [`./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md`](./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md) — the Rule 2 coverage and test-health dashboard,
  which cites the observability decisions in §8, §21 and D193 rather than re-arguing them.
- The Rule 3 onboarding documents — `backend/tests/README.md`, `frontend/TESTING.md` and `e2e/README.md`,
  alongside the additive Testing section of the root [`README.md`](../../README.md) — cover how each suite
  is set up, run and extended. This log carries none of that by design, and each of them defers every
  "why" to here. They are companion deliverables of this same change set, so they are named by path rather
  than linked from a document that must stay readable on its own.

Scope note: this log covers the *test system*, which is the deliverable being built. The production
instrumentation gaps it does not close — no metrics endpoint, no tracing, and health checks in
`docker-compose.yml` and `cd.yml` aimed at a `/health` route that does not exist — are recorded as
suggested next tasks rather than implemented, because the requirements authorize exactly two production
touches and forbid implementing missing product features (D50–D53).

## §1 Frontend HTTP interception seam

Scope: `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.ts`, the msw 1.x handler array the frontend Jest layer installs.
The measured behaviour these decisions are calibrated against was obtained by driving the assembled FastAPI
application through `TestClient(app, raise_server_exceptions=False)` — under both dependency dispositions,
with `app.db.firestore.get_db` left alone and with it overridden, and at both the path the client emits and the
path the backend declares — and by driving the frontend services through msw 1.3.5 under Jest 29 + jsdom. Both
sets of observations are reproduced in `docs/testing/TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §A. Rows D1–D16 were taken against
the overridden disposition alone, which is what D169 and D170 correct.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D1 | **[The layer-2 half is superseded by D169 — the shipped factories are unsetBaseBackendHandlers() and configuredBaseBackendHandlers(), one per base URL; the two-layer principle below stands.]** Ship **two separately named handler layers**: `frontendIsolationHandlers` (nominal success, test-only) and `currentBackendBehaviorHandlers()` (the statuses and bodies the backend returns today). | (a) One default array of nominal 200s — the shape before this decision. (b) Only current-behaviour handlers, no nominal layer. (c) A single array whose responses are switched by an environment flag. | (a) makes every future suite green against a synthetic success contract while appearing to cover integration: `GET /tweets/{id}` really answers 500, `POST /generate-response` really answers 404, and a nominal-only array erases both. (b) makes component rendering untestable, because three of the four routes never return usable data. (c) hides which contract a given test ran against inside global state. Two named layers make the choice explicit at each call site. | A suite author can still pick the isolation layer where a contract assertion was intended. Mitigated by naming (`frontendIsolation…` vs `currentBackendBehavior…`), by the module header table, and by the per-suite obligations in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §A. |
| D2 | Keep `handlers` as an exported name, as an alias of `frontendIsolationHandlers`, rather than renaming it. | (a) Rename to `isolationHandlers` only. (b) Export the isolation array as the module default. | `frontend/TESTING.md` and `frontend/src/test-utils/msw-server.ts` are specified against the name `handlers`; renaming would break a contract fixed by a shipped onboarding document. Both names refer to one array, so there is no second source of truth. | Two names for one value invites the assumption that they differ. Mitigated by an assertion-grade doc comment on the alias and by both names appearing in the module header. |
| D3 | **Screen every request against its route contract inside the handler**, and answer a deviating request with a distinct non-success status instead of the nominal payload. | (a) Match on path only and answer 200 unconditionally. (b) Match on an exact query string in the msw pattern so a deviating request falls through to `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'`. (c) Assert the request only in each individual test. | (a) is the defect this row replaces: `limit=undefined`, an unknown query key, a placeholder path parameter and a wrongly keyed body were all answered 200. (b) breaks the real callers, whose query strings legitimately contain the literal string `undefined`, and would make `onUnhandledRequest` fire for correct traffic. (c) leaves every suite free to omit the assertion. Screening in the handler makes the request contract enforced once, for every suite. | A future caller shape that is legitimate but undocumented is rejected until its contract row is updated. That is the intended trade: an unexpected request must be visible. Each violation message names the exact field and value so the fix is mechanical. |
| D4 | Answer a screened-out request with **status 599** and a JSON body listing the violations. | (a) `res.networkError()`. (b) 400. (c) 500. (d) 422. | Neither FastAPI nor Starlette can emit 599, so the status can never be mistaken for a real backend outcome; 400, 422 and 500 are all statuses the real surface produces or could produce. `res.networkError()` destroys the response body, so the violation list would be unavailable to the failing test. | 599 is unfamiliar. Mitigated by `CONTRACT_VIOLATION_DETAIL` in the body, by the exported `CONTRACT_VIOLATION_STATUS` constant, and by the `console.error` in D5. |
| D5 | Also write the violations to `console.error`. | Rely on the 599 status alone. | `llmService.generateTweetResponse` catches the rejection and rethrows `Error('Failed to generate tweet response')`, and `twitterService` logs and rethrows, so a status alone can reach a test with the cause stripped. The log line carries the route id, the method, the full URL and every violation. | A suite that asserts an exact `console.error` call count sees the extra line. It is only emitted on a violation, which a conforming suite never triggers. |
| D6 | Record every intercepted request in a module-level log exposed through `recordedRequests()` / `lastRecordedRequest()`, and **auto-register `resetRecordedRequests` with `afterEach`** when the module loads inside a test run. | (a) No log; each suite spies on axios. (b) A log with an explicit reset the suites must remember to call. (c) A per-test `captureRequests()` scope helper. | The review requires exact assertions on the query, path and body a caller emitted; a log makes those assertions possible without mocking axios, which would mock the module under test. Explicit-reset-only (b) is an order-dependence hazard, and `setup-jest.ts` — where the `afterEach` would live — is not yet written, so the isolation guarantee cannot be delegated to it. (c) cannot observe requests issued from a component's mount effect. | Registering a hook as an import side effect is not obvious from a call site. Mitigated by the guard (`typeof afterEach === 'function'`), by the reset also being exported for explicit use, and by the operation being idempotent, so a later explicit `afterEach` is harmless. |
| D7 | **[Superseded by D103, and again by D136 — the wording below is the decision as originally taken, not the shipped behaviour.]** Keep host-agnostic `*/…` patterns, but **restrict the origins a handler answers** to `http://localhost` and `http://127.0.0.1`, with `allowRequestOrigin()` as the documented opt-in. | (a) Answer any origin, as a bare wildcard does. (b) Pin the patterns to `http://localhost/undefined`. | The callers' base URL is the literal string `undefined`, so wildcard patterns are mandatory — but a bare wildcard also answers a request to a real host, which is exactly how a mistakenly configured base URL stays invisible. Pinning the pattern (b) would make the handlers stop matching the moment a suite sets a base URL, producing an unhandled-request failure whose message does not name the cause. An origin check answers with the violation instead. | A suite that intentionally points at an absolute URL must call `allowRequestOrigin` first. The failure it gets otherwise names the origin and the allow-list, so the remedy is in the message. |
| D8 | **[Superseded by D170; previously refined by D137 — the branch below is correct, but it is the **dependency-overridden** disposition, not what the unoverridden application returns.]** Model `GET /tweets` current behaviour with a **live coercion branch** — 200 when `skip` and `limit` coerce to `int`, otherwise 422 naming the failing parameter — rather than a fixed 422. | (a) A fixed 422 handler and a separate fixed 200 handler. (b) A fixed 200 handler only, with the 422 left to the backend integration suite. | The backend's answer depends on the request: `fetchTweets(2, 10)` really gets 200 and `getLatestTweets(5)` really gets 422, from the same route. A fixed status would force a suite to choose a handler per assertion and would misrepresent one of the two callers. The branch reproduces the observed rule — trim, then `^[+-]?\d+$` — so `limit=+7` and `limit=" 8 "` are 200 and `limit=undefined`, `limit=10.5` and `limit=` are 422, as measured. | The coercion rule is a reimplementation of pydantic v1's behaviour and could drift from it. Bounded by the fact that it governs a mock only; the authoritative assertions live in the backend integration suite, which exercises the real route. |
| D9 | Model `GET /tweets/{tweet_id}` and `POST /tweets/{tweet_id}/responses` as an **unconditional 500** with the plain-text body `Internal Server Error`, and provide **no handler for their 404 branch**. | (a) 404 when the id is unknown, 500 otherwise. (b) 200, matching the route's declared return type. | Both handlers read `Tweet.id`, which the pydantic model does not declare, so they raise before either `if not tweet` branch is reached: every id produces 500. A 404 handler would model a branch no request can reach and would invite a suite to assert behaviour the application cannot exhibit. | If the production defect is ever fixed, these handlers become wrong. They are named for the behaviour rather than the route, and `ROUTE_CONTRACTS` records the reachability finding, so the mismatch surfaces at the same time as the fix. |
| D10 | Register a handler for `POST */generate-response`, a path the backend does not route, and answer it **404 in the current-behaviour layer**. | (a) Omit the handler and let `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` fire. (b) Omit it and add the endpoint to the backend. | `api.generateResponse` and `llmService.generateTweetResponse` both call this path, so without a handler neither service suite can run at all. (b) is forbidden: no product feature may be implemented. The 404 in the current-behaviour layer is the honest answer, and the nominal 200 in the isolation layer is what lets the service suites exercise the unwrapping of `data.generatedResponse`. | The nominal handler could read as evidence the endpoint exists. Mitigated by `backendContract: 'none - no router declares this path'` in the contract entry and by the mismatch rows in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`. |
| D11 | Register handlers for **exactly four routes** and no others — no `/users`, `/analytics`, `/config`, `/health`, `/api/trends` or `/api/config/twitter`, and no `rest.all('*')` catch-all. | (a) A catch-all returning 404 for everything unregistered. (b) Handlers for the empty routers, to document that they answer 404. | The four registered routes are the complete set that a module under `frontend/src/` requests. A catch-all would swallow every unexpected request and disable `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'`, which is the mechanism that makes an unmocked call fail loudly. The backend's 404 census belongs to the backend route-surface suite, where it is asserted against the real application. | A future frontend module that calls a fifth route fails with an unhandled-request error rather than a contract message. That is the intended signal, and the remedy is one contract entry plus one handler. |
| D12 | Serve tweet payloads built from `makeTweet()`, leaving `timestamp` to serialise to an **ISO-8601 string**, and export `makeDefaultTweetsJson()` as the value a suite asserts against. | (a) Pre-stringify the fixture. (b) Emit a value `tweetSchema` accepts. | The wire shape of a `datetime` is a string, and `frontend/src/schema/tweetSchema.ts` types `timestamp` as `z.date()`, so no raw JSON payload can satisfy that schema. Emitting a schema-satisfying value would hide the divergence; pre-stringifying would put a second tweet literal in the module. | A suite may expect `fetchTweets` to resolve to schema-valid objects. `makeDefaultTweetsJson` is the exported answer, and the `SerializedTweet` type states the difference in the type system. |
| D13 | Express the route contracts as **exported data** (`ROUTE_CONTRACTS`) that the screening runs on, rather than as prose beside the handlers. | (a) Prose comments only. (b) A separate Markdown table with no link to the code. | A description that the code does not execute drifts silently. Because the same `expectation` member drives the screening, a contract that no longer matches the handlers cannot go unnoticed, and a suite or an onboarding document can read the table directly. | The table mixes documentation fields with validation fields. The two groups are separated inside `RouteContract` and the validation half is a single named member. |
| D14 | Keep the isolation payload keys `generatedResponse` and `response` exactly as the callers read them. | Normalise both to one key. | `api.generateResponse` returns `response.data.generatedResponse` and the backend's responses route returns its text under `response`. The two keys differ in production; a mock that unified them would make a suite pass against a shape neither side produces. | None identified: the keys are asserted by the service suites and recorded in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md`. |
| D15 | Fix none of the production defects this seam exposes — the ignored `page`, the missing `skip`, the `Tweet.id` access, the absent `/generate-response` route, the `undefined` base URL, the one-argument `fetchTweets` call. | Correct the defects so mock and backend agree. | The requirements permit exactly two production touches, neither of which covers any of these, and forbid implementing missing product features even where a test asserts one. The obligation is to assert current behaviour, not to normalise it. | The suite encodes defects, so fixing one later breaks tests. Every such test is enumerated in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §A with the defect it pins, so the blast radius of a fix is known in advance. |
| D16 | **Supersedes D6. [The one-owner decision stands; the call list below is the list as of this row. D136 removed `resetAllowedRequestOrigins()` with the mutable allow-list, and the shipped hook now calls `assertNoIsolationViolations()`, then `server.resetHandlers()`, `resetHandlerState()` and a delete of `REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL`.]** Move all per-test cleanup of this module's mutable state into `src/test-utils/setup-jest.ts`, whose `afterEach` calls `server.resetHandlers()`, `resetAllowedRequestOrigins()` and `resetRecordedRequests()`. `handlers.ts` registers no Jest hook at all. | (a) Keep D6's import-time `afterEach` registration and add a second one for the origin set. (b) Leave both resets to each suite. (c) A single exported `resetHandlerState()` that suites call. | D6 registered its hook as an import side effect only because `setup-jest.ts` did not exist yet; it now does, and it is loaded once per test file through `setupFilesAfterEnv`. Two owners of one lifecycle is how the origin allow-list came to have an exported reset that nothing called: an origin added by one test survived into every later test in the file. (b) and (c) leave the guarantee to author discipline. One owner, three resets, no hook hidden in an import. | A suite that runs without `setup-jest.ts` — a hand-rolled Jest invocation with a different config — gets no reset at all. Bounded by the config being the only supported entry point, and by every reset staying exported for explicit use. |

---

## §2 Backend test infrastructure

Scope: `backend/pytest.ini`, `backend/tests/conftest.py`, `backend/tests/integration/conftest.py`,
`backend/tests/factories.py`. Every behaviour these decisions are calibrated against was obtained by
executing the code on CPython 3.9.25 with the pinned stack in `backend/requirements-dev.txt`.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D20 | Seed the eight required `Settings` fields, install the `Optional` shim and neutralise ambient Google credentials at **`conftest.py` module scope**, and import no `app.*` module there. | (a) An `autouse` fixture. (b) `env` entries via a pytest plugin such as `pytest-env`. (c) A committed `.env` file. | `app/core/config.py` runs `settings = Settings()` at import and seven further modules build their own `Settings()` the same way, so the values must exist before *collection* imports any test module — an `autouse` fixture runs far too late. (b) adds a dependency for one assignment loop. (c) puts test values in a file the application would also read. | Module-scope side effects are invisible from a test body. Bounded by `os.environ.setdefault`, so a deliberately exported value still wins, and by the module docstring naming every effect. |
| D21 | Expose the five missing-symbol shims (`Optional`, `verify_token`, `TwitterService`, `LLMService`, `add_response`) **only** as conftest fixtures, and give each of the four class/function shims a fresh object per test. | (a) Each suite installs its own with `patch`. (b) One session-scoped shim set. (c) Create the missing symbols in production. | (a) is how the same shim ends up spelled three different ways; (b) shares call bookkeeping between tests, so a count assertion depends on execution order; (c) implements missing product features, which the requirements forbid. A fixture per shim makes the substitution a declared dependency of each test. | A test that needs a shim and forgets the fixture fails with `AttributeError`/`NameError` rather than a message naming the shim. That is the intended signal: production fails the same way. |
| D22 | Install every shim on the **source module and on each consumer that binds it with `from … import …`**, and unbind it from all of them on teardown, driven by the `MISSING_SYMBOL_CONSUMERS` table. | (a) Patch the source module only. (b) Evict and re-import every consumer per test. (c) Patch each consumer inside the suites that need it. | (a) leaves `app.api.dependencies.verify_token`, `app.api.routes.tweets.LLMService` and `app.tasks.tweet_processor.LLMService` pointing at whichever object they captured first, so a later test passes against a leftover and a fixture-less test passes at all. (b) cannot be applied to `app.main` or the tweets router without breaking the `dependency_overrides` identity match and stacking a second CORS middleware (D23). (c) duplicates the boundary knowledge in every suite. | The table has to be kept in step with production imports. Bounded by the shims being *deleted* on teardown, which turns a stale table entry into an immediate failure rather than a silent pass. |
| D23 | Keep `app.main`, `app.api.routes.tweets`, `app.core.config`, `app.db.firestore` and `app.schema.tweet` **cached for the session**, and evict only `app.tasks.response_generator`. | (a) Reload every module per test. (b) Reload nothing, including `response_generator`. | `app.dependency_overrides` is keyed on the `get_db` object the router captured in its three `Depends(get_db)` defaults, so re-importing the router breaks every override; re-importing `app.core.config` creates a second `Settings` singleton; re-importing `app.main` stacks a second CORS middleware and a second copy of each router. `response_generator` runs `llm_service = LLMService()` at module scope, so it is the one module whose state genuinely has to be rebuilt. | Session-cached modules accumulate whatever a test does to them. Mitigated by D22, which is what makes the caching safe. |
| D24 | Import `app.main` and `app.api.dependencies` **early**, through `_import_get_db_consumers`, before any fixture replaces `app.db.firestore.get_db`. | (a) Let each fixture import them in whatever order it needs. (b) Key `dependency_overrides` on a re-read of `firestore.get_db`. | The router's `Depends(get_db)` defaults hold the function object present at *its* import time. If a fixture patched `get_db` first, the router would capture the mock and no override would ever match. (b) is the same bug from the other side. | An ordering rule enforced by a helper rather than by the type system. Bounded by the helper being idempotent and called from every fixture that patches `get_db`. |
| D25 | Permit a loopback connect **only to a port this process bound**, recording ports by wrapping `socket.socket.bind`/`listen`, and refuse non-loopback `getaddrinfo`/`gethostbyname`. | (a) Allow all loopback traffic, which is what the guard did before. (b) Block loopback entirely. (c) Block only `socket.socket.connect`, leaving resolution alone. | This host is shared between concurrent clones, so loopback also reaches a sibling's dev server or database — allowing it by address makes an accidental cross-clone dependency invisible. (b) breaks the asyncio `ProactorEventLoop` self-pipe and the starlette `TestClient` portal, both of which connect to a port they bound in-process through `socket.socketpair`. (c) lets a lookup of a real host leave the box before any connect is attempted. | Defense in depth, not a guarantee: a module that captured `socket.getaddrinfo` before the fixture ran keeps its own reference, and a C-level socket that never passes through `socket.socket` is not covered — which is exactly why the gRPC channel factories are blocked separately. |
| D26 | Block the gRPC channel factories by name (`grpc`, `grpc.aio`, `google.api_core.grpc_helpers[_async]`) in addition to the socket guard. | (a) Rely on the socket guard. (b) Uninstall the Google client libraries in the test environment. | `google-cloud-firestore` opens its transport in the gRPC C core, which never passes through `socket.socket`: an unpatched `get_tweet` reached Google Cloud for real and returned `NotFound: 404 The database (default) does not exist`. (b) would make the modules unimportable and the suite unable to assert their current behaviour. | The target list is version-specific. Bounded by each target being skipped when absent, so a version without one of them still runs. |
| D27 | Neutralise `google.auth.default` with a stand-in that **returns** a fake credential pair rather than raising. | (a) Let `DefaultCredentialsError` propagate from an absent key file. (b) Set `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` to a fake key file on disk. | A raising stand-in turns every unmocked data-access path into a credential error, which hides *which* boundary was left unpatched. Returning a credential lets the call proceed to the transport, where D25/D26 name the exact target. (b) puts a credential-shaped file in the repository. | A test could mistake the fake pair for real resolution. Bounded by `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` pointing at a path that never exists, so even a bypass reads nothing. |
| D28 | Build the `TestClient`s **without** entering them as context managers, and `close()` them on teardown. | (a) `with TestClient(app) as client:`. (b) Construct and never close. (c) Patch the startup and shutdown handlers, then use the context manager. | The startup handler calls `get_db()` and then `await start_tweet_stream()`, which blocks on a live Twitter stream — so the lifespan must not run, while the handlers must stay *registered* for the lifecycle suite to assert. (b) leaks an `httpx` transport per test. (c) asserts against handlers the test itself replaced. | The startup and shutdown bodies are never executed by any test. Recorded as a coverage ceiling in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` rather than closed. |
| D29 | Give every factory in `backend/tests/factories.py` an `**overrides` parameter and build a fresh mutable payload per call. | (a) Module-level fixture constants. (b) A second literal per edge case. | An edge case is then one mutated or omitted field away from the happy path, and no test can leak a mutation into another. (a) shares mutable state across tests; (b) duplicates the shape, which is how a fixture and its schema drift apart. | A caller can override a field into a shape production would never produce. Bounded by the schema suite validating the unmodified factory output. |

---

## §3 Frontend Jest toolchain

Scope: `frontend/jest.config.js`, `frontend/jest.transform.extensionless.js`, `frontend/src/test-utils/`.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D30 | Reach the four extension-less component **files** through a second Jest transformer that re-invokes `ts-jest` under a synthetic `.tsx` filename, plus `moduleFileExtensions` containing `''` and four explicit `moduleNameMapper` entries. | (a) Rename the four files to `.tsx`. (b) Move them into directories with an `index.tsx`. (c) Skip component testing. | (a) and (b) are production changes outside the two authorized touches, and the requirements direct that where several approaches exist the least invasive wins. (c) abandons the component layer entirely. The transformer keeps every production byte where it is. | Two transformers must agree on compiler options; they are declared once each and documented as mirrored. A cache-key suffix keeps their outputs distinct. |
| D31 | Construct `TsJestTransformer` directly in the wrapper rather than calling `createTransformer`. | Use `require('ts-jest').createTransformer(...)`. | `createTransformer` is not a function in ts-jest 29.4.12; the class is the supported entry point at this pin. | Tied to ts-jest's internal export shape. Bounded by the exact pin in `package.json` and by the transformer being exercised by every component suite. |
| D32 | Run ts-jest with an **inline `tsconfig`** and `diagnostics: false`. | (a) Point at `frontend/tsconfig.json`. (b) Add a test-only `tsconfig.jest.json`. (c) Leave diagnostics on. | `frontend/tsconfig.json` references a `tsconfig.node.json` that does not exist, which breaks every `.tsx` transform that reads it; and the sources do not typecheck (34 errors), so diagnostics on would fail every suite before a single assertion ran. (b) is another file to keep in step for no gain. | Type errors in test code are not caught by the runner. Accepted: the suite's job is to assert runtime behaviour, and `npx tsc --noEmit` remains available. |
| D33 | Map the three non-resolvable bare specifiers (`app/schema/tweet`, `app/schema/user`, `app/services/api`) and the three non-existent modules (`@/services/analyticsService`, `@/services/configService`, `../schema/configSchema`) in `moduleNameMapper`, to real sources and to test-side stubs respectively. | (a) Edit the imports in production source. (b) Add `paths` to `tsconfig.json`. (c) `jest.mock` in each suite. | (a) is an unauthorized production change; (b) edits a file outside the authorized configuration surface and would not help Jest resolve anything by itself; (c) repeats six entries in every suite. | A mapper entry can outlive the defect it works around. Each is listed in `frontend/TESTING.md` with the importer it serves, and the stub modules are named for what they stand in for. |
| D34 | Build the test store in `render.tsx` from the two slices' **default reducer exports**, and never import `src/store/index.ts`. | (a) Import the real store. (b) Repair `src/store/index.ts`. | The real store imports `{ tweetReducer }`/`{ configReducer }`, which the slices never export, so it has no valid reducer and `getState()` returns `{}` — every connected component would render against an empty state. (b) is an unauthorized production change. | The suites exercise a store shape the application does not actually build. Recorded as a divergence, and `src/store/index.test.ts` asserts the real module's current behaviour directly. |
| D35 | Scope Jest's `coverageThreshold` to `./src/store`, `./src/schema` and `./src/services` with no global group. | (a) A global threshold. (b) Per-file thresholds. (c) Codecov-only enforcement. | Those three packages are the ones the requirements put a number on (≥80%); a global gate would fail on `src/pages` and the component ceilings, which no test can close (see §7). (c) would leave the runner silent, and the requirements put the gate in the runner. | A regression in an unthresholded directory is not caught by the gate. Bounded by the coverage report still being produced for the whole `collectCoverageFrom` set. |
| D36 | Declare `jest-junit` inside `reporters` in the config file, and include `'json'` in `coverageReporters`. | (a) Pass `--reporters=jest-junit` on the command line. (b) Rely on `lcov` alone. | The CLI form mis-parses a positional test path as a third reporter and fails with `Could not resolve a module for a custom reporter`. `coverage/coverage-final.json`, which the existing Codecov step uploads, is written by the `json` reporter specifically. | Two places now describe reporting: this config and the CI workflow. The workflow's paths are asserted against the reporter list in §8. |
| D37 | Add `addFileAttribute`, `reportTestSuiteErrors` and `includeConsoleOutput` to the `jest-junit` options. | (a) Keep the minimal option set. (b) Add a second custom reporter for suite errors. | Without `reportTestSuiteErrors` a file that fails to *load* contributes no `<testcase>` at all, which reads downstream as "no tests here" rather than "this suite is broken" — the exact failure mode this project started from. The other two attach the `file` attribute CI annotators read and carry the correlated console lines into `<system-out>`. | `includeConsoleOutput` produces nothing when Jest switches to verbose mode, which it does automatically for a single test file; the comment in the config states this. |

---

## §4 End-to-end harness and runner

Scope: `e2e/**`. Every behaviour below was observed against the running harness, and the four routes were
verified in headless Chromium.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D40 | Serve the E2E layer from a harness owned entirely by `e2e/`, with its own `index.html` and route table, and **never mount `frontend/src/app.tsx`**. | (a) Add `frontend/index.html` + `frontend/vite.config.ts` and mount the real entry. (b) Run Playwright against the preview build. | The application cannot be served as it stands: there is no HTML entry at the Vite root, `npm run build` fails at both legs, and `app.tsx` imports the invalid store, imports a `setupInterceptors` symbol nothing exports, and default-imports a named-only export. (a) is a set of unauthorized production files. | The E2E layer exercises the components, not the real application entry. Stated in `e2e/README.md` and in the traceability matrix, so no result is read as covering `app.tsx`. |
| D41 | Resolve the four extension-less component files through a **NUL-prefixed virtual module id** whose `load` hook hands the real source to `transformWithEsbuild` under a synthetic `.tsx` name. | (a) A `?extless` query suffix. (b) A synthetic `<path>.extless.tsx` id. (c) Rename the files. | Vite normalises (a) and (b) back to the real on-disk path and the `load` hook never fires — established by trying both. (c) is an unauthorized production change. The resolver matches both `@/components/X` and the already-aliased absolute path, because `resolve.alias` runs before `enforce: 'pre'` plugins. | Depends on Vite's virtual-id convention. Pinned to vite 4.5.14 and exercised by every harness route. |
| D42 | Keep the compatibility exports (`api`, `getTweets`, `TweetCard`) at the literal value `undefined`. | (a) Supply working implementations. (b) Throw on access. (c) Omit them and let the module fail to link. | `undefined` is the value these imports already have under Jest and CommonJS, so the harness reaches the same branch the rest of the suite does. (a) implements missing product features; (b) invents a failure mode production does not have; (c) makes the importing module unloadable, so nothing at all could be tested. | A non-empty tweet collection renders `undefined` as an element type and unmounts the whole route — confirmed in Chromium, `#root` becomes empty. D43 is what keeps that dormant, and the matrix carries it as an assertion obligation. |
| D43 | **[Superseded by D126 — the success defaults below were replaced by a fail-closed responder; the wording is the decision as originally taken, not the shipped behaviour.]** Have the harness dev server answer the three API paths the mounted components request — `GET /undefined/tweets` → `[]`, `GET /api/trends` → `e2e/fixtures/trends.json`, `POST /api/config/twitter` → `{}`. | (a) Answer nothing and let each spec intercept. (b) Proxy to a real backend. (c) Answer with a catch-all. | With nothing installed, Vite's SPA fallback returns `index.html` to a `fetch` and `404` to an axios XHR, so `/` produced an unhandled promise rejection and `/analytics` "passed" only because HTML failed to parse as JSON. A spec-independent default makes every route deterministic on its own, and `page.route` still overrides it in the browser. (b) needs credentials and a network; (c) would shadow the client routes. | The default responses are a fixture, not the backend. Each entry is matched on an exact method and path that no client route can collide with, and the table names the caller it serves. |
| D44 | Make the harness's `getTrendData` **always reject**, after checking the status and parsing the body. | (a) Resolve with the fixture. (b) Resolve with `null`. (c) Serve a non-2xx status. | `frontend/src/components/Analytics` builds a `Chart` from the tree-shakeable `{ Chart }` export without calling `Chart.register`, so any renderable series throws out of a `useEffect` and unmounts the route — the `/analytics` screenshot from an earlier run is a blank page for exactly this reason. Rejecting holds the component on the caught-failure path the coverage ceiling names, and doing it *after* the parse keeps the fixture genuinely exercised. (b) discards the payload silently; (c) would stop testing the success side of the transport. | A reader could take the rejection for a transport failure. Bounded by a named error class (`UnrenderableTrendSeriesError`) whose message states the cause and the label count it refused. |
| D45 | Derive the harness port from `CLONE_INDEX` (`4173 + index`, `HARNESS_PORT` overriding) in a single module both configs import. | (a) Keep the fixed `4173`. (b) Let Vite pick a free port and read it back. (c) Duplicate the formula in both files. | A TCP port is host-global and several clones of this repository run at once, so a fixed port lets one run attach to another's server and invalidate every request and artifact it records. (b) makes `webServer.url` unknowable in advance. (c) is two formulas that can drift. | A clone that does not export `CLONE_INDEX` falls back to the base port and can still collide. Bounded by `strictPort`, which fails the start rather than silently moving, and by `reuseExistingServer: false` (D46). |
| D46 | Never reuse an existing dev server: `reuseExistingServer: false` in every environment. | Keep `!process.env.CI`, reusing locally. | Reuse cannot tell the harness this run started from an unrelated process already holding the port; combined with a shared host that is how a spec ends up asserting against someone else's build. | A developer with a harness already running gets a failed start instead of a fast one. That is the intended trade, and the message names the port. |
| D47 | Retain a Playwright trace on **every failing test** (`trace: 'retain-on-failure'`). | (a) `'on-first-retry'`, the shape before this decision. (b) `'on'`. (c) A local retry so the retry-based trace fires. | Local runs have `retries: 0`, so `'on-first-retry'` produced no trace at all where debugging actually happens. (b) records a trace for every passing test too, for artifacts nobody reads. (c) trades determinism for evidence. | Traces for a broadly failing run take disk space. Bounded to failures only, and `e2e/test-results/` is gitignored. |
| D48 | Give `e2e/` its own `package.json` and alias `react`, `react-dom`, `react-redux`, `react-router-dom` and `@reduxjs/toolkit` back into `frontend/node_modules`, with `resolve.dedupe`. | (a) Put the Playwright and Vite dependencies in `frontend/package.json`. (b) A workspace root manifest. | The harness config lives in `e2e/`, so its own `import … from 'vite'` cannot resolve without a manifest there; and two copies of React would produce "Invalid hook call" the moment a component used a hook. (a) mixes the E2E stack into the application manifest, whose authorized surface is devDependencies and scripts for the *Jest* layer. (b) restructures the repository. | Two manifests to keep in step, and no lockfile in either (D93). The React aliases are listed explicitly and deduped, so a second copy fails loudly rather than subtly. |
| D49 | Invoke every E2E command through the `e2e` package — `npm test`, `npm --prefix e2e test`, `npm run harness` — and never `npx` from the repository root. | (a) `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the root, as previously documented. (b) Absolute paths into `node_modules/.bin`. | The repository root has no manifest, so a root `npx playwright` cannot resolve the pinned 1.44.1 binary and may fetch a different version — which on Node 16 is a version that does not run. `npm run` puts `e2e/node_modules/.bin` on `PATH`, which is pinned and cross-platform. (b) needs a different spelling per OS. | Everything now depends on `e2e/package.json` scripts staying accurate. They are four lines, and each documented command names the script it runs. |

---

## §5 The two authorized production touches

Scope: `backend/app/api/routes/` and `backend/app/core/config.py`. Each touch carries a terse
`# TESTING:` marker in the source; the reasoning is here, per the requirements' comment convention and
Rule 1's prohibition on rationale in code.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D50 | Convert the extension-less `app/api/routes` **file** into a package of four modules, `tweets.py` carrying the three endpoints it already implemented and the other three a bare `APIRouter()`, with no `__init__.py`. | (a) Change `app/main.py` to import from the file. (b) Put all four routers in one module. (c) Add an `__init__.py`. | `app/main.py` executes `from app.api.routes import tweets, users, analytics, config`, so the application object cannot be constructed at all until those module names exist; the import statement is a contract the requirements protect. (c) would diverge from the repository's PEP 420 namespace convention, which no other directory under `app/` breaks. | Three of the four modules are empty routers, which could read as a place to add endpoints. Each carries a marker saying it declares none, and the route-surface suite asserts their paths return 404. |
| D51 | Reorder the parameters of `get_tweet` and `generate_response` to put `tweet_id` first. | (a) Give `tweet_id` a default. (b) Leave the file uncompilable and skip every app-level test. | The original does not compile — `SyntaxError: non-default argument follows default argument` at two lines — so no byte-for-byte copy is possible. FastAPI binds path parameters and dependencies **by name**, so the reordering is behaviour-preserving and the OpenAPI schema is identical. (a) changes the HTTP contract by making the path parameter optional. | A reader could take the reordering for a signature change. Bounded by the `# TESTING: param order` marker on both lines and by the integration suite asserting the endpoints' current behaviour. |
| D52 | Declare exactly four undeclared `Settings` fields, with least-privilege defaults: `ALLOWED_ORIGINS: List[str] = []`, `ALGORITHM: str = "HS256"`, `PROJECT_ID: str = ""`, `TWITTER_TRACK_KEYWORDS: List[str] = []`. | (a) `ALLOWED_ORIGINS = ["*"]`. (b) `PROJECT_ID` aliased to `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT`. (c) Also declare `TWITTER_CONSUMER_KEY`/`_SECRET`. (d) Declare nothing and skip the app-level tests. | These four are read at runtime and declared nowhere, so each raises `AttributeError` and the application cannot be constructed. A default that grants nothing cannot widen production behaviour: `["*"]` would silently open CORS, and aliasing `PROJECT_ID` would invent a data-access target that does not exist today. (c) would take the touch beyond the four fields the requirements name — the two consumer keys are supplied by `monkeypatch` in the two tests that need them instead. | An empty `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` means CORS allows no origin in production if the value is not configured. That is strictly narrower than today's behaviour, which is a crash, and it is recorded as a suggested next task. |
| D53 | Fix nothing else in production, including the eleven-plus defects the suite now pins. | Correct the defects so the tests could assert intended behaviour. | The requirements authorize exactly two touches, forbid implementing missing product features "even where existing tests assert them", and direct that current behaviour be asserted as it is. | Tests encode defects, so fixing one later breaks tests. Every such test is enumerated in `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` with the defect it pins, so the blast radius is known before the fix. |

---

## §6 Legacy suite migration

Scope: `backend/tests/test_api.py`, `test_services.py`, `test_tasks.py` — 216 lines, 25 test functions,
six with empty bodies, all three non-collectable. The bidirectional mapping is
`TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §E.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D60 | **Delete** the three legacy modules after dispositioning every function, rather than editing them in place. | (a) Repair them where they are. (b) Keep them alongside the new suite, marked `xfail`. | Each imports from a different non-existent root and fails at collection; of the 25 functions, 15 assert routes or classes that do not exist, so repairing in place would mean deleting most of each file's contents and rewriting the rest — the same result with a misleading history. The requirements name them intent documentation rather than a baseline. (b) leaves a permanently red suite. | Deleting removes the record of intent from the tree. Replaced by §E of the matrix, which lists all 25 with their disposition, and by git history. |
| D61 | Collapse the three import roots to `app.*`, enforced structurally by `pythonpath = .` in `pytest.ini` plus invoking pytest from `backend/`. | (a) `sys.path` manipulation in a conftest. (b) A `src`-layout install (`pip install -e .`). (c) `rootdir` in the ini. | One root is what makes every patch target unambiguous, and the ini plus working directory achieve it with no code. (a) is what the requirements forbid; (b) needs a `setup.py`/`pyproject.toml` the repository does not have; (c) is not a valid ini key — pytest reports `Unknown config option: rootdir`. | Running pytest from the repository root silently finds no tests. `backend/tests/README.md` and every documented command name the working directory. |
| D62 | Turn the three empty stubs into `pytest.mark.skip` with a reason naming the unimplemented feature, and drop the two oracle-free assertions entirely. | (a) Implement media handling, rate limiting and deduplication. (b) Delete the stubs silently. (c) Migrate the oracle-free assertions as they are. | No media, rate-limiting or deduplication logic exists, and implementing it is forbidden — a skip with a reason is a record rather than a silence. `assertIn(sentiment, [...])` and `0 <= rate <= 1` pass for any implementation whatsoever, so migrating them would add green tests that assert nothing. | A skip can be mistaken for a flaky test. Each reason names the missing production feature, and §E records why. |

---

## §7 Coverage scope and gates

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D70 | Apply `--cov-fail-under=90` to `app/core`, `app/services`, `app/tasks` and `app/db` rather than to the whole `app` tree. | (a) `--cov=app --cov-fail-under=90`. (b) A tree-wide gate with `omit` entries for the unreachable regions. (c) Report to Codecov only. | Several branches cannot be executed without changing production: the startup/shutdown bodies, the dead code after `start_twitter_stream`'s `NameError`, and the unreachable 404 of `GET /tweets/{id}`. A tree-wide 90 would therefore fail for reasons no test can fix. The four packages named are the ones the requirement names. (b) hides the ceilings inside a config option instead of stating them; (c) leaves the runner silent. | Coverage of `app/main.py` and `app/api/` is measured but not gated. Both are exercised by the integration layer, and their ceilings are enumerated in the matrix. |
| D71 | Exclude `src/app.tsx` from `collectCoverageFrom`, and keep the four extension-less component paths in it explicitly. | (a) Leave `app.tsx` in the denominator at 0%. (b) Add a coverage-ignore comment in the file. | `app.tsx` cannot be mounted at all (D40), so its zeros are a permanent, uninformative drag on every percentage. The four component files are not matched by a `*.{ts,tsx}` glob, so without explicit entries they would silently vanish from the denominator. (b) is a production edit. | A future change that makes `app.tsx` mountable would be measured only after the exclusion is removed. Recorded here and in §F of the matrix. |
| D72 | Treat `pytest --collect-only -q` reporting zero errors as a first-class readiness gate. | Rely on the test run alone. | The suite this project inherited failed at *collection*: three modules, zero executed assertions. A gate that proves every module imports is the check that would have caught the original state, and it is meaningful in a way a passing-test count is not. | An extra command to run. It takes under a second and is documented alongside the test commands. |

---

## §8 Observability of the test system

Rule 2 applied to the deliverable actually being built, per conflict C1 in the technical specification:
the *test system* is instrumented, and the production instrumentation gaps are recorded as next tasks
(see the scope note at the top of this document). **Reused**, not added: the two existing Codecov upload
steps in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and their `backend` and `frontend` flags.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D80 | Put `test_id` (the pytest nodeid) and `correlation_id` (a stable 8-hex blake2s digest of it) on every log record through `logging.setLogRecordFactory`, and reference `correlation_id` from `log_cli_format` and `log_format`. | (a) A `logging.Filter` on the root logger. (b) A filter attached to pytest's live-log handler. (c) A marker line logged at the start of each test. | The formats are applied to records from production code, from pytest and from third-party libraries such as `httpx`; a factory is the only hook that reaches all of them, and a missing attribute would make the formatter fail. (a) applies only to records logged directly on that logger; (b) covers one of three handlers. (c) brackets a test's output instead of attributing each line. | A record created before the conftest is imported would lack the attribute. Bounded by the conftest being the first module collection imports, and verified: a full run emits no logging errors. |
| D81 | Derive the correlation id by digest rather than by counter. | (a) A per-session counter. (b) A UUID per test. | The same test yields the same id on every run and on every machine, so two runs are comparable line by line — proven by reproducing `2e1b1f04` for one nodeid in a separate process. (a) and (b) change between runs, which is what makes two logs uncomparable. | Digest collision. At four bytes over a suite of this size the probability is negligible, and `test_id` carries the unabbreviated nodeid alongside. |
| D82 | Set `junit_logging = log` with `junit_log_passing_tests = false`. | (a) `junit_logging = no`, the default. (b) `junit_logging = all` for every test. | A failing `<testcase>` then carries the correlated log that explains it, in the artifact CI keeps, while an all-passing run stays lean — measured at 8,629 bytes for 66 tests with zero `<system-out>` elements. (a) leaves the artifact diagnostics-free; (b) inflates every run with output nobody reads. | A passing test's logs are not archived. They are on stdout, and a failure is the case that needs the archive. |
| D83 | Stamp the frontend's per-test id (`<test file> > <full test name>`) on every recorded msw request and on every contract-violation console line. | (a) Rely on Jest's own attribution of the failing assertion. (b) A random per-request id. | A request issued from a mount effect can settle *after* the test that started it — that is how an unmocked axios call once failed a later, unrelated test. An id on the record makes that visible, and the same file-and-name pair is the `<testcase>` classname and name, so a request, a console line and a result all lead back to one test. (b) correlates a request to nothing. | The id is read from `expect.getState()`, a Jest internal. Bounded by a guard that yields `no-test` when it is unavailable — which is itself the signal that a request escaped its test. |

---

## §9 Dependencies and reproducibility

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D90 | Pin `pytest==8.4.2` (and the 8.x-compatible plugin set) rather than 7.4.4 or the current 9.x. | (a) pytest 7.4.4, also validated. (b) pytest 9.1.1, the current release. | Python 3.9 support is dropped in the 9.0 line (changelog `#13719`), and `ci.yml` pins `python-version: [3.9]`, so 8.4.2 is the highest release that runs on the documented runtime. 7.4.4 works but forgoes two years of fixes for nothing. | The pin has to move when the runtime does. Every pin sits in one file with the constraint stated next to it. |
| D91 | Pin `httpx==0.27.2`, below 0.28. | Take the current httpx. | Starlette 0.27 passes `app=` to `httpx.Client`, a shortcut httpx removed in 0.28: with 0.28.1 installed, `TestClient(app)` fails outright with `TypeError: __init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'app'`. Discovered by installing it, not by reading release notes. | The remaining `DeprecationWarning` is suppressed by a targeted `filterwarnings` entry, which would also hide a genuine future deprecation of the same text. |
| D92 | **Reject** `jest-canvas-mock` plus a `ResizeObserver` stub. | Install both, as a Chart.js component would normally require. | They make the outcome strictly worse: with the canvas mock alone Chart.js reaches `ReferenceError: ResizeObserver is not defined`, and with both it reaches `Error: "linear" is not a registered scale`, which escapes to React and *unmounts* `TrendCharts`, leaving nothing to assert. Unshimmed, the component stays mounted with its heading and canvas. | The chart branch is never covered. It is unreachable in a real browser too, for the same missing `Chart.register`, and is recorded as a ceiling rather than a gap. |
| D93 | Commit no lockfile for `frontend` or `e2e`, and use `npm install` rather than `npm ci`. | (a) Commit `package-lock.json` for both. (b) Commit one for `e2e` only. | The authorized frontend configuration surface is `package.json`; a lockfile is a new file whose content is generated by whichever npm and registry state produced it, and the requirements direct the least invasive option. | Installs are not reproducible: the warmed tree already showed `react-router-dom` resolving to a version other than the manifest's, and this is exactly the class of drift a lockfile prevents. Carried as a suggested next task, and the manifest now pins the version the suite was validated against. |

## §10 Harness security hardening and determinism guarantees

Scope: the files that make the three suites deterministic and offline — the backend dependency manifest and
`conftest.py` prologue, the frontend msw seam and its Jest setup module, and the Playwright runner and Vite
harness configuration. §1 records how the frontend mock answers a request; this section records how the
suites are prevented from reaching a real credential, a real host or a neighbouring test's state.

Every outcome named below was measured, not assumed: each row's "Why" rests on behaviour reproduced in this
repository on Windows with CPython 3.9.25, Node 22.23.1, msw 1.3.5, vite 4.5.14 and Chromium 125.0.6422.26.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D100 | Pin `python-jose[cryptography]==3.3.0` in `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, matching AAP §0.6.1 and the version installed in `.venv-backend`. | (a) Pin `3.5.0`, which carries fixes for CVE-2024-33664 (JWE decryption resource consumption) and CVE-2024-33663 (algorithm confusion with an OpenSSH ECDSA key), and install it. (b) Leave manifest and environment disagreeing and record the drift. (c) Add a constraints file that overrides the pin. | AAP §0.6.1 fixes this manifest at 3.3.0, and the warmed environment supplies 3.3.0, so 3.3.0 is the only value at which the manifest, the AAP and the installed tree all agree. D101's closure gate asserts that agreement and fails the suite when it breaks, so (b) is not available. The suite exercises only the HS256 encode/decode round trip in `app/core/security.py`; it never decrypts a JWE and never loads an OpenSSH ECDSA key, so neither CVE is reachable from any test in this repository. | The two CVEs remain present in the pinned distribution even though no test reaches them, so any future production use of JWE decryption or ECDSA key loading would be exposed. Recorded as a suggested next task under Rule 3 rather than fixed here, because raising the pin above the AAP's value is a dependency decision a human owns. |
| D101 | Enforce manifest-versus-installed agreement with a **pytest gate**, `backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py`, that parses every `==` pin out of the manifest and asserts `importlib.metadata.version(...)` matches. | (a) `pip check` alone. (b) A CI-only `pip freeze` diff. (c) Hash-pinned requirements via pip-tools. (d) Nothing beyond the manifest. | `pip check` validates the dependency graph's internal consistency and says nothing about whether the installed version is the pinned one — it reported "No broken requirements found" while the installed python-jose differed from the pinned one, which is precisely how that drift survived. A CI-only check does not protect a developer's local run, and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` is outside this checkpoint's scope. Hash pinning is a larger change to a manifest the AAP fixes. A test fails in the same run as the suite it protects, for the same reason, in the same output. | The gate parses the manifest with a pattern that recognises `==` pins only, so a future range specifier is silently unchecked; a guard test asserts the pin list is non-empty so the parametrisation can never degenerate to zero cases. It also makes an environment problem present as a test failure, which reads as a code failure until the message is read — the message names the distribution, the pinned version and the installed one. |
| D102 | Keep `starlette==0.27.0` and record a **documented exception** for GHSA-f96h-pmfr-66vw / CVE-2024-47874 as a terse marker in the manifest. | (a) Upgrade starlette. (b) Upgrade fastapi so a patched starlette is admissible. (c) Add an explicit `python-multipart` exclusion. | The advisory is a multipart form-parsing denial of service. It is unreachable in this environment: `python-multipart` is not installed, no route declares `Form(...)` or `UploadFile`, and no implemented endpoint accepts a request body at all. Upgrading starlette breaks the AAP's `fastapi==0.95.2` pin, and that pin is load-bearing for `TestClient` under `httpx==0.27.2` — the combination the whole integration layer is calibrated against. | The exception becomes wrong the moment a form route or `python-multipart` is added. The marker sits on the pin itself, so it is in the diff of any change to that line, and D101 makes the installed version verifiable at every run. |
| D103 | **[The pattern half is superseded by D169 — the absolute patterns now spell out the base path prefix rather than carrying a leading wildcard; the ledger and the frozen allow-list stand.]** **Supersedes D7.** Register every msw handler as an absolute pattern under a **frozen** two-entry loopback allow-list (`http://localhost`, `http://127.0.0.1`), delete `allowRequestOrigin()` and `resetAllowedRequestOrigins()`, and make an out-of-scope origin or an unhandled request a **non-swallowable ledger entry** that `assertNoIsolationViolations()` raises on after every test. | (a) Keep the mutable allow-list with an `afterEach` reset. (b) Keep answering a disallowed origin with the catchable 599 of D4. (c) Keep bare `*/…` patterns and rely on `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'`. | A process-global mutable allow-list is order-dependent state of exactly the kind the suite exists to eliminate: one test widening it leaves every later test able to answer a real host, and a reset hook only narrows the window. 599 is a *response*, so `llmService.generateTweetResponse` and `twitterService` — both of which catch and rethrow — convert an escape attempt into a green test; the review found this exact false-green. A ledger is not catchable by the code under test: the entry outlives the request and the assertion runs outside the component. Host-agnostic patterns were also measured answering `http://evil.example.com/undefined/tweets`. | A suite that legitimately drives an absolute non-loopback URL must install its own handler for that exact URL with `server.use(...)`; the violation message names the origin, the allow-list and that remedy. Each layer now registers two handlers per route instead of one, which is visible in array length assertions — both layers document it and the layer-2 factories are named in the plural. |
| D104 | Seed the eight required settings by **unconditional assignment** at `conftest.py` module scope, pop every field the suite expects to fall back to its default, snapshot and restore all of it in `pytest_unconfigure`, and assert at session start that the already-constructed `Settings` singletons hold the synthetic values. | (a) `os.environ.setdefault`, the shape reviewed. (b) An autouse `monkeypatch.setenv` fixture. (c) A committed `.env.test` file. | With `setdefault`, an ambient real value wins — a developer or CI runner with a real `SECRET_KEY`, `OPENAI_API_KEY` or `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT` exported has the suite silently exercise it, and `POPULARITY_THRESHOLD=5` in the environment would rewrite the boundary matrix's oracle. An autouse fixture cannot help at all: `app/core/config.py` runs `settings = Settings()` at import, which happens during collection, before any fixture. The singleton assertion is what turns a leak that would otherwise skew assertions into an error naming the field. | A developer who deliberately exports one of these variables to explore behaviour finds it overridden inside the suite. That is the intent; the override set is a single named constant so what is managed is readable in one place. Restoring in `pytest_unconfigure` keeps the process the pytest run leaves behind identical to the one it entered. |
| D105 | Install a **process-wide egress guard as the last step of the `conftest.py` module prologue** — before collection — covering DNS resolution, `socket.connect` / `connect_ex` / `sendto`, `socket.create_connection`, the gRPC channel factories, the Windows proactor connect paths and network-capable child processes, and release it only in `pytest_unconfigure`. | (a) The reviewed per-test autouse fixture patching TCP connect and some gRPC entry points. (b) `pytest-socket`. (c) Host-level firewall rules or an offline CI runner. | Three measured bypasses drove this. `app/db/firestore.py` and `app/db/bigquery.py` construct clients at module scope, so the first opportunity to reach the network is **collection**, which a per-test fixture is too late for — a probe confirmed `socket.create_connection(('firestore.googleapis.com', 443))` succeeding at collection under the reviewed shape. A TCP-only patch leaves DNS and UDP open, and `google.auth.default()` genuinely resolves here, returning a real ambient project id (redacted here as `<project-id>`; read it from the failure itself if you need it — see D203). And a call made during teardown, after the fixture has been released, escaped entirely. `pytest-socket` is a new dependency the AAP does not pin and does not cover DNS, gRPC factories or subprocesses. | Over-blocking is the live risk, and it materialised: a blanket child-process ban broke the run, because Windows `platform.uname()` shells out to `cmd /c ver`, which `aiohttp` triggers at import (via `openai` 0.27) and pytest's JUnit reporter triggers at session finish. The remedy is in the code — `platform.uname()` is warmed in the prologue and child-process refusal is narrowed to while a test executes — and loopback remains permitted so `TestClient` works. A future dependency that needs a subprocess mid-test will fail loudly with the command in the message. |
| D106 | Replace the missing production symbols (`verify_token`, `TwitterService`, `LLMService`, `add_response`) with **fail-closed sentinels** that raise `MissingProductionSymbolError`, write the sentinel into the defining module *and* every already-imported consumer, and restore the sentinel over any per-test replacement when the fixture exits. | (a) The reviewed truthy `MagicMock`. (b) Implement the four symbols. (c) Skip every test whose import chain needs them. | `app/api/dependencies.py` returns whatever `verify_token` gives it, so a truthy `MagicMock` authorizes **any** token — an authentication test that should prove a 401 passes for the wrong reason. Consumers bind the shim at import, so replacing it later in the defining module leaves the permissive copy in place; the eviction pass is what makes the replacement total. (b) is forbidden — no missing product feature may be implemented. (c) would leave `app.main` untestable, since the routes package imports `TwitterService`. | One consumer cannot take a sentinel: `app/tasks/response_generator.py` runs `llm_service = LLMService()` at module scope, so its fixture supplies a constructible stand-in. That single permissive seam is named in the fixture's own docstring, and every other consumer of `LLMService` calls it inside a function body, where the sentinel holds. |
| D107 | Yield the integration `TestClient`s from fixtures and `close()` them, and clear `app.dependency_overrides` in an autouse fixture **before and after** every integration test. | (a) Return the clients and let the process reclaim them. (b) Clean overrides only in the fixture that installs one, as reviewed. (c) Enter the client as a context manager so lifespan handles the teardown. | An unclosed `TestClient` leaks its transport for the session, and an override installed directly — not through the opt-in fixture — survived into later tests, which is a genuine order dependence rather than a tidiness concern. (c) is not available: entering the context manager runs the startup handler, which calls `get_db().connect()` and `start_tweet_stream()`, and the AAP requires the client be constructed without it. | None material. Clearing before as well as after means a test that inherits a dirty map from an earlier failure still starts clean. |
| D108 | Start msw interception **synchronously while `setup-jest.ts` is being evaluated**, not in `beforeAll`. | (a) `server.listen()` in `beforeAll`, the shape reviewed. (b) A per-suite guard each test file installs. (c) Accept the window and forbid import-time requests by convention. | Jest evaluates a test module — and therefore every import-time side effect in its graph — before it runs `beforeAll`. A module-scope `axios.get(...)` in any suite or any module it imports therefore executes with no interceptor installed. Reproduced directly: with `listen()` moved back into `beforeAll`, a module-scope request produced `ESCAPED AxiosError: Network Error`, an attempt on a real socket; with `listen()` at module scope the same request is intercepted. (b) is per-suite discipline, which is the failure mode. | Interception is active during every suite's module evaluation, including suites that expected no HTTP at all. Harmless by construction: the handlers answer only the four contract routes on a loopback origin, and anything else is a ledger entry that fails the test loudly rather than a silent 200. The `resetHandlers`/`close` teardown hooks are unchanged. |
| D109 | Pin `react-router-dom` to **6.22.3** in `frontend/package.json`, matching AAP §0.6.1 and the installed tree, rather than the 6.30.4 the manifest carried. | (a) 6.30.5 or later in the 6.x line. (b) 7.18.0 or later. (c) Keep 6.30.4 and document it. | 6.30.4 sits inside the affected range of GHSA-jjmj-jmhj-qwj2 (CVE-2026-53668), `>=6.30.2 <=6.30.4`, and no 6.x patch release exists — (a) is not available. (b) is a major-version change the AAP does not authorise and whose API differs. 6.22.3 is outside the affected range, is the AAP's pin, and is what `frontend/node_modules` already resolves, so the committed graph is the one that was actually exercised — the manifest previously described a tree nobody had validated. | A residual remains and is not closed by this row: advisory data for the 6.x line reports `react-router 6.0.0 - 7.17.0` and `react-router-dom 6.0.0-alpha.0 - 6.30.2` as affected by a high-severity advisory, so **no react-router 6.x release is advisory-free** — only `>=7.18.0` is. Escalated as a divergence in the resolution report rather than presented as clean. |
| D110 | Keep `vite@4.5.14` and close GHSA-fx2h-pf6j-xcff / CVE-2026-53571 with a **request guard in `e2e/vite.harness.config.ts`** that refuses NTFS alternate-data-stream syntax and Windows 8.3 short names on every request path, `/@fs/` and root-relative alike, before any Vite middleware sees it. | (a) Upgrade to a patched release — 6.4.3, 7.3.5 or 8.0.16. (b) Rely on Vite's own `server.fs.deny`. (c) Drop the e2e layer. | No 4.x release carries the fix, so closing it by version means a major upgrade: the AAP pins 4.5.14 and the harness design depends on behaviours verified against 4.x — `transformWithEsbuild`, alias resolution running before `enforce: 'pre'` plugins, NUL-prefixed virtual ids and `esbuild.tsconfigRaw`. (b) is what the advisory defeats: measured against the original guard, `/.env::$DATA` and `/.env::$DATA?raw` both returned **200 with file contents**; under the shipped guard both return 403, alongside the single-colon, percent-encoded and short-name spellings. The dev server also binds `127.0.0.1` only, which removes the advisory's exposure precondition. | The guard is bespoke, so a spelling nobody thought of is not covered — it screens the raw path and the fully percent-decoded path, refuses any colon outside a `/@fs/` drive letter, and refuses `~<digit>` anywhere, which is the whole published class. It is also strictly a dev-server guard: nothing here protects a production build, and none exists. |
| D111 | **[The `browsers:verify` half is superseded by D195 — a `--dry-run` reports the location and exits 0 whether or not a browser is there, so it is no longer the download-free check; the provision-out-of-band remedy and the version pin stand.]** Keep `@playwright/test@1.44.1` and take CVE-2025-59288's **other** remedy — provision browsers only from artifacts verified out of band, with `browsers:verify` (`playwright install --dry-run`) as the download-free check and an optional `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` pin. | (a) Upgrade to `>=1.55.1`. (b) Vendor the Chromium archive into the repository. (c) Ignore it, since the download happens outside a test run. | The advisory is that the browser downloader does not verify the TLS chain of the host it fetches from; the fix version requires Node >= 18, and the AAP's CI matrix is Node 16.x, so (a) trades a supply-chain risk for a runtime the project does not declare. (b) puts a ~130 MB binary in git. The remedy taken removes the download from every automated path: the runner never installs, `browsers:verify` fetches nothing and reports the already-present `chromium-1117` install location, and the executable can be pinned explicitly. | `install:browsers` still exists and still downloads. It is deliberately an operator-invoked step, named so it cannot be mistaken for part of a run, and the residual is that an operator who runs it on an untrusted network is exposed. No automated path — `playwright test`, the `webServer` command, `browsers:verify` — reaches it. |
| D112 | Set `webServer.reuseExistingServer: false` unconditionally. | (a) Keep `!IS_CI`, the shape reviewed. (b) Add a build-identity or nonce endpoint to the harness and reuse only a server that answers it. | `reuseExistingServer` decides only whether *something* answers on 127.0.0.1:4173. It cannot distinguish this harness from a stale one started from a different checkout, an unrelated local server, or another user's process on a shared host — and each of those would silently become the subject under test, so a spec's assertions would describe content this configuration never produced. (b) is more code in the harness for a server that starts in under a second. | A developer who likes keeping a harness running loses that; the start cost is sub-second. With `strictPort: true`, a port already held now fails the run instead of quietly moving to another port — which is the intended signal, not a regression. |
| D113 | Invoke the harness through the **pinned local binary**, `node ./node_modules/vite/bin/vite.js`, in both `webServer.command` and the `harness` script, instead of `npx vite`. | (a) `npx --no-install vite`. (b) `npm exec --offline`. (c) `npm run harness` wrapping npx. | `npx` is online-capable: with the local executable missing it resolves the package from the registry and runs whatever it downloads, which substitutes an unpinned, unverified vite for the pinned one during what looks like an ordinary test run. Naming the file fails closed — an absent dependency is `MODULE_NOT_FOUND`. (a) and (b) depend on flag support that varies across npm versions; naming the file does not. | The relative path is hard-coded, so it is only correct with the working directory set to `e2e/`; `webServer.cwd` is pinned to that directory and the npm script inherits it. A future dependency layout change (hoisting `vite` elsewhere) breaks the command loudly rather than silently. |
| D114 | Deny browser egress at **two layers**: Chromium switches in `playwright.config.ts` that fail every non-loopback lookup and route the remainder at a closed port, plus an automatic context-wide abort fixture in the new support module `e2e/tests/harness-fixtures.ts`. | (a) Per-spec `page.route` only. (b) Config switches only. (c) A Playwright global-setup file. | Playwright documents that a page route sees neither a Service Worker's requests nor a popup's first request, and a spec that installs no route has no interception at all — three ways for a component's mount-time fetch to leave the browser. The switches sit below all three and are unconditional. The fixture adds what switches cannot: **attribution** — the aborted URL is named in the failing test's output, measured on both an in-page `fetch` and a `window.open` popup, which landed on `chrome-error://chromewebdata/` with the URL recorded. (c) cannot reach per-test contexts. `serviceWorkers: 'block'` completes it; Playwright implements that by replacing `navigator.serviceWorker.register` with a resolving stub, so `register()` resolving is expected and no worker exists — verified as zero registrations and a null controller. | `harness-fixtures.ts` is a support module the AAP's file list does not name; it is in scope under §0.8.1 (`e2e/**/*` in its entirety) and follows §0.4.5/§0.9.3, which require shared setup in one file rather than copy-pasted. The residual is that a spec written later gets attribution only if it imports `test` from this module — the boundary itself does not depend on that, because the switches apply to every spec. `testMatch` is `**/*.spec.ts`, so the module is never collected as a spec. |
| D115 | Screen a request path for an alternate Windows spelling **before** resolving it, and strip the `/@fs/` prefix and drive letter before that screening. | (a) Screen only the resolved, `realpath`-normalised path. (b) Screen the raw path without stripping the prefix. | Resolution destroys the evidence: `path.resolve` and `fs.realpathSync` both preserve an ADS suffix, and a short name resolves to its long name, so the deny check would already have passed on a name that no longer looks suspicious — the spelling has to be refused as a spelling. (b) is not a hypothetical: the drive-letter exemption is `^`-anchored, so tested against a path beginning `/@fs/C:` it never matches (`@` is not a letter) and **every** `/@fs/` URL on Windows is refused — React, ReactDOM, react-redux, react-router-dom, Redux Toolkit, both store slices and Vite's own dev client. That state was observed: `/main.tsx` fetched 200 but never evaluated, `#root` stayed empty, and because the dev client was refused too there was no error overlay and nothing in the dev-server log. | The stripping logic now exists in two places — this screen and `resolveServedPath` — and they must agree. Both are documented as such, and the guard's 13-case matrix covers the legitimate `/@fs/` module URLs alongside every refused spelling, so a divergence fails the check rather than blanking the page. |

## §11 Reconciled decisions where two remediations of the same finding disagreed

Scope: the points at which independently-written remediations of the same review finding could not both
stand, and the single behaviour the delivered tree has. Each row names what was kept, what was set aside,
and why. Nothing here re-opens a finding: every finding these rows touch is closed in the tree.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D116 | **[Superseded by D136 — both premises below were measured false; the wording is the decision as originally taken, not the shipped behaviour.]** Keep the **mutable allowed-origin list** in `frontend/src/test-utils/handlers.ts` - `allowRequestOrigin()`, `resetAllowedRequestOrigins()`, and frozen `DEFAULT_REQUEST_ORIGINS` - with the reset centralised in `setup-jest.ts`, and register every handler **host-agnostically** so a request from a non-admitted origin is screened to `CONTRACT_VIOLATION_STATUS` and ledgered rather than left unmatched. | (a) Freeze the list and delete the mutators, registering one absolute pattern per allowed origin, so a request from any other origin matches nothing and `onUnhandledRequest` fires.
