(b) Keep the freeze and delete the tests that admit an origin. | The finding asked for the mutable state to be **reset after every test**, not removed: removing it also removes the only way a suite can observe `services/api.ts` under a configured `REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL`, which `setup-jest.test.ts` covers in four ordered tests. Screening at request time is also the stronger guard: the unmatched-pattern route produces an msw error that `getLatestTweets`, `generateTweetResponse` and both list components all swallow, whereas a screened request is answered 599 **and** appended to the isolation ledger, which `assertNoIsolationViolations()` cannot swallow. Admitting an origin widens only what is mocked - the socket guard and `onUnhandledRequest` still stop egress. | A test can admit a remote origin, so a base URL pointing somewhere real is mocked rather than refused for the duration of that test. Bounded by the per-test reset and by the ledger entry that any non-admitted origin still produces. |
| D117 | Add `acknowledgeIsolationViolations()`, which drains the ledger and returns what it held, and call it from the one test that provokes a screening on purpose. | (a) Drop the `assertNoIsolationViolations()` call from the shared `afterEach`. (b) Delete the test that asserts a screened-out origin. (c) Let the ledger ignore `origin` breaches. | The ledger exists to catch a breach **nobody noticed**; a test that asserts the 599, the violation message and the `console.error` line has noticed it. An explicit acknowledgement keeps the default fail-closed for every other test, which (a) and (c) would not. | A test could acknowledge a breach it did not mean to provoke and hide it. Bounded by the acknowledgement being explicit, returning the entries so they can be asserted, and appearing in exactly one test. |
| D118 | Pin the twelve defaulted `Settings` fields by **unconditional assignment** of their declared values (`DEFAULTED_SETTINGS_ENV`), and remove only `NOTION_API_KEY` and the two undeclared consumer fields (`UNPINNABLE_SETTINGS_NAMES`, `UNDECLARED_CONSUMER_FIELDS`). | (a) Remove all thirteen names from the environment so the declared default governs. (b) Assign some and remove others without a stated rule. | pydantic v1 resolves a field from `os.environ` **before** the `.env` file named by `Settings.Config.env_file`, so assignment makes the singleton independent of a `backend/.env` as well as of an exported variable, while removal leaves a `.env` free to decide the value. `NOTION_API_KEY` is declared `Optional[str] = None`, a value no environment string expresses, so it is the one field that must be removed instead. | Twelve more names are written to the process environment. Bounded by `_record_environment` / `_restore_environment`, which return every managed name to its pre-prologue value at session end, distinguishing "was unset" from "was set to empty" with an `_ABSENT` sentinel - load-bearing because `PROJECT_ID` is pinned to `""`. |
| D119 | Keep the eight required credential fields on **unconditional assignment**, not `setdefault`. | Let a value exported by the caller win, on the grounds that no assertion reads a literal from those eight. | A suite that runs against an ambient real `SECRET_KEY`, Twitter, OpenAI or Google value is neither deterministic nor offline, and the risk is asymmetric: the cost of overriding an exported value is nil, the cost of honouring a real one is a live credential inside the test process. | A developer who exports one of the eight to point the suite somewhere is silently overridden. Bounded by this row and by the module docstring, which states the rule. |
| D120 | Verify the settings normalisation in **one** `pytest_configure`, which installs the child-process guard and then runs the readiness gate. | (a) Two `pytest_configure` hooks, one per concern. (b) The readiness gate as an autouse fixture. | Python binds one name per module, so two definitions of the hook silently discard the first - which would have dropped the child-process egress guard while appearing to work. One hook makes both effects observable, and both need the parsed configuration. | A failure in either half aborts the run before collection. That is the intended behaviour for both: an unverified configuration and an unguarded process are each reasons not to run. |
| D121 | Classify a host with `ipaddress` rather than by string prefix, and guard `sendmsg` alongside `sendto`. | Keep `host.startswith('127.')` plus a literal set, and guard `sendto` only. | A prefix test accepts a *name* beginning `127.` and rejects the abbreviated `127.1`, so it is wrong in both directions; `ipaddress` decides `127.0.0.5`, `127.1`, `::1` and `::ffff:127.0.0.1` correctly and treats anything unparseable as remote, which fails closed. `sendmsg` is a datagram path no connect guard sees. | `ipaddress.ip_address` runs on every guarded call. Immaterial next to the suite's runtime, and only reached for a non-loopback literal. |
| D122 | Resolve the harness origin in the single module `e2e/harness-origin.ts`, imported by both the Vite config and the Playwright config, and accept `HARNESS_PORT` **and** `E2E_PORT` as explicit overrides before falling back to `4173 + CLONE_INDEX`. | (a) A `harnessPort()` helper in each config, passing the value between them through `webServer.env`. (b) One env-var name only. | Two independent computations of the same port can disagree; one module imported by both cannot. Accepting both names honours the contract each config documented without leaving a second resolution path in the tree. | Two names for one setting is mild redundancy. Bounded by the resolution order being stated in the module header and by neither name being required. |
| D123 | Invoke the harness through the pinned local binary **and** pass the resolved port explicitly: `node ./node_modules/vite/bin/vite.js --config vite.harness.config.ts --port <port> --strictPort`. | (a) The pinned binary with no port argument. (b) `npm run harness -- --port <port> --strictPort`. | The direct `node` invocation cannot silently fetch an unpinned `vite` the way `npx` can, and it does not depend on `PATH`; the explicit port puts the value on the process command line where a collision is diagnosable, and `--strictPort` fails the start rather than moving to another port. | The command names a path inside `node_modules`, so a layout change in `vite` breaks it loudly. Preferred over a silent version substitution. |
| D124 | Where a comment-only remediation and a functional remediation touched the same lines, keep the text that describes the **delivered** behaviour. | Apply the comment-only wording throughout and accept that it describes the pre-remediation code. | Most of that pass applied cleanly and is in the tree - the support modules lost far more prose than they gained. Where it collided, its wording predates the reconciled origin design, the pinning prologue and the unified guard, and two of its hunks would have restored a `_is_local_address` that predates D121 and a `defaultConfig` export a finding had removed. A comment that misdescribes the code is itself a defect. | Some files carry longer comments than that pass intended. Their line counts are not a contract; accuracy was preferred. |
| D125 | Pin `python-jose[cryptography]==3.3.0`, matching AAP §0.6.1 and the installed tree. | Pin `3.5.0` on CVE grounds. | Recorded in full as D100; restated here because three separate remediations corrected this pin to 3.3.0 and one raised it, and D101's closure gate fails the suite whenever the manifest and the environment disagree. | Carried by D100. |

## §12 Code-review remediation

Scope: the seventeen findings of the cross-stack harness review, and the decisions taken while closing them.
Four rows supersede or refine an earlier decision and name it; the rest are new. Every outcome below was
measured in this repository — the backend suite under CPython 3.9.25, the frontend suite under Jest 29 with
msw 1.3.5, and the harness in headless Chromium 125.0.6422.26 — and the rows that describe a browser
observation were verified on all four harness routes.

One finding needed no row. The review found the backend egress guard admitting **all** loopback traffic, but
§2 **D25** already records the decision that it admit only a port this process bound — naming "allow all
loopback traffic, which is what the guard did before" as the alternative it rejected. The code had never
implemented it. The remediation therefore implements D25 rather than revising it, and the id **D133**,
drafted for that rule, is withdrawn as a duplicate and is not reused.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D126 | **Supersedes D43.** Make the harness dev server **fail closed** on the API surface: a request to any path in `HARNESS_API_SURFACE` that no spec intercepted is answered `503` with `{"error":"harness-api-not-intercepted","request":…,"remedy":"page.route(…)"}` and a `no-store` header, and the omission is warned on the dev-server's stderr. | (a) Keep D43's success defaults. (b) Answer nothing and let Vite's SPA fallback reply — the state D43 itself replaced. (c) Refuse to start unless every spec declares its routes up front. | D43's defaults made a route spec pass with **no** browser interception and no payload assertion: the mounted component's fetch received `[]` or the trends fixture, the success path rendered, and a spec that had forgotten `page.route` was indistinguishable from one that had it. That was measured — with all intercepts removed, `/` still rendered and its heading assertion still passed. Answering nothing (b) reintroduces the unhandled rejection and the HTML-parsed-as-JSON pass that D43 was written to remove, whereas a named 503 is diagnosable in one line of output. (c) cannot know a spec's routes before it runs. | A spec must now install `page.route` for every path the route it visits requests; `HARNESS_API_SURFACE` carries the exact snippet per entry. The table is keyed `<METHOD> <path>`, so a method it does not list falls through to the SPA fallback — deliberate, since only the three listed methods are reachable from a mounted component. |
| D127 | **Refines D44.** Validate the parsed trends payload with `describeTrendSeriesViolation` and throw a distinct named `TrendSeriesContractError` **before** reaching D44's designed `UnrenderableTrendSeriesError`. | (a) Keep the single rejection, the reviewed shape. (b) Validate but reuse `UnrenderableTrendSeriesError`. (c) Validate with zod. | D44 makes the stub always reject, so with one error class a structurally invalid fixture — absent `labels`, a string inside `values`, a non-object body — produced exactly the same rejection as the intentional one: fixture drift was indistinguishable from designed behaviour, and `describeTrendSeriesViolation` was dead code that could never report it. Two classes separate "the fixture is wrong" from "the component cannot render a valid series." (b) leaves one message for two causes. (c) adds a fourth dependency to a harness whose manifest the AAP freezes at three. | The validator is hand-written, so a shape it does not describe still reaches the D44 path. Bounded by the message naming the exact offending member — `"values[0]" is "x"`, `"labels" is undefined` — verified against six drift shapes and the committed fixture. |
| D128 | **Refines D114.** Set `--proxy-bypass-list=<-loopback>;<harness host>:<port>` and restrict `--host-resolver-rules` to `MAP * ~NOTFOUND, EXCLUDE <harness host>`, admitting one origin instead of all of loopback. | (a) Keep `127.0.0.1;localhost`. (b) Rely on the host-resolver rules alone. (c) Run Chromium in a network namespace. | Chromium bypasses proxies for **every** loopback address by default, so `127.0.0.1;localhost` widened nothing and admitted nothing it needed to — while leaving every other loopback port reachable, including a sibling service on a shared host and another clone's harness. `<-loopback>` removes that implicit bypass, and only after it does naming the exact `host:port` restrict anything at all. (b) leaves the proxy path open for a literal IP. (c) is not available on this host. | A future harness that legitimately fetches a second loopback port must add it explicitly. It fails as a closed-port connection error naming the URL, and `harness-fixtures.ts` ledgers it as well (D131). |
| D129 | **[The `browsers:verify` half is superseded by D195, which also removes the downloader invocation this row's premise had missed in `ci.yml` and the root `README.md`; deleting `install:browsers` stands.]** **Refines D111.** Delete the `install:browsers` script from `e2e/package.json`. Browsers are provisioned out of band from a verified artifact; `browsers:verify` (`playwright install --dry-run`) stays as the download-free check, with `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` available as an explicit pin. | (a) Keep it as an operator-only step, which was D111's position. (b) Upgrade to `>=1.55.1`. (c) Vendor the Chromium archive. | D111 recorded its own residual precisely: "`install:browsers` still exists and still downloads." A script in a manifest is one `npm run` away, and it is what gets copied into an onboarding document or a CI job without the caveat that justified it — so its presence *is* the exposure to CVE-2025-59288's unverified TLS chain. Nothing in the tree needs it: Chromium 125.0.6422.26 (build 1117) is already provisioned and `browsers:verify` reports its location while fetching nothing. (b) requires Node >= 18, which the AAP's CI matrix does not declare. (c) puts ~130 MB in git. | A fresh machine has no in-repo command that obtains a browser and must provision from a verified artifact first. `e2e/package.json`'s description and `e2e/README.md` state that requirement, and `browsers:verify` fails naming the path it expected. |
| D130 | **Refines D122.** Have every e2e consumer read the harness host and port from `e2e/harness-origin.ts` and declare neither locally: the Vite config imports `HARNESS_HOST`/`HARNESS_PORT`, and `e2e/tests/harness-fixtures.ts` imports and re-exports `HARNESS_ORIGIN` instead of a literal. | (a) Keep the literal `http://127.0.0.1:4173` in the fixture and the local port helper in the Vite config. (b) Pass the origin through `webServer.env` and read it at run time. | D122 established the single module, but two consumers still computed or hard-coded the value, and the result was a live divergence rather than untidiness: with `HARNESS_PORT=4190` exported, the local redeclaration shadowed the import so Vite bound **4173** while Playwright polled **4190**; and the fixture's literal made the automatic egress guard abort the *real* harness as external on any non-default port. One module imported everywhere is the only shape in which a port override is honoured by all three files. (b) makes the value unknowable to a config that is evaluated before the server exists. | Every e2e file now depends on that module resolving. It is dependency-free, and every run exercises it. |
| D131 | Ledger the harness's un-intercepted API requests as well as its blocked foreign-origin ones: `harness-fixtures.ts` aborts a foreign origin `blockedbyclient` into `abortedUrls`, records a same-origin request whose pathname is in `HARNESS_API_PATHS` into `unInterceptedApiRequests` and lets it reach the D126 responder, falls everything else through, and raises one combined error at teardown. | (a) Rely on D126's 503 status alone. (b) Abort un-intercepted API requests instead of answering them. (c) Assert per spec. | A status cannot fail a test here — measured: with the intercepts removed, the route rendered, the heading assertion **passed**, and only the teardown ledger failed the run. This is the same reasoning as D103 and D117: an error inside the request lifecycle is swallowed by the code under test, so the record has to outlive the request and be read from outside the page. (b) would also destroy the evidence of *which* path the component wanted. (c) is the per-spec discipline whose omission the finding was about. | A spec that means to observe the un-intercepted path must read `unInterceptedApiRequestUrls()` rather than let teardown raise. It is exported for exactly that. |
| D132 | Wrap the harness route outlet in exactly one semantic `<main>` in `e2e/harness/main.tsx`, carrying no class, style or other attribute, and leave `index.html`'s root element generic. | (a) Put the landmark in `index.html` around `#root`. (b) One landmark per route component. (c) A full shell with header and nav. | The harness is the only place the landmark can go: the four route components are production files, and only two production touches are authorized, neither of them here. One landmark per document is what a screen reader and an automated audit expect, so (b) would produce multiple `main` elements on any route that composes components. (c) invents a visual shell no design reference covers and would make the harness diverge from what the Jest component suites render. Attribute-free keeps the rendered output identical apart from the landmark — verified `className` `""`, `style` `null`, zero attributes and `document.styleSheets.length` 0 on every route. | None material. Verified in Chromium as `main` count 1 and `#root > main` count 1 on all four routes, and again after a client-side pushState traversal, with content asserted inside the landmark. |
| D134 | Delete the obsolete parallel symbol table from `backend/tests/conftest.py` — `_MissingSymbol`, the four `*_SYMBOL` records, `APPLICATION_SYMBOLS`, `_reject_every_token`, `_unavailable_add_response`, `_rebind_importer_alias` and `_shimmed` — and correct the comment that still named `_shimmed`. | (a) Repair `_shimmed`'s three-argument call against the current two-argument `_install_missing_symbol`. (b) Leave it in place, since nothing calls it. | D106's fail-closed sentinels replaced this mechanism; what survived was a second, divergent description of the same four missing symbols whose only entry point would have raised `TypeError` the moment anything used it. Two descriptions of one mechanism is how the wrong one comes to be extended — and a comment already pointed a reader at the dead one. (a) revives a code path with no caller. | None. The four symbols are supplied solely through the D106 fixtures, the suite is green, and a repository-wide grep finds no residual reference to any deleted name. |
| D135 | **Refines D80.** Restore the base log-record factory in `pytest_unconfigure`, alongside the environment and egress-guard restorations already performed there. | (a) Leave the correlation factory installed. (b) Install it per test instead of globally. | D80 installs the factory process-wide through `logging.setLogRecordFactory` and nothing removed it, so the interpreter pytest left behind differed from the one it entered — the same leak D104 closes for the environment and D105 for the egress guard, and the reason both restore explicitly. (b) cannot stamp a record emitted during collection, which is where the imports that matter happen, so the factory has to stay global while the session runs. | None. Verified in-process as `RESTORED: True`, with D80's `correlation_id` output and the JUnit correlation unchanged. |
| D136 | **[The pattern half is superseded by D169 — patterns now name the base path prefix in full instead of absorbing it with a wildcard, and ase is read from the registration; the frozen allow-list and the no-mutator rule stand.]** **Supersedes D116.** Adopt D103 as delivered: a frozen `ALLOWED_REQUEST_ORIGINS`, one **absolute** pattern per allowed origin (eight isolation handlers), `base` rebuilt from the origin plus the wildcard capture, and no mutator of any kind. | (a) Keep D116 — the mutable list plus host-agnostic patterns. (b) Keep the frozen list but retain a mutator for the base-URL suite alone. | D116 rested on two premises, and both were measured false. It held that deleting the mutator removes the only way to observe `services/api.ts` under a configured `REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL`; `server.use(rest.get(<exact absolute URL>, …))` does that in one line, and `setup-jest.test.ts` now covers it that way. It held that an unmatched request yields only a swallowable msw error; `setup-jest.ts`'s `onUnhandledRequest` callback appends the request to the ledger **before** `print.error()` raises, so an unmatched request is ledgered exactly as a screened one is — verified as a `kind: 'unhandled-request'` entry with the request never performed. With both premises false, D116's remaining substance is process-global mutable state, which is the hazard this suite exists to remove. D103 is also what `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §B already documents, so the tree now agrees with both artifacts instead of contradicting each. | Each layer registers two handlers per route, so an array-length assertion sees eight rather than four; asserted directly and documented in both layers. A suite driving a non-loopback absolute URL must install its own exact-URL handler, and the violation message names the origin, the allow-list and that remedy. |
| D137 | **[Superseded by D170 — the 422 is returned under both dependency dispositions, because coercion precedes the endpoint, and every failing parameter is reported in declaration order.]** **Refines D8.** Split layer 2's `GET /tweets` by **dependency disposition**: `currentBehaviorTweetsHandlers()` answers the unoverridden 500, the new `dependencyOverriddenTweetsHandlers()` carries D8's live coercion branch (200, and 422 for a non-coercing `limit`), every `RouteContract` records both outcomes, and `currentBackendBehaviorHandlers()` excludes the override set. | (a) Keep D8's single 200/422 handler as the whole of current behaviour, the reviewed shape. (b) Keep only the 500 and drop the coercion contract. (c) One handler switched by a flag. | D8's coercion branch is right, but it was measured the way §1's scope note states — through `TestClient` with `app.db.firestore.get_db` **overridden** — and layer 2 presented it as what the assembled application returns. It is not: `app/api/routes/tweets.py::get_tweets` calls `db.query(Tweet)` on whatever `Depends(get_db)` yields, and `app/db/firestore.get_db` yields a Firestore `Client`, which has no `query`, so the unoverridden route answers 500 and D8's two statuses are reachable only under a SQLAlchemy-shaped override. Labelling them "current backend behaviour" overstated the surface a suite had covered. (b) discards the pagination and coercion contract the backend integration layer genuinely does assert under an override. (c) is the global switch D1 already rejected, and for the same reason — it hides which contract a test ran against. | A suite must pick the factory matching the disposition it means to assert. The names say which, the module header table gives both columns for all four routes, and `dependencyOverriddenOutcome` is `null` on the one route where no override changes the outcome. |

## §13 Identity of the artifacts a run produces

Scope: the names a run writes into its coverage and report artifacts, and the generated files a documented
workflow leaves in the working tree. Each row closes a runtime finding raised against the artifacts rather
than against the tests: the coverage reporters named four modules by a path that is not in the repository,
and two documented workflows left generated files git offered to commit.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D138 | Restore the real extension-less path in the source map `frontend/jest.transform.extensionless.js` emits — rewriting `file` and every `sources` entry that names the synthetic `<path>.tsx`, in the inline data-URL payload and in a separate `map` field alike — while still handing ts-jest the synthetic name. | (a) Leave it, on the grounds that the percentages are right. (b) Post-process the reporters instead, mapping the four synthetic paths back after the fact. (c) Stop passing a synthetic name and give ts-jest the real one. (d) Rename the four production files to `.tsx`. | The synthetic name does not stay inside ts-jest as D30 assumed: ts-jest writes it into the map it emits, Jest adopts that map as `inputSourceMap` — this transformer declares no `canInstrument`, so Jest instruments the output itself — and `istanbul-lib-source-maps` then attributes each module to `inputSourceMap.sources[0]`. Measured: 4 of 19 entries in `coverage-final.json`, `lcov.info`, `cobertura-coverage.xml` and `coverage-summary.json` named `src\components\*.tsx`, none of which exists, and the first of those is the file the `frontend`-flagged Codecov step uploads, which matches report paths against the commit's file list. (a) publishes a coverage claim about files the repository does not contain. (b) leaves the wrong identity in the instrumented module and in stack traces, and puts the correction in a second place that can drift from the first. (c) removes the loader inference this transformer exists for — without a `.tsx` name ts-jest cannot compile JSX. (d) is a production change outside the two authorized touches. The emitted map is the narrowest point at which the name can be corrected before it becomes an identity. | The rewrite parses the emitted map, so a payload it cannot decode is passed through unchanged rather than corrupted, and the compiled code, `mappings`, `names` and `sourcesContent` are never touched — verified by identical statement, branch and function counts for all 19 files before and after. It does depend on ts-jest emitting a JSON source map: were a future version to emit none, the identity would fall back to the filename Jest already holds, which is the real one. D140 is the gate that fails if either half stops holding. |
| D139 | Bump the transform cache namespace to `:extensionless:real-source-identity`. | (a) Keep `:extensionless`. (b) Delete `getCacheKey` and let Jest key the entry itself. | Jest uses a transformer's `getCacheKey` verbatim when one is provided, and the inner key covers the source text and ts-jest's configuration — not this wrapper's output contract. With the suffix unchanged, a developer or CI runner holding a cache from before D138 is served the pre-fix artifact, with its synthetic identity, and the fix appears not to work. (b) loses the namespace separation from the primary transformer that D30 relies on. | The suffix has to be bumped again by whoever next changes what `process` returns. Stated in the constant's own comment, and D140 fails on a stale artifact rather than accepting it. |
| D140 | Commit `frontend/src/test-utils/jest-transform-extensionless.test.ts`, a 17-case contract suite over the transformer: the emitted map names the real file, the synthetic name appears nowhere, the output is still CommonJS using the automatic JSX runtime, and the mappings and source text are carried through. | (a) No committed gate — verify once by hand. (b) Assert on `coverage-final.json` from inside a later component suite, as the finding suggested. (c) Assert it from a CI shell step. | The identity is invisible in a passing run: every suite stays green while the artifacts name files that do not exist, which is how this reached a QA boundary in the first place. (a) leaves nothing to fail when D138 or D139 is undone. (b) needs a coverage run to have happened, cannot run under plain `jest`, and reads a global artifact from a test about one component; this suite proves the same property at the seam that produces it, in about three seconds, with no coverage run. (c) puts the assertion outside the runner that owns the behaviour. Negative validation: with the rewrite removed, 8 of the 17 cases fail naming the `.tsx` path; restored, 17 pass. | The suite reaches into a build-tool module from a jsdom test file, so it is coupled to ts-jest emitting an inline source map — the same dependency D138 carries, asserted rather than assumed. It is a file the AAP's list does not name; it is in scope under §0.8.1, which covers `frontend/src/test-utils/**/*` in its entirety, it sits outside `collectCoverageFrom`, and it imports no component, so it moves no coverage number. |
| D141 | Ignore the generated `package-lock.json` with one unanchored rule in `.gitignore`, keeping D93's decision to commit no lockfile and to install with `npm install`. | (a) Add `.npmrc` files carrying `package-lock=false` to `frontend/` and `e2e/`. (b) Commit both lockfiles and return CI to `npm ci`. (c) Enumerate `frontend/package-lock.json` and `e2e/package-lock.json` separately. | Measured: `npm config get package-lock` is `true`, no `.npmrc` exists at any level, and the documented install therefore leaves a 319 KB and an 18.5 KB untracked, unignored file behind — every developer and every CI run dirties the tree. (a) adds two new files and changes install behaviour to reach the same end. (b) contradicts D93 and puts generated content in git. (c) is the same rule written twice and still misses a root-level install. | Ignoring rather than suppressing means the file still occupies disk and can go stale against `package.json`; nothing reads it, since `npm install` re-resolves. The reproducibility risk D93 recorded is untouched by this row and remains a suggested next task — this closes the tree-cleanliness half only. |
| D142 | Ignore `reports/` unanchored, in addition to the enumerated `backend/reports/`, rather than moving where pytest writes its JUnit XML. | (a) Anchor the artifact to rootdir from `backend/tests/conftest.py` at `pytest_configure`. (b) Change `--junitxml` in `backend/pytest.ini`. (c) Ignore only `/reports/` at the repository root. | pytest resolves `--junitxml` with `os.path.abspath`, against the invocation directory rather than rootdir, and no ini key can anchor it — so `pytest backend\tests` from the repository root writes `<root>/reports/junit.xml`, which `backend/reports/` never covered. (a) puts a behavioural hook in the shared conftest for a path problem and would move an artifact location the AAP §0.9.4 table names. (b) cannot express an anchored path. (c) closes the one invocation observed and none of the others. The unanchored rule covers every depth at once, and the trailing `/` keeps it to directories — a file named `reports.py` or `reports.ts` stays visible, verified. | An unanchored directory rule would also hide a future *committed* `reports/` directory. None exists: a `--no-index` `git check-ignore` sweep over all 92 tracked files matches nothing, and the enumerated `backend/reports/`, `frontend/reports/` and `e2e/reports/` rules are kept so the sanctioned artifact locations stay readable in the file. |

---

## §14 Observability of the harness's own HTTP

Scope: what the two harness `fetch` stubs do with a response body they are not going to use. The row below
closes a runtime finding raised against the harness rather than against a test: the un-intercepted API
request that the D126 responder answers `503` was invisible to the browser's own performance timeline, and
unreadable through the DevTools protocol, because nobody consumed it.

Measured on Windows with Chromium 125.0.6422.26 and the harness under vite 4.5.14, before and after the
change, on `/analytics` and `/configuration`.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D143 | Read the response body to completion and discard it on the `!response.ok` branch of both harness `fetch` stubs — `getTrendData` and `updateTwitterAPIConfig` — with `await response.text().catch(() => undefined)` immediately before the status `throw`. The thrown error, its message and the always-reject contract of D44 and D127 are unchanged, and the success path still reads the body exactly once. | (a) Leave it: the status check already precedes the parse, which is literally what both file briefs asked for. (b) `response.body?.cancel()` rather than reading. (c) Drain unconditionally, ahead of the status check. (d) Change no code and require every census to be driven from CDP `Network` events instead. | An abandoned body stream is not a tidiness question here, it is a visibility question, and both halves were measured. `GET /api/trends` was issued, was answered `503`, and Chrome saw it — yet it was **absent from `performance.getEntriesByType('resource')`**: 29 requests captured, one of them the navigation, so 28 resource entries were due and 27 existed, the set difference being exactly that request, and `initiatorType: 'fetch'` had no entries at all. It was still absent 286 s later, so not a race; 27 of a 250-entry buffer, so not truncation; same-origin, so not `Timing-Allow-Origin`. The control settles the cause: `/favicon.ico` answers `204` with **zero body bytes** and still earns a full entry, so neither a non-2xx status nor an empty body is the differentiator — the unconsumed stream is, because Chrome withholds `loadingFinished` until the body is drained. The second half is worse than a missing measurement: `Network.getResponseBody` on that request blocks indefinitely, which cost a QA run a 15-minute timeout and a forced browser restart. Both halves are one cause, and AAP §0.4.6 and §0.7.4 require this harness to be deterministic and safely drivable by automation, which a request that hangs the debugger and hides from the timeline is not. (a) preserves the brief's wording at the cost of its purpose: the purpose of checking status first is that the informative status error wins over a parse error and no body-derived fallback is produced, and reading-then-discarding keeps all of that — nothing is parsed, nothing is returned, an empty body still yields the status error, and the message is byte-identical. (b) does not work: cancelling aborts the fetch, which Chrome reports as a failure rather than a completion, so it would deliver neither the timing entry nor the retrievable body. (c) would consume the body the success path needs, breaking `response.json()` and the empty-body tolerance the `configService` brief requires. (d) leaves the debugger-wedging half entirely open and makes correct instrumentation a rule every future author must know rather than a property the code has. | The `catch` swallows a body-read failure. That is deliberate and bounded: the status error is thrown unconditionally either way, so the failure that matters is never masked — only a transport fault while draining a body already destined for the bin is. The drain adds one `await` to a path that always ends in a rejection; the response it reads is the D126 responder's 197-byte JSON, so the cost is not measurable. A future stub that acquires a non-ok path must repeat the drain; there are exactly two `fetch` call sites in `e2e/`, both carry it, and both say so in their `@throws` documentation. Verified after the change on both routes: the entry is present with real timings, the body is retrievable in milliseconds rather than hanging, and the rendered output, the console text and the fail-closed `503` are unchanged. Separately worth knowing and not fixable in code — a harness network census should be driven from `page.on('request')`/`page.on('response')` or CDP `Network` events rather than from `performance.getEntriesByType('resource')`, which reports only requests whose bodies were consumed. |

---

## §15 Code-review remediation — the ignore boundary, the comment/rationale split and the `TweetList` assertion oracles

Scope: the two findings raised against this checkpoint. The first is a scope finding against `.gitignore`,
whose authorized content is generated test artifacts and nothing else. The second is a Rule 1 finding against
thirty test and configuration files whose comments argued for the approach they took; the rows below hold that
reasoning instead, and each of those files now cites the row rather than restating it.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D144 | **Supersedes D141.** Carry no lockfile rule in `.gitignore` at all, so a `package-lock.json` appearing in `frontend/`, `e2e/` or the repository root is visible to `git status` and to review. D93's decision to commit no lockfile is unchanged; the file is suppressed where it would be created, with `npm install --package-lock=false`, rather than hidden after the fact. | (a) Keep D141's unanchored ignore. (b) Enumerate `frontend/package-lock.json` and `e2e/package-lock.json` instead. (c) Commit both lockfiles and return the CI install step to `npm ci`. (d) Add `.npmrc` files carrying `package-lock=false` to `frontend/` and `e2e/`. | A lockfile is dependency-resolution state, not a test artifact, and `.gitignore`'s authorized content is test artifacts only (AAP §0.5.4, §0.8.1, §0.8.2). The ignore also carried a consequence D141 did not weigh: `npm install` **reads** a lockfile when one is present and resolves from it, so an ignored lockfile can change what a documented install produces while never appearing in a diff. This tree had already drifted — `react-router-dom` resolved to 6.30.4 against an exact `6.22.3` pin (D109) — which is precisely the class of change that has to stay reviewable. (a) and (b) both keep that state invisible; (b) merely says it twice and still misses a root-level install. (c) contradicts D93 and AAP §0.6.2, which fix the authorized frontend configuration surface at `package.json`. (d) adds two files outside that surface and changes install behaviour for everything run in those directories, including tooling that is not ours; passing the flag at the one point a lockfile would be created keeps the suppression explicit where it applies. | A plain `npm install` without the flag now leaves an **untracked** lockfile rather than an ignored one. That is the intended trade — visible rather than silent — but it means whoever writes the onboarding and CI install steps (both future indexes) must carry `--package-lock=false` for the tree to stay clean, and a developer who omits it sees a dirty tree. Verified after the change: no lockfile exists anywhere on disk, `git check-ignore` reports `frontend/package-lock.json`, `e2e/package-lock.json` and a root `package-lock.json` as visible, and the `react-router-dom@6.22.3` alignment was installed with the flag and produced none. The reproducibility risk D93 recorded is untouched and remains a suggested next task. |
| D145 | **Supersedes D142.** Ignore report output by enumerated runner path only — `backend/reports/`, `frontend/reports/`, `e2e/reports/` — and drop the unanchored `reports/` rule. | (a) Keep D142's unanchored rule. (b) Anchor a single rule at the repository root, `/reports/`, for the off-spec invocation D142 measured. (c) Move where pytest writes its JUnit XML. | The unanchored rule matched `reports/` at every depth, so it hid any *source or documentation* directory of that name: measured before this change, `docs/reports/x.md` and `src/reports/index.ts` were both ignored, and both are visible after it. The three enumerated paths are exactly the ones AAP §0.5.4 and the §0.9.4 artifact table name, and every documented command writes into one of them because AAP §0.9.2 invokes pytest from `backend/`. (a) keeps a source-shadowing rule alive for an artifact no sanctioned command produces. (b) still shadows a future root-level `reports/` directory, and buys tree-cleanliness only for an invocation the AAP does not sanction. (c) would move an artifact location the AAP §0.9.4 table fixes. | `pytest backend\tests` run **from the repository root** resolves `--junitxml` against the invocation directory and writes `<root>/reports/junit.xml`, which is now untracked and visible instead of ignored. That is the off-spec invocation only — the sanctioned one is `cd backend && pytest`, which writes `backend/reports/`. Verified after the change: all three enumerated report directories plus `frontend/coverage/`, `e2e/test-results/`, `e2e/playwright-report/`, `node_modules/`, `__pycache__/` and the coverage files are still ignored, and a `git check-ignore --no-index` sweep over all 119 tracked files matches nothing. |
| D146 | Pin `process.env.TZ = 'UTC'` in `frontend/jest.config.js`, at config-evaluation time in the main process. | (a) Set it in `frontend/src/test-utils/setup-jest.ts`. (b) Set it inside `frontend/src/utils/dateUtils.test.ts`, the only suite it governs. (c) Assert nothing that depends on a zone: drop the `formatDate` local-part assertions. (d) Put `TZ=UTC` in front of the npm scripts in `frontend/package.json`. | `dayjs(...).format(...)` renders *local* date parts, so `formatDate('2024-01-15T00:00:00Z', 'YYYY-MM-DD')` is `'2024-01-15'` only in a zone at or ahead of UTC — on a machine behind it the same call returns the previous day, and the suite would pass in CI and fail in a US timezone. (a) and (b) both run inside a worker, where `process.env` is a sandboxed copy with no libuv-backed setter: assigning `TZ` there leaves V8's cached zone untouched, so the pin silently does nothing. (c) abandons a real contract of the wrapper. (d) works but splits one guarantee across four script strings and leaves `npx jest` unpinned. | The pin is invisible from the suite that depends on it, which is why that suite names it and this row exists; `dateUtils.test.ts` additionally scopes and restores the variable in its own hooks so the value is stated where it is read. A future suite that asserts a *non*-UTC rendering has to set the zone per process rather than per test, which this arrangement does not make easy. |
| D147 | Answer `/favicon.ico` with `204 No Content` from the harness dev server, matched as an exact path. | (a) Leave it to Vite, which 404s. (b) Ship an icon file under `e2e/harness/` and let Vite serve it. (c) Declare `<link rel="icon" href="data:,">` in `e2e/harness/index.html`. (d) Match a prefix or a pattern rather than the exact path. | A browser requests this path unprompted on every top-level navigation, and no document in the harness references it. Left at 404 it is logged at error level on every load, which puts a failure that is not one into the console census a spec may assert on and into the Playwright network record a reader has to interpret. A `204` is a success carrying no body, so nothing is logged and no asset is invented. (b) adds a binary to a directory whose whole purpose is to contain no product surface. (c) suppresses the request in the harness's own document but not for a navigation a spec drives directly, and edits the entry the harness owns for a browser-side concern. (d) would swallow other unknown dotted paths, and an unknown path 404ing is information worth keeping — the fail-closed API responder (D126) depends on exactly that. | One more special case in the request-handling middleware, and it precedes the API surface check, so a future route literally named `/favicon.ico` could not be intercepted by a spec. Verified on the running harness: the path answers `204` with zero body bytes, `/` still serves the document, an unknown dotted path still 404s, and the API surface still answers `503 harness-api-not-intercepted`. |
| D148 | State behaviour in comments and reasoning here: a comment in a test or test-configuration file documents what the subject does, what the file asserts, the exact oracle, the input shape, the invariants the file guarantees and the reason a skip cannot run — and cites a row below for why the approach was chosen. Every "chosen because", "rather than" and "the alternative would" moves into this log. Thirty files were rewritten to that boundary in this checkpoint, and the vague pointers they carried ("the single source of truth for every why") were replaced with exact row numbers. | (a) Leave the reasoning in the headers, where the reader of the test already is. (b) Delete it outright, on the grounds that the log already holds most of it. (c) Keep it in both places and accept the duplication. (d) Move whole headers into `frontend/TESTING.md` and `backend/tests/README.md` instead. | Rule 1 makes this log the single source of truth for "why" and forbids rationale in code comments, so (a) and (c) are violations however useful they read — and (c) is the worse of the two, because two copies drift and the reader cannot tell which is current. (b) would lose reasoning that no row held: the timezone pin, the harness favicon route, the `Settings` stand-ins, the layered stream safety, the frontend observation boundary, the store-suite action strategy and the component arrangements were all argued only in comments, so deleting them would have destroyed the record Rule 1 exists to keep — they are rows D146-D154 instead. (d) puts the reasoning in onboarding documents whose job is teaching the workflow, not adjudicating a choice, and those two files are future indexes. | A header that states behaviour without arguing for it reads as flatter, and a reader who wants the argument has to follow a citation. The boundary is also a judgement rather than a rule a linter can enforce: "the body is read to completion on both paths" is a contract, while "so the response is never left with an open stream" is the reason it matters, and reviewers will draw that line in slightly different places. Where a mechanism *is* the asserted behaviour — pydantic v1 moving fields off the class namespace, `Tweet.id` resolving to nothing, `combineReducers` filtering non-function values — it stayed, because deleting it would remove the oracle's derivation and leave a bare literal nobody can check. |
| D149 | Replace the attribute on the **importing** module, never the library or the module that defines the symbol: `app.services.analytics_service.run_query` rather than `app.db.bigquery.run_query`; `app.db.firestore.get_db` rather than `google.cloud.firestore.Client`; `app.services.llm_service.Completion.create` rather than `openai.Completion.create`; `app.tasks.tweet_processor.add_tweet` rather than `app.db.firestore.add_tweet`. Two tests assert the binding itself, so the boundary is proven rather than assumed. | (a) Patch at the library, which is what the deleted `backend/tests/test_services.py` did. (b) Patch at the defining module. (c) Inject collaborators as parameters, which needs production signatures to change. (d) Install an import hook that swaps the third-party package wholesale. | Every one of these modules binds its collaborator by value with `from … import …` at import time, so a later patch of the source has no effect on the copy the subject already holds — the legacy suite's `services.llm_service.openai.Completion.create` target could not have worked even with the import root fixed. (b) fails for the same reason. (c) is a production change outside the two authorized touches. (d) replaces far more than the seam under test and makes every assertion about the stand-in rather than about the subject. Patching the importing module's attribute is also the narrowest edit: it is undone with the test, and a sibling suite that binds the same name is unaffected. | The target string names a module the reader has to know binds that symbol, so a production `import` moved from module scope into a function body would silently make the patch a no-op — a test would then exercise the real collaborator. `test_run_query_is_bound_into_the_subject_module` and `test_mock_run_query_replaces_the_subject_module_attribute` are the guard for the analytics seam; the egress guard (D105) is the backstop everywhere else, which fails a test loudly rather than letting it reach a network. |
| D150 | Supply fields production reads but `Settings` never declares from the **test** side, per test: the `bigquery_settings` fixture substitutes a project-carrying stand-in for the `Settings` name bound into `app.db.bigquery`, and `monkeypatch.setitem` writes `TWITTER_CONSUMER_KEY` and `TWITTER_CONSUMER_SECRET` into a pydantic-v1 instance's `__dict__`. Both are released on teardown, and the unsupplied `AttributeError` is asserted as current behaviour first. | (a) Declare the missing fields in `app/core/config.py`, extending authorized touch #2 from four fields to seven. (b) Set them as environment variables in `conftest.py`'s prologue. (c) Leave the modules untested and record them as unreachable. (d) `setattr` the fields onto the real `Settings` instance. | Touch #2 is bounded to the four fields the requirements name (D52), and three of these would not be fixable by declaring anything: `app/db/bigquery.py` reads `Settings.GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT` off the **class**, and pydantic v1 moves declared fields out of the class namespace, so (a) cannot reach `run_query` or `insert_tweet_analytics` at all — the substitution is the only thing that unlocks them. (b) has the same defect for the class read, and would additionally change what every other suite's `Settings` contains. (c) abandons two data-access modules and one stream starter to zero coverage when a test-side substitution reaches them. (d) raises: pydantic v1 rejects assignment of an undeclared field, which is exactly why `monkeypatch.setitem` on `__dict__` is used. | The stand-ins are shaped by hand, so a field production starts reading is not automatically present — the failure is an `AttributeError` inside the test, which is loud, but it is a maintenance cost. Substituting `Settings` also means the second half of `test_db_bigquery.py` asserts behaviour that cannot occur in production as shipped; the first half asserts the shipped `AttributeError` so both facts are on the record, and the divergence is registered rather than hidden. |
| D151 | Keep three independent barriers between `start_tweet_stream` / `start_twitter_stream` and a live Twitter connection: `sys.modules['tweepy']` replaced for the whole of every call in `test_tasks_tweet_processor.py`; the `OAuthHandler`, `API` and `add_tweet` names replaced on the subject in `test_services_twitter.py`; and a `settings` stand-in that carries no `TWITTER_TRACK_KEYWORDS`, so evaluating `stream.filter`'s argument raises before the call is made. | (a) One barrier — replace `tweepy` and rely on it. (b) Rely on the `NameError` at `twitter_service.py` line 47, which already makes that function dead code. (c) Rely on the autouse egress guard (D105) alone. (d) Skip both stream starters entirely. | An unpatched `start_tweet_stream()` was measured **blocking to a 300-second timeout** on a live connection, so the cost of one barrier failing is not a failed test but a wedged run — and `tweet_processor.py` imports `tweepy` **inside the function body** (line 53), which is a different seam from `twitter_service.py`'s module-scope `from tweepy import …`, so no single technique covers both. (b) is true today and would silently stop being the guarantee the moment anyone adds the missing `import tweepy` — that is a one-line production change away. (c) is a real backstop but it protects the socket, not the interpreter: `stream.filter` would still be entered. (d) forfeits the coverage the AAP's ≥90% gate needs on `app/tasks`. | Three barriers cost a reader three places to look before believing the suite is safe, which is why they are enumerated in the two module docstrings. They also mean a test can pass for the wrong reason — for instance while the `NameError` fires and a stand-in is never consulted — so `test_services_twitter.py` asserts the terminal state explicitly rather than merely asserting that nothing hung. Verified: all 32 tests in that module pass in fully reversed declaration order and the two natural-state cases pass in isolation, so no barrier depends on execution order. |
| D152 | Run the frontend subject and everything under it for real, and replace only the transport: msw intercepts by default, a `jest.spyOn(axios, …)` with a mocked settled value stands in where a test needs a specific outcome, and `jest.mock` is used only on a *collaborator* module the subject imports (`@/services/twitterService`, `@/services/configService`, `@/services/analyticsService`) — never on the module under test and never on `services/api.ts`. | (a) `jest.mock('../services/api')` in the service and component suites, which is the obvious way to control a return value. (b) Hand-rolled `axios` adapter or `axios-mock-adapter` instead of msw. (c) msw only, with no spies at all. (d) Spies only, with no msw. | Mocking `api.ts` would delete the very thing several oracles are about: the literal `undefined/` base URL, the query-string assembly, the `response.data.generatedResponse` unwrap and the propagate-versus-replace difference between `twitterService` and `llmService` are all *its* behaviour, and a mock would assert the mock. (b) replaces the adapter, so the request never reaches the boundary where a URL is resolved and screened, and it duplicates what `handlers.ts` already does with route contracts (D3). (c) cannot express "this call rejects with exactly this instance" without inventing a status the route contract would then screen, and cannot observe the pre-resolution URL string at all — jsdom rewrites `undefined/tweets` to `/undefined/tweets` before msw sees it, and both strings are asserted. (d) loses the fail-closed `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` guarantee that an unmocked request cannot escape. The two together give one observation point per question: the spy sees what the subject constructed, msw sees what left the boundary. | Two observation points mean a reader has to know which one a given assertion is about, which is why both files that use both say so. A spy created without a mocked return value **calls through and opens a real socket**, so the pattern depends on every `spyOn` being given its outcome in the same statement — stated in `api.test.ts` and backstopped by the msw isolation ledger, which fails the test rather than letting the request escape. Automocking a collaborator also means a signature drift in that collaborator is invisible to these suites; the schema and factory gates cover the data shapes, not the call shapes. |
| D153 | Drive the store suites by applying actions directly to the slice reducer, dispatch through `makeStore` only where the real path is the only path (`fetchTweets` `rejected`), and load `src/store/index.ts` in an isolated module registry under a `console.error` recorder installed **before** the import. | (a) Dispatch everything through a store, including `pending` and `fulfilled`. (b) Import `src/store/index.ts` normally and assert on its exported `store`. (c) Stub `combineReducers`' warning so the console stays quiet. (d) Assert only that construction does not throw, and skip the console contract. | `tweetSlice`'s thunk can never fulfil — `api` is `undefined`, so line 12 throws before any transport — so (a) cannot reach `pending`'s or `fulfilled`'s reducer at all and would leave two of the three documented transitions unasserted. `src/store/index.ts` emits its invalid-reducer report while it is still evaluating, because `store = setupStore()` runs at module scope, so (b) misses the report entirely: by the time the test body runs, the console call has already happened. (c) suppresses the exact string that is the oracle. (d) drops the most surprising fact about the module — that it builds a fully functional store with no reducers and `getState() === {}` — which is what a reader needs to know before trusting anything built on it. | Applying a generated action creator to a reducer asserts the reducer, not the dispatch pipeline, so a middleware or serialisability change would not be caught here; the `rejected` case is dispatched for real precisely so one end-to-end path is covered. The isolated registry is also a Jest-specific mechanism, so the console contract is coupled to Jest's module registry rather than to Redux, and a Redux upgrade that reworded the report would fail these assertions — which is the intended signal, since the wording *is* the observable behaviour. |
| D154 | Arrange the four component suites around three properties of the components as shipped: advance fake timers and flush microtasks as **separate** steps (`Dashboard`); replace `@/services/twitterService` with a spread of its own exports so `getTweets` stays absent exactly as the module leaves it, and attach it per case where a test needs the call to succeed (`TweetManagement`); and install no canvas or resize-observer shim, so Chart.js runs unmodified against jsdom (`Analytics`). | (a) `jest.runAllTimers()` or `await` alone, treating the poll and the mount fetch as one queue. (b) A hand-written factory for `twitterService` listing the two functions, or `jest.mock` with an explicit `getTweets` stub. (c) The shims of D92, so the chart branch can be reached. (d) `jest.useRealTimers()` with a 30-second wait for the poll assertion. | The poll and the mount fetch settle on different queues: advancing the clock does not settle a promise and settling a promise does not fire a timer, so (a) cannot distinguish "not refetched at 29,999 ms" from "refetched but not yet observed" — the 29,999-versus-30,000 boundary is the assertion, so the two steps must stay separate. (b) would state the module's export list in a second place that drifts, and an explicit `getTweets` stub would erase the exact `TypeError: (0 , twitterService_1.getTweets) is not a function` that is the oracle; the spread keeps the absence structural while leaving the property writable for the one case that needs a value. (c) is settled by D92 with measurements: with both shims Chart.js reaches `"linear" is not a registered scale`, which escapes to React and **unmounts** `TrendCharts`, leaving less assertable than the unshimmed path. (d) makes a 30-second suite. | The timer arrangement is easy to get wrong in a suite added later, and getting it wrong produces a passing test rather than a failing one — a refetch asserted after `runAllTimers` would pass whatever the interval were set to. The spread mock depends on the CommonJS emit reading `twitterService_1.getTweets` at call time; under a future ESM emit the per-case attachment would not be seen by the component. And the unshimmed jsdom path means the console census these suites assert on is jsdom's and Chart.js's exact wording, which a Chart.js upgrade can change. |
| D155 | Assert the **identity** of the scroll callback in the `TweetList` cleanup case: capture the argument `window.addEventListener` received, and require `window.removeEventListener` to receive that same object exactly once with a matching argument list. The post-unmount dispatch is kept as a corollary rather than as the proof. | (a) Keep `expect.any(Function)` plus the post-unmount dispatch, the shape before this decision. (b) Keep only the behavioural form — unmount, dispatch, assert no refetch. (c) Maintain a listener registry in the test by replacing both DOM methods, and assert it is empty after unmount. (d) Assert `name === 'handleScroll'` on the removed callback. | `handleScroll` is a fresh function on every render while the effect registering it has an empty dependency list, so its cleanup closes over the mount pass's copy — and `removeEventListener` only takes effect for the exact object that was registered. `expect.any(Function)` is satisfied by any of those copies, so it passes for the stale-closure leak it exists to exclude. (b) cannot close the gap either: React discards a state update from an unmounted tree, so a leaked listener records no further fetch and the dispatch proves nothing about removal. (c) reimplements a registry the DOM already keeps. (d) matches every render's copy, since they all carry the same inferred name. Arity is asserted next to identity because removal matches on the capture flag as well, so a third argument present on one side alone would leave the listener attached. | Both spies now wrap `window.addEventListener` and `window.removeEventListener` for every case in the file, so the two cases that dispatch a `'scroll'` event depend on them calling through: they are installed without an implementation, restored per test, and those two cases assert real listener effects, which is what keeps that honest. The capture is positional (`registration[1]`), so a DOM signature that took options first would need it updated — the asserted arity turns that into a failure rather than a silent pass. |
| D156 | Assert React's dev-mode diagnostics for the undefined `TweetCard` element instead of only silencing them: the whole `console.error` census, partitioned into the two `React.jsx: type is invalid` records, the one `The above error occurred` record and the two objects jsdom's virtual console forwards. Library-owned prose is matched by containment; stack frames are matched by module and position. | (a) Assert the rethrown error only, the shape before this decision. (b) Assert the exact format string and the full component-stack text. (c) Assert only that `console.error` was called. (d) Stop silencing the records so the run's output carries them. | The suite spies `console.error` with an empty implementation, so React's report of the invalid element is captured and thrown away — yet those records are the only place the failure names the element type and the component that built it, and a change in how React reports it would otherwise pass unnoticed. (b) is brittle in one *measured* way: `react` and `react-dom` float at `^18.2.0`, and the frame name is not stable even within this repository — coverage instrumentation rewrites the function whose name V8 infers, so the identical frame reads `at TweetList` under `npm test` and `at filters` under `npm run test:ci`. Matching module and position is invocation-independent and still pins where the element was built, and `TweetList` is still asserted from the guidance argument, where React names it stably. (c) passes for any implementation. (d) puts an expected failure in the output of a passing suite. The three counts are asserted exactly — 2, 1 and 2 — because they were measured identically under both invocations across repeat runs and `jest-environment-jsdom` is pinned; the partition total is asserted so a fourth kind of record cannot appear unnoticed. | A React minor that rewords its warning, changes the synchronous replay that produces the second validation record, or drops the guidance argument fails this case. That is the intended signal, and each assertion names what to look at. The two forwarded jsdom objects are environment plumbing rather than production behaviour, so a `jest-environment-jsdom` bump could change their count — the exact pin holds it today, and the partition assertion is what would report it. |

---

## §16 Result correlation and test-artifact hygiene

Scope: the three findings of the core-test-inventory review — the identity a run publishes for each executed
test, and the two ignore rules that hid files Git had to be able to show. Two rows revise an earlier decision
and name it.

Measured in this clone on Windows: `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml` before and after the change, over the
whole 17-suite run, and `git check-ignore --no-index` over every tracked path.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D157 | **Refines D37.** Emit the canonical test identity from `jest-junit` through **template functions**: `classname` and the suite name are the `{filepath}` with separators rewritten to `/`, `name` is the ancestor titles plus the leaf title, and `ancestorSeparator` is the single space Jest itself joins them with — so `classname` + `' > '` + `name` is byte-identical to `handlers.currentTestId()`. | (a) Keep `classNameTemplate: '{filepath}'` / `titleTemplate: '{title}'`, the reviewed shape. (b) Keep the string templates and rename every duplicated leaf title instead. (c) Emit `{classname} > {title}` with `ancestorSeparator: ' > '`, the shape the finding illustrated. (d) Use `{classname} {title}` strings and normalise nothing. | (a) publishes an unusable report, and it was measured rather than reasoned: over the three service suites alone, 40 `<testcase>` elements carried **7 colliding `classname`+`name` identities** — four in `twitterService.test.ts` across `getLatestTweets` and `getTweetDetails`, three in `api.test.ts` across `fetchTweets`, `fetchTweetById` and `generateResponse` — so a failing result named a title that existed three times and no consumer could say which operation broke; and every `classname` carried Windows backslashes while the request ledger stamped forward slashes, so the two could not be matched by equality at all. (b) makes 7 authors responsible in perpetuity for a property the reporter can hold once, and leaves the separator half open. (c) reads better but cannot be reproduced: `expect.getState()` exposes `currentTestName` and `testPath` and **no** `ancestorTitles`, and `jest-circus` joins that name with a single space, so the ledger cannot rebuild a `' > '`-joined name — the two ids would differ by punctuation forever. With a space, the emitted name *is* `currentTestName`, which makes the equality provable and the name a valid `jest -t` pattern verbatim. (d) leaves the Windows/POSIX mismatch, which is half the finding. A function is the only form that can rewrite a separator; `jest-junit` 16 supports one wherever it accepts a template. | Two residuals, both bounded and both outside what a template can reach. The `file` attribute `addFileAttribute` writes is the raw platform path, so on Windows it carries backslashes while `classname` does not — deliberate, since a CI annotator wants the path its own checkout uses, and on the `ubuntu-latest` runner the two are identical because `path.relative` returns POSIX separators there. And a suite that fails to **load** is named by `jest-junit` itself, which overrides all three templates internally; that path stays as it was and still reports, which is what D37 added `reportTestSuiteErrors` for. |
| D158 | Commit `frontend/src/test-utils/junit-correlation.test.ts`, a 12-case contract suite that reads the reporter options out of `jest.config.js` and applies its real template functions to the variables `jest-junit` builds, then compares the result with `currentTestId()` and with the ledger entry of a request the same test made. | (a) No committed gate — inspect the XML once by hand. (b) Assert on `reports/jest-junit.xml` from a later suite. (c) Assert it from a CI shell step that greps the artifact. (d) Restate the expected templates as literals in the test rather than reading the config. | The property is invisible in a passing run: all 179 tests were green while the report was unusable, which is exactly how this reached a review boundary. (a) leaves nothing to fail when a future edit reverts a template. (b) needs the previous run's artifact to exist, cannot run under a single-file `jest` invocation, and reads a whole-run global from a test about one seam. (c) puts the assertion outside the runner that owns the behaviour and only after a full run. (d) is a second description of the same templates, which is how the two come to disagree — reading the exported options means the test exercises what `jest-junit` will actually be handed. Negative validation: with the four pre-fix options restored, **11 of the 12 cases fail**, the survivor being the artifact-path assertion the perturbation did not touch; restored, 12 pass in about 3 s. | The suite reaches out of `src/` to `require('../../jest.config.js')`, so it is coupled to the reporter entry keeping its `['jest-junit', options]` shape; it throws a named error rather than asserting `undefined` if that entry disappears. It is a file the AAP's table does not list: it is in scope under §0.8.1, which covers `frontend/src/test-utils/**/*` in its entirety, it sits outside `collectCoverageFrom`, and the whole-run coverage numbers are unchanged by it — 65.76% statements before and after. |
| D159 | **Supersedes D141 and D144.** Delete the `package-lock.json` rule from `.gitignore` and suppress the file at its source instead: `frontend/.npmrc` and `e2e/.npmrc` each carry `package-lock=false`. D93's decision — no committed lockfile, `npm install` rather than `npm ci` — is unchanged. | (a) Keep D141's ignore rule. (b) Anchor it as `/frontend/package-lock.json` and `/e2e/package-lock.json`. (c) Commit both lockfiles and return CI to `npm ci`. | D141 solved tree cleanliness by making the file invisible, and invisibility is the problem: `npm install` **consumes** an existing lockfile as well as writing one, so an ignored, stale lockfile silently pins a local install to versions a clean CI checkout would never resolve, and neither `git status` nor a reviewer can see that it exists. Not hypothetical for this tree — the manifest pin and the installed graph had already diverged once (D109, H8). (b) narrows the blast radius without changing that property at all. (c) contradicts D93 and puts generated content in git; the review names it explicitly as a separate reproducibility change, not this one. Suppressing generation is the only option under which the decision is *stated* — `npm config get package-lock` answers `false` in both directories, where before no `.npmrc` existed at any level and the answer was `true`. | Two new files whose only job is to hold one line. They are read from the package directory, so an `npm install` run from the repository root would still write a root lockfile; no manifest, script, workflow step or document runs npm from there, and the root has no `package.json` for npm to install from. The reproducibility risk D93 recorded is untouched by this row and remains a suggested next task: no lockfile now exists to go stale, and none pins a version either. |
| D160 | **Refines D142.** Anchor the root artifact rule as `/reports/`, and keep the enumerated `backend/reports/`, `frontend/reports/` and `e2e/reports/`. | (a) Keep D142's unanchored `reports/`. (b) Delete the rule and accept that a root-invocation artifact is untracked. (c) Move where pytest writes its JUnit XML. | D142's own rejected alternative (c) is the correct one, and its stated reason for rejecting it — "closes the one invocation observed and none of the others" — does not survive checking: `--junitxml` resolves against the invocation directory, and every documented invocation runs from `backend/`, `frontend/` or `e2e/`, all three of which have their own enumerated rule. The only invocation the anchored form leaves is the repository root, which is precisely what `/reports/` covers. Meanwhile the unanchored form hid any `reports/` directory at any depth: measured, it hid `docs/reports/observability.md`, `docs/testing/reports/dashboard.md`, `frontend/src/reports/template.json`, `e2e/tests/reports/spec-notes.md` and `backend/app/reports/query.py` — one of them inside `docs/testing/`, which is where this project's own Rule 1 and Rule 2 deliverables live. (b) leaves the root artifact dirtying the tree, which is what D142 was written for. (c) would move a path the AAP §0.9.4 artifact table names. | A `reports/` directory nested under a directory that has no rule of its own is no longer ignored, so a future runner writing there would dirty the tree until a rule is added. That is the intended trade: a visible artifact is a fixable oversight, a hidden source file is not. Verified both ways — all four sanctioned locations still ignored (`reports/junit.xml` by this rule, the other three by their own), all five nested source paths above now visible, and a `--no-index` sweep over all 119 tracked files matches nothing. |
| D161 | **Refines D109.** Reconcile the *installed tree* to the manifest pin rather than the manifest to the tree: `npm install` in `frontend/` was run until `react-router-dom` resolved 6.22.3, leaving `package.json` byte-for-byte unchanged. | (a) Re-pin the manifest to the installed 6.30.4 and restate H8. (b) Leave the divergence and soften the H8 claim to "pins a version". (c) Pin a 6.x release later than 6.30.4. | D109 pinned 6.22.3 and asserted it "is what `frontend/node_modules` already resolves"; that had stopped being true — the warmed tree held 6.30.4, `npm ls --depth=0` exited 1 with `ELSPROBLEMS`, and `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` H8 published a clean resolution that could not be reproduced. Local verification evidence is worth nothing while the graph under test is not the graph the manifest describes, which is why this is a fix and not housekeeping. (a) would put the tree back inside the GHSA-jjmj-jmhj-qwj2 affected range `>=6.30.2 <=6.30.4` that D109 pinned away from, and would make the security decision follow whatever the environment happened to hold. (b) publishes a weaker guarantee to avoid doing the work. (c) does not exist: there is no 6.x release above 6.30.4. | The reconciliation is an environment action, so a machine that has not run `npm install` since the pin changed still holds the old graph; `npm ls --depth=0` exits non-zero there, which is how it announces itself. D109's residual is unchanged and still escalated: no react-router 6.x release is advisory-free, only `>=7.18.0`, which is a major version the AAP does not authorise. |

## §17 Security-negative coverage of caller-supplied SQL input

Scope: what the analytics date-range matrix in `backend/tests/unit/test_services_analytics.py` proves about
the two aggregations' handling of the strings a caller hands them. The row below closes a review finding
raised against the test suite rather than against production: the matrix characterised the *validation* gap
in `app/services/analytics_service.py` and never the *neutralisation* gap, so no test in the repository
could tell an interpolating subject from an escaping or parameter-binding one.

Measured on CPython 3.9.25 with `run_query` replaced on the subject module, so no BigQuery client is
constructed and no query leaves the process.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D162 | Characterise the exposure in the test suite and leave production untouched: add one shared row to `UNVALIDATED_DATE_RANGES`, a start date of `FIRST_ANALYTICS_DATE` followed by `' OR 1=1 --`, and assert in both existing parametrised consumers that each argument reaches the emitted SQL verbatim and inside the `BETWEEN` clause the subject builds. | (a) Validate or parameterise the dates in `app/services/analytics_service.py`, which is the actual remedy. (b) Add a dedicated pair of injection tests beside the matrix rather than a shared row. (c) Assert the absence of the escaped forms (`''`, `\'`) as well. (d) Assert the emitted SQL is safe -- reject the metacharacters -- which is what a suite written against the design documents would do. (e) Leave the matrix as it was, on the grounds that the two rows already record that no validation happens. | Both aggregations build `DATE(created_at) BETWEEN '{start_date}' AND '{end_date}'` by f-string, so a value carrying a quote closes the literal the subject opened and the remainder is emitted as SQL text. That is a production defect already in the noted-but-not-fixed register of the plan, and (a) is forbidden twice over -- the two authorised production touches are the `routes` package and four `Settings` fields, and the requirements direct that noted defects are recorded rather than repaired. (b) duplicates two call sites that already exist and are already parametrised over exactly this matrix; a shared row reaches both functions at the cost of one line, which is also why the row belongs to the matrix rather than to a new test. (c) is redundant, proven rather than assumed: the clause assertion cannot hold under either escape, since `BETWEEN '2024-01-01' OR 1=1 --' AND '2024-01-02'` is not a substring of a query in which the quote was doubled. (d) would assert behaviour the subject does not have and would fail on the current code, against the standing instruction to assert current behaviour. (e) is what the finding rejected: neither an inverted range nor `not-a-date` contains a metacharacter, so both rows pass unchanged whether the subject interpolates or escapes -- the property was untested, not merely unfixed. | The two new cases fail the day someone escapes, quotes or binds these values -- which is the intended remedy. That is deliberate: a characterisation test is the record that the behaviour changed, and whoever fixes production updates the two cases and this row rather than deleting them. Nothing else in the suite hard-codes the matrix, and the row adds no dependency, no fixture and no I/O. The exposure itself is untouched by this row and stays a suggested next task: the fix is parameterised queries in both functions, not a validating wrapper, because a wrapper would still interpolate. Negative validation, described rather than committed: with the start date locally escaped by doubling its quote, exactly the two new `[injection]` cases fail and the four `[inverted]` and `[malformed]` cases still pass; the subject was then restored and verified byte-identical by digest and by an empty `git diff`. |
