## §21 Conflict resolutions — where a rule and an instruction pulled in opposite directions

Seven conflicts arose between the four user-specified rules and the requirements' own constraints. Each is
recorded with the resolution and, in the "Why this choice" column, the argument for why that resolution is
the defensible one — because a conflict silently resolved is indistinguishable from a requirement missed.

The general principle applied throughout: where a specific, explicit instruction from the requirements
meets a general obligation from a rule, the specific instruction bounds *what may be changed* and the rule
is satisfied within that boundary rather than abandoned. Every one of the seven is resolved that way.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| C1 | **Rule 2 (Observability) versus the minimal-change clause and the two-touch allow-list.** Resolved by applying Rule 2 to the *test system* — the deliverable actually being built — rather than to the production application. | (a) Instrument production: a metrics endpoint, tracing and correlation-id middleware in `app/main.py`. (b) Declare Rule 2 inapplicable to a testing change set. (c) A partial reading: add health checks only. | (a) is simultaneously an unauthorized production change and the implementation of missing product features, both forbidden outright; the user's boundaries are specific and enumerated while Rule 2 is general, so the specific instruction governs the scope and the rule is met inside it. (b) would abandon a rule that can be honoured honestly. Rule 2's check-reuse-document clause is satisfied in full: **reused** are the two Codecov upload steps and their `backend` and `frontend` flags; **added** are the JUnit reporters on all three suites, the extra coverage reporters, the correlation identifiers of §8 and the collectability gate of D72. Production gaps are routed to §23 by D194, and the metrics surface is defined by D193. | Production remains unobservable, and this log is the only record of that fact. |
| C2 | **Rule 1's prohibition on rationale in code comments versus the requirements' mandated `# TESTING:` markers.** Resolved by splitting marker from rationale: the comments name only the blocker, and every alternative and risk lives here. | (a) Expand each marker into full inline rationale. (b) Drop the markers and rely on this log alone. (c) Carry the rationale in both places. | Rule 1 (Explainability) forbids this log's *content* migrating into comments; it does not forbid a marker existing. The requirements want a greppable trace in the diff, and terse markers give exactly that — `# TESTING: undeclared setting` on each of the four new fields, `# TESTING: param order` on each reordered signature, and `# TESTING: app.main route-module import` in each router-only module. Because nothing is duplicated, the two cannot drift apart, which is the failure mode (c) would introduce. | A reader of the diff alone sees the blocker but not the reasoning, and must follow the marker back to this log. |
| C3 | **Rule 3's instruction to update existing onboarding docs versus the requirements' instruction that the stale `README.md` identity be noted and not fixed.** Resolved by making every `README.md` change strictly additive and leaving the inaccurate identity text byte-for-byte intact. | (a) Correct the product identity while editing the file anyway. (b) Leave `README.md` untouched entirely. (c) Replace the README with an accurate one. | Rule 3 (Onboarding & Continued Development) asks that gaps be filled without replacing what is already there, and the absent testing onboarding is precisely such a gap; the requirements separately direct that the stale identity be recorded rather than repaired, and §23 records it. Both instructions are satisfiable at once because one is about what is *missing* and the other about what is *wrong*. | The README still describes an unrelated product above its new Testing section. |
| C4 | **Rule 4 forcing an executive deck into a change set whose write allow-list names no deck.** Resolved by treating the deck as a non-production documentation artifact in a new top-level `blitzy-deck/` directory. | (a) Omit the deck and accept a rule violation. (b) Place it inside one of the protected directories. (c) Place it in `docs/testing/` with this log. | The allow-list exists to protect production runtime behaviour and the excluded infrastructure directories, and a static HTML deck touches neither, so honouring Rule 4 (Executive Presentation) costs the protected surface nothing. The location is not a free choice either: the rule names `blitzy-deck/references/` as canonical. The artifact is flagged as in scope *only* because a rule requires it, so no reader mistakes it for a requirement of the work. See D188. | A top-level directory that exists for a rule rather than for the product. |
| C5 | **Rule 1's bidirectional traceability matrix versus the requirements' separately mandated disposition table.** Resolved as complementary rather than conflicting: one artifact serves both, with the disposition table as a projected view of the matrix. | (a) Two documents, a matrix and a standalone disposition table. (b) Put the disposition table in this log instead. | Both ask for the same mapping at different granularities, so a single bidirectional artifact satisfies both at the 100%-coverage standard Rule 1 sets, and §E of `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` carries all 25 legacy functions with their disposition in both directions. Two documents would duplicate one mapping, and the predictable failure is that one is updated and the other is not. Keeping it out of this log preserves the division of labour where this file holds *why* and the matrix holds *what maps to what*. | Readers looking for a disposition table have to know it is §E of the matrix. |
| C6 | **Rule 4's twelve-slide floor versus a code base of roughly a thousand lines.** Resolved by scoping the deck to the testing programme rather than to the product. | (a) Pad to the floor with filler slides. (b) Ship fewer slides than the rule requires. (c) Present the product's capabilities instead of the work performed. | Rule 4 itself directs that the presentation be scoped to the work performed, and the work here is the test programme — five layers, the collection-failure remediation, the two authorized touches, the coverage gates, the documented ceilings, the risk register and onboarding. That is enough substance to fill the target without padding, which is why this row exists: so nobody reads the slide floor as licence for filler. (c) would describe a product this change set did not build. | Leadership sees the testing programme, not the product's overall state. |
| C7 | **[Refined by D199 and D200 — the resolution stands, but "green" is now scoped differently: the test steps and the `e2e` job run and report on every push regardless of the lint conclusion, so the workflow's red conclusion no longer implies an unrun suite.]** **The expectation of a green CI matrix versus the prohibition on touching non-test steps**, given that `flake8 .`, `mypy .` and `npm run lint` are broken independently of testing. Resolved by changing the test steps and their install prerequisites, leaving lint and type-check alone, and scoping "green" to the two test steps and the new `e2e` job. | (a) Repair the lint and type-check steps so the whole workflow passes. (b) Remove them. (c) Report the workflow as failing with no explanation. | The requirements explicitly direct installing `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, which makes the install steps prerequisites of a test step rather than independent non-test steps (D186). The lint and type-check steps fail for reasons that predate testing — no linter or type checker is declared anywhere and the sources do not typecheck — so (a) needs new configuration plus production edits far outside the two touches (D187). The same reasoning keeps `frontend/package-lock.json` outside the authorized surface, so `npm ci` becomes `npm install`. | Installs are not byte-reproducible without a committed lockfile. |

---

## §22 The two authorized production touches, in full

Exactly two production changes were authorized, and both were required before the application object could
be constructed at all. This section carries their **complete** rationale, because the markers in the code
carry none: by C2 a `# TESTING:` comment names only the blocker it unblocks. Nothing below should be copied
back into those comments. The decision rows are D50, D51, D52 and D53; this is the argument behind them.

### Touch #1 — `backend/app/api/routes` becomes a package

`backend/app/api/routes` was a single extension-less **file** of 35 lines. Line 3 of `app/main.py` executes
`from app.api.routes import tweets, users, analytics, config`, which cannot resolve against a file, so
importing `app.main` failed with `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'app.api.routes'` and *no*
application-level test was possible — not a failing one, not a skipped one, none. The file is therefore
replaced by a package of four modules named for the four symbols that import statement asks for.

A byte-for-byte copy of the original body into `tweets.py` was impossible, because the original does not
compile. Two of its three endpoint signatures place a **defaulted parameter before a required one** — at
line 17, `def get_tweet(db: Session = Depends(get_db), tweet_id: str)`, and again at line 24,
`def generate_response(db: Session = Depends(get_db), tweet_id: str)`. CPython rejects this at parse time;
the exact wording of the message has changed across releases, so the defect is described here by its shape
rather than pinned to one string. The resolution is to reorder each signature to put the required parameter
first, giving `def get_tweet(tweet_id: str, db: Session = Depends(get_db))`, marked `# TESTING: param
order` at the two lines it applies to.

That reorder is **behaviour-preserving**, and the reason is specific rather than hopeful: FastAPI binds
dependencies and path parameters **by name**, not by position. It inspects the signature, matches
`tweet_id` to the `{tweet_id}` path segment and resolves `db` through `Depends(get_db)`, so parameter order
affects neither the runtime binding nor the generated OpenAPI schema. `get_tweets`, whose parameters were
already legal, is untouched.

`users.py`, `analytics.py` and `config.py` contain a `router = APIRouter()` and nothing else. **No endpoint
is created** in any of them, which matters because the legacy suite asserted against ten routes and the
requirements forbid implementing the nine that do not exist; each module carries a marker saying it
declares none, so an empty router is not mistaken for an invitation. No `__init__.py` is added, matching
the repository's existing PEP 420 convention throughout `backend/app/` (D50 alternative (c), and D185).

### Touch #2 — four `Settings` fields that production reads but never declared

Four attributes are read at runtime and declared nowhere, so each raised `AttributeError` on the path that
reads it: `configure_cors` reads `settings.ALLOWED_ORIGINS`, `create_access_token` reads
`settings.ALGORITHM`, `firestore.get_db` reads `settings.PROJECT_ID`, and both stream starters read
`settings.TWITTER_TRACK_KEYWORDS`. They are declared with least-privilege defaults:

```python
ALLOWED_ORIGINS: List[str] = []        # not ["*"] — the least permissive value that constructs
ALGORITHM: str = "HS256"
PROJECT_ID: str = ""                    # deliberately NOT an alias of GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT
TWITTER_TRACK_KEYWORDS: List[str] = []
```

Two of these defaults are deliberately inconvenient, and a deployer must set them. An empty
`ALLOWED_ORIGINS` means CORS permits **no** origin until it is configured — strictly narrower than the
previous behaviour, which was to raise, so nothing that worked before stops working. An empty `PROJECT_ID`
fails loudly at the point of use rather than silently addressing whichever project `GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT`
names, which is why aliasing the two was rejected: a wrong-project write is far worse than a clear error.

**No existing field name, type or default changed**, and no field was removed, so the settings contract the
requirements protect is intact. The touch stops at four fields: `TWITTER_CONSUMER_KEY` and
`TWITTER_CONSUMER_SECRET` are also read by production and also undeclared, but they are supplied from the
test side instead, keeping this touch to the four fields the requirements name (D52 alternative (c), D150).

### Blockers deliberately left unresolved

The requirements' fallback instruction is explicit: where a blocker cannot be resolved inside the two
allowances, test below the break and skip the higher-level test with a reason rather than expand scope.
Every blocker below is handled that way — **zero** additional production change.

| Blocker | Why the two allowances do not cover it | Fallback applied | Risk carried |
|---|---|---|---|
| The four frontend "component directories" are extension-less **files** — `Dashboard`, `TweetManagement`, `Analytics`, `Configuration` | The same defect class as `app/api/routes`, but the allow-list names only that one path; renaming four files would be four unauthorized production changes | Solved wholly in test configuration: a second Jest transformer that hands each file to ts-jest under a synthetic `.tsx` name (D30, D31), and a NUL-prefixed virtual module in the Vite harness (D41) | Two transformers with distinct cache keys is subtle configuration a future contributor may break |
| `Settings.GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT` is read as a **class** attribute in `app/db/bigquery.py` | Touch #2 declares *instance* fields, and pydantic v1 strips declared fields from the class namespace, so no declaration can fix it; patching `get_bq_client` alone does not help either, because a later line reads the class attribute again | A test-side `Settings` stand-in exposing the attribute (D150), with the unpatched `AttributeError` also asserted as current behaviour | A substituted symbol means the test would not notice production changing how it resolves the project id |
| Four modules cannot be imported at all, needing `Optional`, `LLMService`, `add_response` and `verify_token` | Supplying them would mean implementing missing production symbols | Fixture-scoped shims, exposed only as fixtures so no test file installs its own (D21, D22, D106), each missing symbol recorded as a divergence in the matrix | Shims are shaped by hand, so a symbol production starts using is not automatically present |
| `src/app.tsx`, three of the four page modules, `TweetCard`, and Chart.js registration | Each would require new production code — a valid store, two exported hooks, a component that exists nowhere, a `Chart.register` call | Tests written and explicitly skipped with reasons naming the missing symbol, or the current failure asserted as behaviour (D71, D156) | Assertions pin defective behaviour, so fixing a defect later turns a passing test red by design |
| `frontend/tsconfig.json` references a `tsconfig.node.json` that does not exist, which breaks every `.tsx` transform under Vite | Outside the authorized configuration surface, which for the frontend is `package.json` plus the new test configuration | Bypassed rather than repaired: an inline `tsconfig` in the Jest transform (D32) and `esbuild.tsconfigRaw` in the harness config | The repository still cannot build, and only the test and harness paths work around it |

---

## §26 Code-review remediation — guard completeness, gate exactness, the restored AAP pin, and the diagnostics verdict

Scope: the twenty-six findings raised at the README checkpoint. Three concerned the test system's own
security posture — a credential value reachable through a failure message, a loopback port that stayed
authorized after its socket closed, and a child process permitted whenever no test was executing. Three
concerned the contract between the runners and CI — a coverage gate that compared at whole percentages, a
dependency pin that had drifted from the frozen plan, and an E2E job invoked where no manifest exists. The
rest concerned oracle strength and documentary accuracy, including a whole layer whose diagnostics were
attached but never asserted.

Two of these supersede rows shipped earlier: **D227 supersedes D163**, and **D234 supersedes D192**. Per
D190 the superseded rows are left verbatim and marked in place.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this one | Risks accepted |
|---|----------|-------------------------|--------------|----------------|
| D223 | Refuse **every** child process from the `conftest.py` prologue through `pytest_unconfigure`, and admit exceptions only through `ALLOWED_CHILD_PROCESS_COMMANDS` — one regex, matching only the `cmd[.exe] /c ver` shape Windows `platform.uname()` needs. | (a) Keep the reviewed timing bypass, which returned to the real call whenever `test_id` was `None`. (b) Ban child processes outright. (c) Widen the guard to collection but leave teardown open. | The bypass left two windows open that the guard exists to close: collection, where both `db` modules construct clients at import, and teardown, after `block_network_access` has released and cleared `test_id`. A command allow-list is narrower than a timing allow-list because it does not vary with *when* the call happens, and the one command that genuinely needs admitting is a fixed, arguments-and-all string. (b) is what D105 already records as having broken the run. | A future dependency that shells out mid-run fails loudly with the command in the message rather than silently escaping. That is the intended trade, and the remedy is one reviewed line in the allow-list. |
| D224 | Track loopback port authorization as **live socket ownership**: a refcount per port, incremented on `bind`/`listen`, decremented when the owning socket is closed or detached, with the port de-authorized at zero. | (a) Keep the grow-only `_PROCESS_BOUND_PORTS` set. (b) Clear the whole set between tests. (c) Drop the loopback allowance and mock `TestClient`'s transport. | A set that only grows authorizes a *recycled* port: once a `TestClient` releases port N, the OS can hand N to anything, and the guard would still permit a connect to it. Refcounting is what makes the authorization co-terminous with the socket that earned it. (b) breaks a socket that legitimately outlives one test; (c) is a far larger change to reach a guarantee refcounting already gives. | The lock over the registry had to become an `RLock`, because a weakref collection callback can fire during GC on a thread already holding it. Reentrancy is the cost of releasing ports from a finalizer rather than from an explicit close path. The constant this row called `_BOUND_PORTS_LOCK` is `_OWNED_ENDPOINTS_LOCK` in the delivered tree, and D293 is the row that makes it an `RLock` in fact. |
| D225 | Redact sensitive values in the Settings-normalization failure message — field name, type, length and a **keyed** blake2s fingerprint, never the value — and raise `AssertionError` explicitly instead of using a bare `assert`. | (a) Interpolate `actual!r`, the reviewed shape. (b) Redact but keep the `assert` statement. (c) Report only the field name, with no discriminator at all. | (a) puts a real `SECRET_KEY` or API token into pytest output and thence into `reports/junit.xml`, which CI publishes as an artifact. (b) is the trap: `conftest.py` is assertion-rewritten, so pytest appends `repr()` of both operands *underneath* the redacted message — the redaction is worthless without the explicit `raise`, which was found by probing rather than by reading. The fingerprint is keyed with `os.urandom(16)` per process because an unkeyed digest of a short secret is recoverable by enumeration. (c) makes two different wrong values indistinguishable. | A fingerprint cannot be compared across processes, by design. Diagnosing "which value leaked in" therefore means reproducing locally, which is the right cost for not printing the value. |
| D226 | Add `--cov-precision=2` to `pytest.ini` `addopts` and to the CI backend command, so `--cov-fail-under=90` compares at two decimals. | (a) Leave the default precision. (b) Raise the threshold to 91 to create headroom. (c) Replace the pytest gate with a separate `coverage report --fail-under` step. | coverage.py compares the total **after rounding it to the report's precision**, and the default is zero decimals — so 89.5% rounds to 90 and passes. This was reproduced before being fixed: at precision 0 against a real 91.79% measurement, `--cov-fail-under=92` printed `FAIL Required test coverage of 92% not reached` and **exited 0**; at precision 2 the same measurement raised `Coverage failure: total of 91.79 is less than fail-under=92.00` and exited 1. (b) moves the goalposts instead of fixing the comparison; (c) adds a step for a behaviour one flag gives. | The gate is now genuinely tight, so a change that drops coverage by a tenth of a point fails the build. That is the point of a gate. |
| D227 | **Supersedes D163.** Restore `python-jose[cryptography]==3.3.0` in `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, matching AAP §0.6.1, and record the security escalation separately rather than resolving it by editing the pin. | (a) Keep 3.5.0, as D163 chose. (b) Pin 3.3.0 but suppress the advisory in a comment. (c) Remove the dependency and skip the JWT tests. | The AAP is the frozen source of truth and it names 3.3.0; D163 raised the pin to 3.5.0 on security grounds, which is a good instinct applied at the wrong layer — a code-review remediation cannot amend the plan it is being reviewed against. The correct shape is: code matches the plan, and the conflict is escalated as a tracked item a human decides. CVE-2024-33663 and CVE-2024-33664 are recorded in `backend/tests/README.md`'s suggested-next-tasks list, where a human sees them. | The suite installs and asserts a release with two known advisories. Neither is reachable from any test here — no algorithm confusion and no oversized-payload decode occurs in the suite — but the dependency is present, and that is the accepted cost of not amending a frozen plan unilaterally. D163's reasoning about the advisories remains correct; only its remedy is superseded. |
| D228 | Invoke the E2E suite in CI as `working-directory: e2e` plus the local `npm test` script, and provision the browser out of band with `npm run browsers:verify` reporting what the runner expects. | (a) The literal `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the repository root, as AAP §0.5.4 writes it. (b) Keep the root invocation but add `npx playwright install`. (c) Add a root `package.json` so the root form resolves. | The root has no manifest and no `node_modules`, so `npx` there resolves nothing locally and falls back to fetching from the registry — a different, unpinned runner than the one `e2e/package.json` fixes at 1.44.1. AAP §0.9.2 documents `cd e2e && npx playwright test` for the same suite, so the *intent* is the e2e-local runner; this form satisfies it while also satisfying D111 and D129, which forbid the downloader (CVE-2025-59288). (c) creates a manifest the plan does not authorize. | The CI runner image must already carry a Chromium-family browser; nothing in this repository can assert that it does, and the `e2e` job fails if it does not. That failure is loud and its remedy is one line of image configuration. |
| D229 | Put the `pages/TweetManagement` callback tests in a **separate colocated file**, `TweetManagement.callbacks.test.tsx`, rather than extending the existing page suite. | (a) Add them to `pages/TweetManagement.test.tsx`. (b) Use `jest.doMock` inside the existing file. (c) Assert the callbacks indirectly through the real child. | Reaching the callbacks needs `jest.mock('@/components/TweetManagement')`, which is hoisted and module-wide: adding it to the existing suite blanked the render its four passing tests assert — observed, not predicted. (b) requires abandoning static imports throughout the file for one test's benefit. (c) cannot distinguish "the page passed the right callback" from "the child happened to work". | Two files now cover one module, which a reader must notice to see the whole picture. §F of the traceability matrix names both, and each file's docstring names the other. |
| D230 | Turn the browser's console and page errors into a **verdict** with an automatic `browserDiagnostics` fixture: attach the whole ledger whatever the outcome, and fail the test at teardown for any `console.error` or `pageerror` the test did not declare through `allow(...)`. | (a) Keep the per-spec recorders that attached diagnostics and asserted nothing. (b) Fail on *any* console error, with no allow-list. (c) Assert an expected error only where a test happens to care, leaving the rest unchecked. | (a) is a false-pass generator: a test asserting a heading passes identically with a React teardown error and a rejected promise in the console, so an attachment nobody reads is not an assertion. Proven by injecting a `console.error` into the harness entry — under (a) nothing changed; under this fixture five specs failed naming it. (b) is unusable here, because three of the four routes legitimately log a caught production failure. The allow-list makes each expectation *reviewable*: one line per thing this route does wrong today, next to the assertion about what it does right. | An expected-failure pattern that is written too loosely tolerates more than intended. Mitigated by keying every pattern on message text rather than record kind — `/^pageerror: /` was tried first and replaced for exactly this reason — and by `resourceFailure(status)`, which binds Chrome's non-2xx notice to the status the spec chose. |
| D231 | Make `e2e/harness/stubs/analyticsService.ts` forward a well-formed series **only** when the response carries `x-harness-forward-trend-series: 1`, and have one spec opt in to assert the Chart.js ceiling. | (a) Delete the permanently-skipped chart test, the other resolution the review offered. (b) Make the stub always forward. (c) Select the path through a query parameter or a body envelope field. | The skipped test was dead code masquerading as coverage: its `HARNESS_FORWARDS_TREND_SERIES` flag was hard-coded `false` and could never flip. (a) is honest but loses a ceiling that *is* observable at this layer, where a real browser supplies a live 2D context and Chart.js reaches its empty registry — the assertion now covers the unregistered part, the uncaught escape and the unmount. (b) is destructive for the two specs that rely on rejection. (c) is not available: the URL is built inside the stub from the component's own prop, and a body field would fail the `TrendSeries` shape check the stub and `fixtures/trends.json` share. | The stub now has two behaviours instead of one, so a spec author must know which they are getting. The default is unchanged and the opt-in is named in the stub's docstring, its rejection message, the harness route comment and `e2e/README.md` §4. |
| D232 | Remove the `dist/`, `build/`, `.vite/`, `.DS_Store`, `Thumbs.db` and `*.swp` rules from `.gitignore`, and state in the header that their absence is deliberate. | (a) Keep them as contributor convenience. (b) Remove them silently. (c) Move them to a personal global ignore file and say so. | AAP §0.5.4 enumerates this file's contents and §0.8.2 states it "covers test artifacts only"; build output and editor noise are neither, and no suite here produces either — `npm run build` fails at both legs, and Vite's cache lives inside `node_modules/`. (b) invites the next contributor to re-add them without a reason, which is why the header now names them. | A build artifact or an editor swap file will show as untracked until someone adds a rule in a change that has a reason to. Verified in both directions by asking `git check-ignore` about 45 synthetic paths. |
| D233 | Redact the ambient Google Cloud project id in `backend/tests/README.md` and in D105 above, replacing it with `<ambient-project>` while keeping the failure it evidences intact. | (a) Leave it, since the failure it documents is the point. (b) Remove the whole quoted failure. (c) Append a new row saying it should be redacted, leaving the value in place, as append-only would otherwise suggest. | The id is real infrastructure metadata in a document intended for publication, and it is not what the evidence rests on — a `404` about a *database* rather than a connection error is what proves the request was authenticated and answered, and that survives redaction. (b) discards the pitfall. (c) is the append-only rule applied past its purpose: it exists to preserve *reasoning*, and a redaction changes no reasoning while leaving the value published would defeat the redaction entirely. | An in-place edit to a shipped row, narrowly scoped to removing a value. Recorded here so the edit is not mistaken for a change of decision. |
| D234 | **[Superseded by D235 - the Rule 4 deck render has since been performed and is reported as such; only leadership acceptance remains.]** **Supersedes D192.** Report the E2E browser run as **performed** — 12 passed, 0 skipped, exit 0, in Chrome 151 — and keep the Rule 4 deck render outstanding until it is observed. | (a) Leave D192's blanket "no claim rests on browser evidence" in place. (b) Report both as verified once the deck is rendered, in one later change. (c) Drop the distinction and describe the layer as configured rather than verified. | D192 was accurate when written and is no longer: the suite has been executed in a real browser and its JUnit artifact records `tests=12 failures=0 skipped=0`. Continuing to report it as unperformed understates the delivery exactly as reporting it early would have overstated it. Splitting the two steps is what keeps the remaining gap visible — one artifact is verified, the other is not, and a single combined status would blur them. | The deck's rendering remains unverified, and any summary derived from these documents must say so. §7.4 of the dashboard template states it in those terms. |
| D235 | **Supersedes D234.** Report the Rule 4 deck render as **performed** — 16 slides walked in headless Chrome 151, 5 of 5 Mermaid diagrams rendered as SVG, `i[data-lucide]` 0 with 19 `svg.lucide`, console empty, 9 of 9 requests 200 — and record the measured table rather than the captured screenshots. | (a) Keep D234's "deck outstanding" status until someone reviews the deck editorially, conflating rendering with acceptance. (b) Commit the 16 screenshots and the traversal recording as the evidence. (c) Assert the deck renders because its markup is structurally valid and its CDN pins resolve. | D234 was accurate when written and is no longer. Option (c) is the trap this programme has avoided everywhere else: structural validity is exactly what a Mermaid syntax error or an unresolved icon name would survive, so it cannot stand in for rendering. Option (b) fails a different test — the artifacts are ~20 MB of PNG and WebM, are not an AAP deliverable, and `.gitignore` is scoped to test artifacts, so committing them would both bloat the repository and widen a boundary §0.8 draws deliberately. What is durable is the measurement, so the numbers are transcribed into dashboard §7.4 and the artifacts are left as session evidence. The wording of the deck's own closing status was corrected in the same change and then re-verified in the browser, because an edit made after a passing render is unverified until it is re-rendered. | Rendering is now evidenced; editorial acceptance by the intended audience is not, and the deck says so on its closing slide. The evidence is a transcribed table rather than reproducible artifacts, so re-establishing it means re-running the check rather than opening a file. One cosmetic defect is knowingly shipped: label text on 6 of 30 diagram nodes extends 2–11 px past its box, unclipped and legible, recorded in dashboard §7.4 and left alone. |

## §23 The noted-but-not-fixed register

**None of the items below is fixed by this change set, and that is deliberate on three separate grounds.**
The minimal-change clause permits no production refactoring beyond what testability strictly requires; the
allow-list authorizes exactly two production touches (§22) and no third; and implementing a missing product
feature is forbidden outright, *even where an existing test asserted it*. Several of these are consequently
**pinned by assertions** rather than repaired — the suite asserts today's defective behaviour, so a future
fix will turn a passing test red on purpose, and `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` records which test pins which
defect so the blast radius of a fix is known before it is attempted.

This register is the source for the "suggested next tasks" section that Rule 3 (Onboarding & Continued
Development) requires of `../../backend/tests/README.md`, `../../frontend/TESTING.md`,
`../../e2e/README.md` and the additive Testing section of `../../README.md`. Each line is written to be
liftable on its own. Items are grouped by area, and every line names the file it concerns.

### Named in the requirements

- Routes issue SQLAlchemy `Session` type hints and `db.query(...)` calls against a Firestore-backed `get_db` dependency — two incompatible data-access models in one call path.
- Five frontend runtime dependencies were imported by the source but declared in no manifest at the frozen baseline: `react-router-dom`, `@reduxjs/toolkit`, `react-redux`, `zod`, `dayjs`.
- `README.md` describes an unrelated static-analysis product; the stale identity is left byte-for-byte intact by C3.
- The port triangle: `README.md` line 81 directs users to `http://localhost:8000`, `docker-compose.yml` maps `80:80` for the frontend (line 9) and `5000:5000` for the backend (line 27), and `nginx.conf` listens on 80 (line 13) and 443 (line 22). No two agree.

### Configuration and settings

- `app/db/bigquery.py` reads `Settings.GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT` as a **class** attribute in two places, which pydantic v1 can never resolve because it strips declared fields from the class namespace.
- `settings.openai_engine` is read in lowercase while `case_sensitive = True` (`app/core/config.py` line 30), so it can never resolve.
- `settings.TWITTER_CONSUMER_KEY` and `settings.TWITTER_CONSUMER_SECRET` are read, but the declared fields are `TWITTER_API_KEY` and `TWITTER_API_SECRET`.
- `DOUBT_RATING_THRESHOLD = 0.7` (line 20) is referenced by **no production code anywhere**, so the doubt-rating gate the requirements describe does not exist; the constant is asserted, the gate is recorded as absent.
- `create_access_token` hardcodes a 15-minute expiry and ignores `ACCESS_TOKEN_EXPIRE_MINUTES = 30` (line 8).
- `API_V1_STR = "/api/v1"` (line 6) is declared, but no route is prefixed with it.

### Backend behaviour

- `app/db/firestore.py` has no `add_response`, although `app/tasks/response_generator.py` imports it.
- `response_generator.generate_response` awaits the synchronous `firestore.get_tweet`, so it fails on awaiting a `dict`.
- `process_pending_responses` calls `get_tweet(response_status="pending")` against a signature that takes one positional parameter.
- `twitter_service.start_twitter_stream` references `tweepy.Stream` without the module importing `tweepy`, so it always raises `NameError` — the function is dead code that can never complete.
- `TweetStreamListener.on_status` builds a `Tweet` from seven field names the schema does not declare, so every tweet that passes the popularity gate produces an eight-error `ValidationError` instead of a write.
- `GET /tweets/{tweet_id}` filters on `Tweet.id`, which the pydantic model does not have, so it returns HTTP 500 and its own 404 branch is unreachable.
- No implemented endpoint accepts a request body, so the real surface has no 422 path at all.
- Analytics SQL is assembled by f-string interpolation of caller-supplied date strings — an injection exposure as well as a validation gap; characterised by assertions under D162, not repaired.
- Inverted and malformed date ranges are accepted without validation and interpolated verbatim into the query.
- One of the ten routes the legacy suite asserted is implemented: there is no `/users`, `/analytics`, `/config`, `/token` or `/health` router, and the requirements forbid adding them.

### Frontend behaviour

- `src/store/index.ts` imports `{ tweetReducer }` and `{ configReducer }`, which the slices never export; the store silently has no valid reducer, Redux logs an invalid-reducer error, and `getState()` returns `{}`.
- `useAppDispatch` and `useAppSelector` are imported by three page modules and exported by nothing.
- `TweetCard` is imported by two components — `TweetManagement` imports it **from itself** — and is defined nowhere, so any non-empty tweet list renders an invalid-element-type error.
- `src/services/twitterService.ts` imports `getTweets`, which is not exported, and calls the two-parameter `fetchTweets` with a single argument.
- `src/services/api.ts` exports neither `api` nor `setupInterceptors`, both of which `src/app.tsx` imports.
- `src/store/configSlice.ts` imports a nonexistent `../schema/configSchema`, and the `Analytics` and `Configuration` components import service modules that do not exist.
- The axios base URL evaluates to the literal string `"undefined"`, so every request is issued to `undefined/tweets?...` — which is why the mock layer must match that base explicitly (D169).
- `formatNumber(1234.5678)` returns `'1,234.5,678'`: the grouping regex mangles the decimal part.
- `tweetSlice.updateTweet` compares `tweet.id === action.payload.id` on a shape that has no `id`, so `undefined === undefined` always matches and it overwrites index 0 regardless of which tweet was intended.
- `tweetSchema` types `timestamp` as `z.date()`, which no raw JSON payload can ever satisfy.
- `Analytics` imports the tree-shakeable `{ Chart }` and never calls `Chart.register`, so chart construction fails in every environment including a real browser (D92).
- `src/index.tsx` calls `ReactDOM.render`, a React 17 API, under React 18.
- All four `src/pages/*.tsx` modules are orphaned: `src/app.tsx` routes only to `src/components/*`, so no route reaches a page module.
- `src/components/Analytics` L68 renders a bare `<canvas id="trendChart">`: no `role`, `aria-label`, `aria-labelledby`, `aria-describedby`, `title` or fallback content, and no table or textual summary beside it. A canvas has no implicit ARIA role, so the whole of the analytics content is unavailable to a screen reader and the heading is all that is announced (G9, D228).
- `src/components/TweetManagement` L62 renders its loading indicator as a bare `<div>` inside a `div.tweet-list` that is not a live region, and `src/components/Dashboard` L22 renders a bare `div.real-time-feed` that L16 refetches every 30 000 ms. Neither declares `role`, `aria-live`, `aria-busy`, `aria-atomic` or `aria-relevant`, so a fetch starting, a fetch finishing and a polled update arriving are all silent (G10, D228).
- `src/components/Configuration` L11-L25 awaits the credential write but holds no state for it: the L70 button is never `disabled`, never carries `aria-busy`, and no status element is rendered. A second activation while the first write is open therefore issues a **second** credential write with the same body and opens a **second** dialog (G11, D228).
- `src/components/Configuration` L31-L38 and L51-L58 render `API Key` and `Access Token` as `type="text"`, so both are on screen in clear text; only the two `Secret`-suffixed fields are `type="password"`, and no field declares an `autocomplete` policy. Masking follows the field's name rather than whether the value is a credential (G12, D228).

### Build, CI and repository hygiene

- `frontend/tsconfig.json` declares a project reference to a nonexistent `tsconfig.node.json`, which breaks **every** `.tsx` transform under Vite.
- There is no `frontend/vite.config.ts` and no HTML entry at the Vite root, so the dev server answers `/` with 404 and `npm run build` fails at both legs — `tsc` on the dangling reference, then Rollup on the missing entry module.
- `frontend/public/index.html` is a Create React App leftover carrying unsubstituted `%PUBLIC_URL%` tokens, a third unrelated product title, and no script tag for the application entry.
- `frontend/package.json` is named `"data-visualization-dashboard"`, a second unrelated product identity.
- No lockfile exists for any package, so no install is byte-reproducible; committing one is unblocked but out of the authorized surface (C7).
- The repository had **no `.gitignore` at all** before this change set; the one added covers generated artifacts only (D189).
- The `ci.yml` lint and type-check steps are broken independently of testing — no linter or type checker is declared anywhere in the repository (D187).
- The `e2e` job of `ci.yml` needs a Chromium build provisioned out of band and named by `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`; no step in the workflow can obtain one, by design (D223). Repairing this properly means an upstream Node bump so `@playwright/test` >= 1.55.1 becomes available and the downloader is no longer the CVE-2025-59288 path (D111).
- `postcss-load-config@6.0.1`, arriving transitively through the floating `tailwindcss ^3.3.2`, emits an `EBADENGINE` warning on Node 16.
- 28 `# HUMAN ASSISTANCE NEEDED` placeholder comments remain across 22 production modules, plus one each in `ci.yml`, `cd.yml`, `docker-compose.yml` and `nginx.conf` (D191).

### Production observability gaps

Routed here rather than to implementation by C1 and D194. Rule 2's check-reuse-document clause is answered
by documenting each gap; closing any of them is production work outside the two authorized touches.

- No metrics endpoint exists anywhere in the application, and none was added (D193).
- No tracing, and no correlation identifier on any production code path — the correlation ids in §8 belong to the test system.
- No structured logging: `app/db/bigquery.py` line 26 and `app/services/llm_service.py` line 28 report failures with `print`, and nothing under `backend/app/` imports `logging` or calls `getLogger` at all.
- `docker-compose.yml` line 36 health-checks `curl -f http://localhost:5000/health` against a `/health` route the application does not declare, so the container can never become healthy.
- `.github/workflows/cd.yml` lines 46-51 is a "Run post-deployment health checks" step whose `run:` block contains only comments, including a commented-out `curl -f https://your-app-url.com/health || exit 1` — it therefore passes vacuously and gates nothing.
- `docker-compose.yml` line 42 declares a `postgres:13` service and line 33 a `DATABASE_URL`, although the application uses Firestore and BigQuery and no code reads either.
- `scripts/setup_environment.sh` installs Node 14 (line 13), then references a `requirements.txt` (line 29) and an `.env.example` (line 37) that do not exist.

---

## §25 Code-review remediation — the E2E CI runner, harness-origin route anchoring, page skips, the chart-path split, the accessibility ceilings and the executive deck

Eleven decisions taken while closing the nineteen findings raised at the final frontend, E2E, documentation,
CI and deck checkpoint. Rows are appended here rather than folded into the sections they touch, because
roughly sixty files cite existing row ids by number and renumbering would dangle every one of them (D190).

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D212 | **Bring the `e2e` job of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` onto the contract D49, D111 and D129 already set**: delete the `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` step, add the download-free `npm run browsers:verify`, and run the suite as `npm test` with `working-directory: e2e`, with the executable supplied through `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`. | (a) Leave the job as it was and treat the contradiction as documentation drift. (b) Keep the downloader but pin the archive by hash. (c) Change the three decision rows to permit the downloader instead. (d) Vendor Chromium into the repository. | The job contradicted three standing rows and the header of the very config file it invoked: it downloaded a browser through a downloader that does not verify its host's TLS chain (CVE-2025-59288, D111/D129), and it ran `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from a repository root that has no manifest, so no project-owned runner resolves and an unpinned release can be fetched instead — which on the declared Node 16 ceiling is a release that does not run (D49). (a) leaves a non-deterministic job in the only automated path that exercises a browser. (b) still executes the affected code path. (c) inverts the decision to suit the defect. (d) puts ~130 MB in git. | CI now depends on a browser being provisioned out of band. `browsers:verify` fails naming the path it expected, which is the intended signal, but the job cannot obtain a browser by itself — by design. |
| D213 | **Anchor every `page.route` pattern in `e2e/tests/` to `HARNESS_ORIGIN`**, add `consumeAbortedRequestUrls()` to `e2e/tests/harness-fixtures.ts`, spell the `HARNESS_API_SURFACE` remedy hints in the anchored form, and add `e2e/tests/isolation.spec.ts` to assert the property for every intercepted path. | (a) Keep the host-agnostic globs and add an origin check inside each handler that calls `route.fallback()`. (b) Anchor the patterns and add no negative test. (c) Move the interception into the automatic fixture so no spec registers its own. | Playwright matches a page route before the context-wide rule, so a host-agnostic `'**/tweets*'` claims that path on **any** origin: a handler fulfils a request the page addressed elsewhere, every assertion on the recorded body passes, and the ledger that exists to attribute egress never sees it. On the credential endpoint that is a green test over credentials sent to a foreign host. Anchoring removes the ambiguity at the source; (a) achieves the same outcome but leaves the hazard one forgotten guard away and puts the rule in four places instead of one. (b) would leave the property unproven — and it is the kind of property that regresses silently, which is exactly why (c), moving interception away from the spec that needs it, was refused: a spec must still declare what it mocks. | A spec author must now spell the origin, and a pattern written the old way still compiles and still passes its own flow test. `isolation.spec.ts` is what catches it, and only for the four paths it enumerates — a fifth intercepted path needs a fifth case. |
| D214 | **Cap local Playwright workers** at `min(4, floor(cpus / 4))` instead of leaving `workers: undefined` outside CI. | (a) Leave the default. (b) Serialise locally as well as in CI. (c) Raise the per-test timeout instead. | Each worker is a whole browser process, and a container that reports many cores while sharing a much smaller memory and I/O budget oversubscribes badly at Playwright's default of half the reported count. Measured here: on a 64-core host the default of 32 simultaneous Chromium launches pushed two **unrelated, pre-existing** tests past the 30 s per-test timeout, while the identical suite passed in 12 s at one worker and in ~19 s at four. (b) throws away parallelism the suite is written for — no spec shares state. (c) hides a resource problem behind a longer wait and slows every genuine failure. | The cap is a heuristic, not a measurement of the host's real budget. A machine that could support more workers runs the suite slower than it needs to. |
| D215 | **Give each blocked page test its own skip with its own blocker reason** — ordinary `describe`, every test `it.skip`, and the blocker appended to each individual title — rather than one blanket `describe.skip` carrying the reason on the group. | (a) Keep `describe.skip` with the reason in the group title. (b) `test.todo`. (c) Delete the three suites and record the gap in prose. (d) `expect(...).rejects` around a mount, asserting the `TypeError` instead of skipping. | Jest's skip takes no reason argument, so a title is the only place one can live — and under a blanket skip each `<testcase>` in `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml` is marked skipped with no reason of its own, leaving a reader of one result nothing to go on. Suffixing each title puts the blocker on the identity that is actually published: verified by parsing the report, 24 skipped `<testcase>` elements, none without a reason. (b) discards the bodies, which are complete and are what un-skipping restores. (c) loses the record entirely. (d) would assert a `TypeError` as though it were intended behaviour and hide the fact that these pages are unreachable. | Jest's *summary* still aggregates a suite whose every test is pending as `skipped`, so the summary line looks identical either way; the difference is visible only per `<testcase>`. The titles are long. |
| D216 | **[The out-of-reach classification of the fourth outcome is superseded by D308 — `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts` test 4 now asserts it in a real browser. The separation of the four outcomes, which is this row's substance, stands.]** **Separate the four chart outcomes in `src/components/Analytics` and stop describing any of them as a caught construction failure.** | (a) Keep the single "caught chart-construction failure" description. (b) Simulate the real-browser failure with a throwing `Chart` stand-in and assert the unmount. (c) Install `jest-canvas-mock` and a `ResizeObserver` stub to reach the registration failure for real. | The module's only `try`/`catch` wraps the `getTrendData` call; `renderCharts` and the `new Chart(...)` inside it are wrapped by nothing, so no chart failure is ever caught by the subject. Three outcomes are reachable and were being conflated: a controlled constructor, where nothing fails at all; jsdom with the real library, where `chart.js` cannot acquire a 2D context and **returns early of its own accord** — nothing thrown, nothing caught; and a rejected trend request, where `chartData` stays null so `renderCharts` is never entered, which is the only path the E2E layer can reach. The fourth — context acquired, unregistered `'line'` controller raising, error propagating out of the unwrapped effect and unmounting the subject — is out of reach in every environment this repository has. (b) would assert the behaviour of a stand-in rather than of the subject. (c) is refused for the reason recorded at D92: with both shims installed Chart.js reaches `"linear" is not a registered scale`, which escapes to React and unmounts the component, leaving strictly less assertable surface. | The real-browser unmount is documented but never executed, so its exact diagnostic text is not pinned by anything. |
| D217 | **Record the four accessibility and credential-handling gaps as ceilings (§G G9-G12) and characterise them with assertions, rather than fixing them.** | (a) Fix them: an accessible name or a table alternative for the chart canvas, `role="status"`/`aria-live` on the loading indicator and the polled feed, a pending state on the credential submit, `type="password"` on the API key and access token. (b) Note them in prose and assert nothing. (c) Treat them as out of scope entirely and say nothing. | Every one of the four needs an edit to a module under `frontend/src`, and the allow-list authorizes exactly two production touches, both in `backend/` (§22) — so (a) is not available however plainly correct it is. The choice is therefore between (b) and characterising, and characterising is what makes the gap durable: the suites now assert the absence of each mechanism, so supplying one turns a passing test red on purpose and the fix cannot land unnoticed. (c) would leave a screen-reader-inaccessible dashboard and a credential field rendered in clear text as silent properties of a suite that reports itself green. | A green suite now *encodes* four behaviours nobody wants. Each is recorded in §23 and in every suggested-next-tasks list, and `./TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` §G names the test that pins each, so the blast radius of a fix is known before it is attempted. |
| D218 | **Supersedes D192.** The E2E suite **has** been executed in a real browser, and this log records that outcome instead of listing it as outstanding. The Rule 4 deck render was still open when this row was taken and was closed immediately afterwards by D221, so both halves of D192 are now discharged. | (a) Leave D192 as written. (b) Edit D192 in place. (c) Drop the acceptance list now that part of it is done. | D192's position was correct when it was taken and is now false in one half: `../../e2e/README.md` §8 records a full browser run — `@playwright/test` 1.44.1, one `chromium` project, the browser named by `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, `playwright-report/index.html`, `reports/e2e-junit.xml` and `test-results/` all produced — and the suite has since been re-run green after the D213 and D214 changes, at one worker and at four. Reporting it as outstanding is the same class of defect as reporting it verified prematurely. (b) is forbidden by D190: rows are appended, never rewritten. (c) would discard the half that is still genuinely open. | A reader who stops at D192 gets a stale answer for both halves now. The supersession list at the head of this document and §7 of `./DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` both point here, and the deck half continues to D221. |
| D219 | **Bring every content slide of `blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html` under Rule 4's four-bullet and forty-word body cap by compressing prose and terse-ing table cells, and move displaced substance into the slide's own visual rather than deleting it.** Seven slides were over: 66, 41, 95, 45, 87, 149 and 46 body words. | (a) Delete the surplus sentences outright. (b) Split the long slides into more sections. (c) Move the surplus into `.eyebrow` spans, which the cap does not count. (d) Read the cap as excluding tables and KPI labels and change nothing. | The cap exists so a leadership slide is read at a glance, so the fix has to reduce what a reader must read, not relabel it. (a) loses facts the deck exists to carry - the five documented limits, the reason each authorised production change was unavoidable. (b) cannot work arithmetically: three slides needed splitting and the ceiling is 18 sections against 16 already used. (c) is gaming - an eyebrow is a label, and stuffing a sentence into one keeps the reading burden while hiding it from the measure. (d) is contradicted by the review, which counted table cells and KPI labels and reproduced its numbers exactly. Diagram labels are genuinely part of a visual, so one wordy table became a flowchart with short node labels; everything else was compressed in place. | Compression costs nuance: the deck no longer states on the slide that the framework binds routes by name or that the 90% bar is the client's own acceptance criterion in the approved proposal - after the further compression **D300** records, both live in this log and in the dashboard rather than on a slide. The per-slide figures are the ones D300 states and a test now re-measures them. |
| D220 | **Mark all nineteen `data-lucide` icons decorative with `aria-hidden="true"` and `focusable="false"` on the placeholder, and additionally `aria-hidden="true"` on the fourteen icon-only wrapper spans.** | (a) Give each icon a `role="img"` and an `aria-label`. (b) Wrap every icon in an extra `aria-hidden` span. (c) Rely on the wrapper spans alone. (d) Leave the icons as they were. | Every icon in this deck duplicates adjacent text - a heading, a KPI label, or the item label beside it - so naming them would make a screen reader announce each label twice, which is worse than silence. (b) adds a DOM level the CSS targets through `svg` descendant selectors and risks the layout for no gain. (c) cannot work for the five icons that sit beside their own label text, since hiding the wrapper would hide the label too. The placeholder was the right carrier only if the icon library copies attributes onto the `<svg>` it substitutes, which was the open question; the wrapper attribute was added as a second line of defence for the fourteen cases where it is safe. | The behaviour depends on Lucide copying attributes onto the emitted element. That was verified empirically at 0.460.0 - all nineteen rendered SVGs carry both attributes on themselves and Chromium prunes every one from the accessibility tree - but a library upgrade could change it, and nothing in the deck asserts it automatically. |
| D221 | **Reflow the limits flowchart on the deck's limits slide so no label line exceeds Mermaid's 200 px wrapping width - `flowchart TB` instead of `LR`, and every line re-broken to at most 22 characters.** Found by browser verification, which failed its first pass on this slide alone. | (a) Raise `flowchart.wrappingWidth` in `mermaid.initialize()`. (b) Set `htmlLabels: false` for the deck. (c) Remove the deck's `foreignObject { overflow: visible }` rule so the excess is clipped rather than painted. (d) Revert the slide to the table it was converted from. | The diagram parsed and reported success, yet a measured 117 px of unwrappable text - `clientWidth` 200 against `scrollWidth` 317, with the wrapper computing `white-space: nowrap` - painted across node and subgraph borders and was clipped mid-phrase at the SVG edge, so a reader saw "probes point nowher". (a) and (b) are global settings that would relayout the five diagrams that already render correctly and are byte-identical between runs. (c) trades visible overflow for silent truncation and defeats the rule's stated purpose. (d) reintroduces the 149-word slide D219 exists to fix. Shortening the labels removes the cause instead of the symptom: both `nowrap` and `overflow: visible` remain in force and no label now needs more room than its box. | The labels are terser than the prose they replaced. The margin is finite - the widest wrapper is 168 px against the 200 px limit - so lengthening any line on that slide can reintroduce the defect, and nothing detects it automatically: Mermaid reports success and the console stays empty. It is visible only in a rendered screenshot or by measuring the painted ink box. |
| D222 | **Add Subresource Integrity plus `crossorigin="anonymous"` and `referrerpolicy="no-referrer"` to the four pinned CDN assets, and record the Google Fonts stylesheet as a residual rather than hashing it.** | (a) Leave the version pins as the only protection. (b) Vendor all four files into `blitzy-deck/` and drop the CDN. (c) Hash the fonts stylesheet too. (d) Inline the runtimes into the HTML. | A version pin fixes which artifact is requested but not which bytes arrive, so (a) leaves a tampered or silently republished file executing with full page privileges in a deck sent to leadership. (b) and (d) would make the file self-contained but (b) breaks the single-file constraint the rule sets and (d) would inline roughly 3 MB of minified library into a 48 KB document. (c) is impossible rather than merely unwise: the `fonts.googleapis.com/css2` response is generated per user agent, so its bytes and therefore its hash differ between browsers - and it is a font declaration only, degrading to the local fallbacks the theme already names. | The four hashes pin exact bytes, so any future version bump must recompute them or the deck silently loses reveal.js, Mermaid or its icons. The fonts stylesheet remains unverified by design; it can restyle text but cannot execute script. All four hashes were recomputed from the live bytes and confirmed applied - not merely returned - during the browser verification (D221). |
## §27 Code-review remediation — the E2E CI seam, browser provenance, the order-free cleanup, and acceptance-evidence status

Scope: the seven findings of the final-batch whole-project integration review. Two of them are the same
defect seen from opposite ends — the workflow invoked a browser downloader this log had already promised no
automated path would reach, and it invoked the runner from a directory that owns no runner — and two more are
artifacts of this document and its companions asserting things the tree contradicted. Three rows supersede or
refine an earlier decision and name it.

Two rows below record a **deliberate departure from a literal reading of the requirements**, which Rule 1
requires be logged rather than absorbed silently: the plan's §0.5.4 CI table, its §0.9.1 setup sequence, its
§0.9.2 command table and its §0.9.4 CI graph all publish `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium`
followed by `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the repository root, and the
delivered workflow now does neither. Every outcome below was measured in this repository.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D236 | **Supersedes the `browsers:verify` half of D111 and D129.** Replace the script's body with `node ./scripts/verify-browser.js` — a filesystem gate that checks `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` and, only if it is unset, the exact path `chromium.executablePath()` reports, and **exits non-zero** when neither holds a launchable file — and delete `playwright install --with-deps chromium` from the `e2e` job of `ci.yml` and from the root `README.md` install block, replacing it with a step that resolves a browser already on the runner image into that variable and then runs the gate. | (a) Keep `playwright install --dry-run chromium` and document the caveat in prose, which is what D111 and D129 did. (b) Parse the dry run's stdout and grep for the install location, then test that path. (c) Upgrade to `@playwright/test >= 1.55.1`, which carries the fix. (d) Vendor the Chromium archive. | D111 and D129 both rest on the claim that no automated path reaches the downloader, and `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` H12 stated it as a guarantee — while `ci.yml` ran the downloader on every push and the root README told every new developer to run it. That is not a documentation slip: the presence of the invocation *is* the exposure D129 was written to remove, and it had simply been reintroduced one directory away. The dry run could not substitute for a check, and this was measured rather than argued: in a container whose Playwright cache held only `ffmpeg-1009`, `playwright install --dry-run chromium` **exited 0** and printed `Install location: <cache>\chromium-1117` while `fs.existsSync(chromium.executablePath())` returned **false**. A gate that cannot fail is worse than none, because a pipeline that ran it looked green while every test was about to fail at launch. (b) makes the contract a screen-scrape of a CLI's human-readable output. (c) requires Node >= 18, which the CI matrix does not declare — the same reason D111 rejected it. (d) puts ~130 MB in git. | The CI gate now validates that a browser **exists and is executable**; it does not verify the binary's provenance, signature or that it actually launches, and the browser it validates comes from the runner image rather than from an artifact this repository checksums. That residual is stated in `e2e/README.md` section 9 rather than presented as closed. On a self-hosted runner without a system Chrome the job now fails at the resolve step instead of downloading one — which is the intended signal, not a regression. |
| D237 | Run the E2E suite as `npm test` with `working-directory: e2e`, not `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the repository root. | (a) The root form the plan's §0.5.4 table and this file's own change contract prescribe, with the concern recorded and left in place. (b) `npm --prefix e2e test` from the root. (c) `npx --no-install playwright test` from the root. | The repository root has no `package.json` and no `node_modules`, so a bare `npx` there resolves nothing locally: it either fetches an unpinned Playwright CLI from the registry and runs whatever it downloads, or fails before collecting a single spec. Both outcomes defeat the pinning D113 and D123 exist to guarantee — those rows already established that every invocation in this layer must name a local binary, and the workflow was the one place that did not. `working-directory: e2e` plus `npm test` puts the pinned `@playwright/test` 1.44.1 in charge of both execution and config discovery, and it is the form `e2e/README.md` section 3 documents. (b) works and is offered to developers in the root README, but inside a workflow the explicit working directory is the clearer contract. Verified: `npm test -- --list` from `e2e/` reports `Total: 12 tests in 4 files` through the local binary. | The workflow no longer matches the command text published in the plan. That divergence is the point of this row; it is also carried in the resolution report so nobody reconciles the two by reverting the workflow. |
| D238 | **Refines the cleanup half of D108 and D136.** Extract the per-test cleanup into `frontend/src/test-utils/reset-shared-state.ts` as `resetSharedTestState()` plus the hook body `runSharedAfterEach()`, have `setup-jest.ts` register the latter **by reference**, and rewrite `setup-jest.test.ts` so each state contract is proven inside a single test — change, call the cleanup, assert it is gone — with the hook wiring verified separately by loading `setup-jest.ts` into an isolated module registry and asserting the registered `afterEach` *is* `runSharedAfterEach`. | (a) Keep the four declaration-ordered test pairs and record the ordering requirement as an accepted exception. (b) Keep the pairs but prove the reset with an extra `afterEach` probe registered inside the suite. (c) Delete the pairs and assert only that the cleanup functions exist. | The pairs were an order dependence in the one suite whose subject is order independence, and the AAP forbids order-dependent tests outright — so (a) is not available at any price. They were also weak on their own terms: a pair's second test passes from a clean process without ever proving the first test's state was discarded, so `-t`, a reorder, or a split turns the proof into a tautology. Measured while making this change: the naive fix, an import-time probe answered by a *module* handler, put an entry in the shared request log and three tests then failed when run alone — proof that the isolation check earns its keep, and the reason the probe now uses a suite-local handler on a path no contract names. (b) leaves the hook's semantics dependent on hook registration order, which Jest does not contract. (c) discards the behaviour entirely. The same row covers a second choice: `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` H7 claimed a test proving an import-time request is intercepted, and no such test existed — the claim is answered by **writing** that test, order-independently, rather than by deleting the sentence. | The wiring test reaches into the module registry with `jest.isolateModules` plus `jest.doMock`, so it is coupled to `setup-jest.ts` registering the hook by reference rather than wrapping it in an arrow function; wrapping it again would fail that test by identity, which is the intended tripwire. Every one of the suite's 13 tests was confirmed to pass standalone as well as in the full run. |
| D239 | **Supersedes D192.** Record the E2E browser run, its failure-evidence half and the Rule 4 deck render as **performed**, report each with what was observed, and keep the Node-runtime gap as the one open item rather than folding it into a single pass/fail. | (a) Leave D192 standing. (b) Report the acceptance list as fully discharged. (c) Drop the acceptance list now that the steps have run. | D192 was right when it was written and wrong once the steps ran, and the cost of leaving it was measurable: `e2e/README.md` recorded a completed browser run while this log, `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` and the executive deck all still said browser behaviour was unproven, so the artifact every other file defers to for status was contradicting them. All three steps have now been executed and observed — the suite green at **11 passed / 1 skipped / 0 failed** through the D237 form with the gate passing first; an induced failure producing `trace.zip`, `test-failed-1.png` and `video.webm` alongside the HTML report and a `failures="1"` JUnit file; and the deck rendering in Chrome with 16 sections, 5 of 5 Mermaid diagrams, 19 of 19 Lucide icons, a non-text visual on every slide and zero console errors, reproduced across two cold sessions. (b) would replace one false claim with another, because those runs used **Node v22.23.1** and not the **Node 16.x** the CI matrix declares. (c) would discard the record of what is still unproven. | The evidence is a local run on a newer Node than CI declares, so anything specific to Node 16 remains unproven; the `e2e` job has not been executed on the declared runner. Stated in `e2e/README.md` section 8, `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` section 7 and the deck rather than left for a reader to infer. |
| D240 | Correct the two `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` guarantee rows (H2, H12) and the five inaccurate root-`README.md` claims to describe the **delivered** mechanism, rather than changing the implementation to match what they said. | (a) Change `conftest.py` to actually remove all thirteen settings names, so H2's wording becomes true. (b) Leave the wording and note the discrepancy. (c) Soften both rows into claims general enough to be unfalsifiable. | H2 said every defaulted and undeclared settings name is removed from the environment. The implementation pins twelve declared defaults by unconditional assignment and removes only `NOTION_API_KEY` and the two undeclared consumer fields — and D118 records **why**, with a measured reason: pydantic v1 resolves a field from `os.environ` before the `.env` file named by `Settings.Config.env_file`, so assignment is what makes the singleton independent of a stray `backend/.env`, while removal would leave that file free to decide the value. Implementing (a) would therefore undo a decision taken on evidence in order to rescue a sentence. The root README's claims fail the same way and were re-measured one by one: a bare root `pytest` **collects fine** — 576 tests, zero errors, because pytest's `prepend` import mode inserts `backend/` — and what actually breaks is that no ini file exists at the root, so `asyncio_mode = auto` never applies and 21 async tests fail with "async def functions are not natively supported"; `twitter_service.start_twitter_stream` raises `NameError: name 'tweepy' is not defined` one statement before any connection and cannot hang, while only `tweet_processor.start_tweet_stream`, with its function-local `import tweepy`, reaches a live stream; and the msw handlers use exact absolute URLs over a frozen two-origin allow-list, never a wildcard host. (c) is how a matrix stops being an oracle. | A guarantee row is now longer and names a mechanism rather than an outcome, which is harder to skim. Preferred over a short sentence that is false. |
## §28 Code-review remediation — the CI runner and browser contract, the frozen-manifest pins, and the acceptance record

Scope: the eighteen findings raised at the final configuration-and-infrastructure checkpoint. One critical
and two major of them share a single root cause worth naming plainly, because it is the reason this section
exists rather than a correction inside §4 — **`.github/workflows/ci.yml` was written to the literal command
strings the frozen plan's ci.yml row prints, and this repository's own E2E package had by then been built to
forbid both of them.** The plan's row names `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` and
`npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` from the repository root; D49 had already ruled out
root `npx`, D111 and D129 had already removed every automated path to the browser downloader, and
`e2e/package.json` ships neither an `install:browsers` script nor any reliance on `npx`. So the workflow and
the package described two mutually exclusive contracts, and the rows below settle on one and reconcile every
document to it.

The remaining rows cover the two frozen-manifest values the review found drifted, the reporting gap that made
three dashboard KPIs unsourceable, and the acceptance step that has since been performed. Rows are appended,
never rewritten: each row that replaces an earlier one names it.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D241 | **Supersedes the CI half of D49.** Run the E2E suite in CI through the package that owns it: `working-directory: e2e` with `npm test`. | (a) Keep `npx playwright test --config e2e/playwright.config.ts` at the repository root, which is what the plan's ci.yml row prints and what shipped. (b) `npm --prefix e2e test` from the root, with no `working-directory`. (c) Give the repository a root `package.json` so a root `npx` resolves. | Measured, in this checkout: `npx --no-install playwright --version` at the root reports `npx canceled due to missing packages and no YES option: ["playwright@1.62.1"]` — there is no root manifest, so (a) resolves no project-owned runner and, without `--no-install`, fetches and executes **1.62.1**, an unpinned version that also breaks the Node 16 ceiling the matrix declares. The same command inside `e2e/` prints `Version 1.44.1`. (b) works and is documented, but `working-directory` is the idiom the two test steps in the `build` job already use, so (a)→this is the smaller diff and the more legible one. (c) restructures the repository for a workflow's convenience. Note the plan is not uniformly against this: its own command table publishes `cd e2e && npx playwright test` for local use, so only the ci.yml row named the root form. | Diverges from the literal text of the plan's ci.yml row, and is escalated as such rather than presented as compliant. The plan's completion criteria also require the new `e2e` job to **pass**, which the literal form cannot, so the two clauses could not both be honoured. |
| D242 | **Refines D111 and D129 into CI.** Delete the `npx playwright install --with-deps chromium` step. Select a browser already present on the runner into `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` — trying `$CHROME_BIN`, then `/usr/bin/google-chrome`, `/usr/bin/chromium-browser`, `/usr/bin/chromium`, and failing the job with a pointer to `e2e/README.md` section 2 if none is executable — then run the download-free `npm run browsers:verify`. | (a) Keep the downloader step, which is what the plan's row prints and what shipped. (b) Add a third-party browser-provisioning action. (c) Cache Playwright's browser directory and download only on a cache miss. (d) Upgrade to `@playwright/test >= 1.55.1`, where the advisory is fixed. | D129's whole point was that a script one `npm run` away *is* the exposure to CVE-2025-59288's unverified TLS chain, and it deleted `install:browsers` for exactly that reason — leaving the same command inline in a workflow step reinstates what that row removed, one layer down. The runner image is an attested artifact, which is what "provision only from artifacts verified out of band" asks for, and `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` is already a first-class input of `playwright.config.ts`. (b) adds an unreviewed action to the supply chain the same finding is about. (c) still downloads on a miss, so the exposure is intermittent rather than removed. (d) requires `node>=18`, which the frozen matrix does not declare. Verified locally: the suite runs green against a system Chrome named this way, and `browsers:verify` exits 0 without fetching. | The job now depends on the runner image shipping a browser. Bounded by failing closed with a message naming the four paths it tried, rather than silently falling back to a download. A runner image that drops Chrome breaks this job loudly, and the fix is to name a provisioned executable rather than to reinstate the downloader. |
| D243 | Persist all three JUnit result streams as CI artifacts — `backend/reports/junit.xml`, `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml`, `e2e/reports/e2e-junit.xml` — each through `actions/upload-artifact` with `if: always()`. | (a) Leave them unpersisted, which is what shipped, and delete the dashboard KPIs and the onboarding claims that read them. (b) Install a test-report publisher action that annotates the run. (c) Upload only the failing runs' results. | Three reporters were configured and three documents describe their output as a CI input, but nothing preserved the files past the job, so dashboard KPIs K3, K4 and K6 had no admissible source and the frontend onboarding guide named a file that CI discarded. Uploading is the smallest change that makes the existing configuration and the existing prose true at the same time. (b) adds an action and a permission scope for presentation the dashboard already specifies. (c) loses the pass-side trend the KPIs are for. `if: always()` is required rather than stylistic: the run that most needs its results is the one that failed. | Artifact storage per run, and three more steps to keep in step with the reporter paths. The paths are asserted by the runner configurations that write them, so a drift breaks a named upload rather than silently emptying a KPI. |
| D244 | Add two readiness steps to the `build` job, each immediately before the suite it gates: `pytest --collect-only -q` with `working-directory: backend`, and `npm test -- --listTests` with `working-directory: frontend`. | (a) No readiness step, relying on the suites themselves to fail on an unimportable module. (b) Backend only, since that is the gate the plan names. (c) A separate readiness job. | This suite's historical failure mode was collection, not assertion: all three legacy modules died on import. A collection gate therefore separates "the graph does not resolve" from "an assertion is wrong", and it is the check the plan's completion criteria name. It was also being published as a pipeline feature by the executive deck while existing nowhere in the workflow, which is the accuracy defect the review raised. The frontend half costs about four seconds and closes the same gap for the other stack. (c) duplicates installation for a two-command check. | Two more steps, and a few seconds per run. `--listTests` prints an "did not exit" advisory under some npm wrappers; it exits 0 regardless, verified here. |
| D245 | Give the `e2e` job an explicit `permissions: contents: read` and check out with `persist-credentials: false`. | (a) Leave both at the action defaults, which is what shipped. (b) Set a workflow-level `permissions` block covering both jobs. | The job installs and executes third-party npm lifecycle scripts and a browser; the default token scope and a persisted credential helper are both available to anything that runs. Nothing in the job writes to the repository, so read-only and non-persisted are the least privilege that still works. (b) would change the `build` job as well, and that job's steps are outside the surface the plan authorizes — the `e2e` job is new and therefore wholly in scope, so the narrower change is also the compliant one. | The `build` job keeps the default token scope. That is a deliberate scope boundary rather than an oversight, and it is listed as a suggested next task. |
